T\subset S
a compact open subset by definition, and it follows in general because
\delta_{S}
preserves finite disjoint unions.
∎

Theorem 4.15
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a map in
\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}
. The following properties are equivalent.

1.

For all towers
(S_{n})_{n}
of light profinite sets with limit
S=\varprojlim_{n}S_{n}
, the map
f
has the unique right lifting property with respect to the map (of pro-objects)
S\rightarrow\underset{n}{``\varprojlim"}S_{n}
. In other words,
\varinjlim_{n}X(S_{n})\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}X(S)\times_{Y(S)}\left(\varinjlim_{n}Y(S_{n})\right)
.

2.

The functor
(\operatorname{ProfSet}^{light}_{/Y})^{op}\rightarrow\operatorname{An}
represented by
f:X\to Y
preserves countable filtered colimits.

3.

For all
S\in\operatorname{ProfSet}^{light}
and all maps
S\rightarrow Y
, the pullback
X\times_{Y}S\rightarrow S
lies in the essential image of the fully faithful functor
\delta_{S}:\operatorname{Sh}(S)\rightarrow\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}_{/S}
from Lemma
4.14
.

Proof.

The equivalence of 1 and 2 is purely formal, and the equivalence of 2 and 3 is a translation of Lemma
4.14
.
∎

Definition 4.16
.

A map
X\rightarrow Y
in
\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}
is called
etale
if it satisfies the equivalent properties listed in Theorem
4.15
.

Lemma 4.17
.

1.

The collection of etale maps of light condensed anima is closed under pullbacks and compositions, and contains all isomorphisms. It is also closed under passage to diagonals; or, equivalently (given the previous properties), if
X\rightarrow Y\rightarrow Z
are maps of light condensed anima such that
X\rightarrow Z
and
Y\rightarrow Z
are etale, then
X\rightarrow Y
is etale.

2.

For
S\in\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}
, the full subcategory of
\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}_{/S}
spanned by the etale maps is closed under finite limits and arbitrary colimits.

3.

A map
X\rightarrow Y
of light condensed anima is etale if and only if its
n^{th}
relative Postnikov truncation is etale for all
n
.

4.

If
\{S_{i}\rightarrow S\}_{i\in I}
is a collection of maps of light condensed anima such that
\sqcup_{i\in I}S_{i}\rightarrow S
is surjective, then a map
X\rightarrow S
is etale if and only if the pullback
X\times_{S}S_{i}\rightarrow S_{i}
is etale for all
i\in I
.

Proof.

Property 1 is a general fact about a collection of maps defined by a unique right lifting property. For property 2, by part 3 of Theorem
4.15
we can reduce to the case
S\in\operatorname{ProfSet}^{light}
. There it follows from Lemma
4.14
. For property 3, the closure under Postnikov truncation follows from property 2 (in any
\infty
-topos,
\tau_{\leq n}X
is the colimit over
\Delta^{op}
of the
(n+1)^{st}
coskeleton of the constant simplicial object on
X
). For the converse, by Theorem
4.15
part 3 we can reduce to
S\in\operatorname{ProfSet}^{light}
. In that case, the claim follows from the fact that a map in
\mathcal{T}op^{L}
between Postnikov-complete
\infty
-topoi preserves inverse limits of Postnikov towers. (Namely, we apply this to
\delta_{S}:\operatorname{Sh}(S)\rightarrow\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}_{/S}
.)

Finally, for property 4, we can reduce to the case where
S,S_{i}\in\operatorname{ProfSet}^{light}
for all
i
, and
I
is finite. We want to show that for
X\in\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}_{/S}
, the associated
X:(\operatorname{ProfSet}^{light}_{/S})^{op}\rightarrow\operatorname{An}
preserves countable filtered colimits if and only if it does so on restriction to light profinite sets over
S_{i}
for all
i\in I
. This is a problem of commuting countable filtered colimits with limits over
\Delta
. However, by Property 3 we can reduce to the case where
X
is truncated, in which case there is no problem because limits over
\Delta
in
\operatorname{An}_{\leq n}
are finite limits.
∎

Remark 4.18
.

A useful property is the following. If
S\in\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}
and
X\to S
and
Y\to S
are etale maps to
S
, then a map
X\to Y
over
S
is an isomorphism if and only if it is so on pullback along
\ast\to S
for any map
\ast\to S
from a point. Indeed, we can reduce to
S
light profinite and then this follows because we can check isomorphisms of sheaves on stalks (recall that we are homotopy dimension zero, so this reduces to the classical case of sheaves of sets).

Sometimes, instead of thinking of maps
X\rightarrow S
in
\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}
as geometric/topological objects over
S
, we’d rather think of them as a kind of sheaves on
S
. For such a purpose, let us adopt some abbreviated notation, borrowed from
[
Sch22
]
.

Definition 4.19
.

For
S\in\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}
, let us denote by

\operatorname{Sh}_{v}(S):=\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}_{/S},

and by
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(S)\subset\operatorname{Sh}_{v}(S)
the full subcategory of etale maps to
S
.

Note that
v
-sheaves have the obvious pullback functoriality, namely
\operatorname{Sh}_{v}(-):(\operatorname{CondAn}^{light})^{op}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}op^{L}
. This functor preserves limits by descent applied to the
\infty
-topos
\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}
. The full subcategory
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(-)\subset\operatorname{Sh}_{v}(-)
is closed under pullback and is of local nature by Lemma
4.17
, so
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(-):(\operatorname{CondAn}^{light})^{op}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}op^{L}
inherits the functoriality and the limit-preservation. It follows that
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(-)
can also be described as the right Kan extension of its restriction to
(\operatorname{ProfSet}^{light})^{op}
, where by Lemma
4.14
it identifies with the usual theory of sheaves of anima on the underlying topological space.

Thus, to understand
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(S)
for a condensed anima
S
, one can write
S
as a colimit of light profinite sets and try to identify the resulting limit of sheaf categories. Let us carry this procedure out in three classes of examples:

1.

The “discrete” case: the case where
S
lies in the essential image of the unique map in
\mathcal{T}op^{L}

\operatorname{An}\to\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}.

2.

The “topological” case: the case where
S
lies in the essential image of the functor

\operatorname{Top}\to\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}

sending
K\mapsto\underline{K}:S\mapsto\operatorname{Cont}(S,K).

3.

The “profinite” case: the case where
S
is a countable inverse limit of (discrete)
\pi
-finite anima.

The discrete case:

We start with the discrete case, which is quite simple. The special case
S=\ast
of Lemma
4.14
shows that the unique map in
\mathcal{T}op^{L}

\delta:\operatorname{An}\rightarrow\operatorname{CondAn}

is fully faithful with essential image those light condensed anima which are
discrete
, i.e. etale over
\ast
. By the 2 out of 3 property of etale maps, it follows that for
X\in\operatorname{An}
,

\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(\delta(X))=\operatorname{An}_{/X}=\operatorname{Fun}(X,\operatorname{An}),

the usual
\infty
-category of “local systems of anima over
X
”.

The topological case:

Now we consider the topological case. There is a functor
\operatorname{Top}\rightarrow\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}
, sending a topological space
K
to the light condensed anima
\underline{K}
defined by

\underline{K}(S)=\operatorname{Cont}(S,K).

Note that this satisfies the sheaf condition because surjective maps of compact Hausdorff spaces are quotient maps. We are interested in relating etale sheaves on
\underline{K}
to usual sheaves on
K
, extending what we already know in the case
K\in\operatorname{ProfSet}^{light}
.

Lemma 4.20
.

Let
K
be a topological space.

1.

Suppose
\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I}
is an open cover of
K
. Then
\{\underline{U_{i}}\rightarrow\underline{S}\}_{i\in I}
is a cover in
\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}
.

2.

Suppose
f:K^{\prime}\rightarrow K
is a local homeomorphism. Then
\underline{K^{\prime}}\rightarrow\underline{K}
is etale.

3.

There is a unique map
\operatorname{Sh}(K)\rightarrow\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(\underline{K})
in
\mathcal{T}op^{L}
which sends the Yoneda image of an open subset
U\subset K
to the inclusion
\underline{U}\rightarrow\underline{K}
.

4.

Suppose every point
x\in K
admits a neighborhood
L
which is a second-countable compact Hausdorff space. Then this functor
\operatorname{Sh}(K)\rightarrow\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(\underline{K})
realizes the target as the Postnikov-completion of the source.

Proof.

For 1, we are formally reduced to the case where
K\in\operatorname{ProfSet}^{light}
. Then
\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I}
admits a refinement which is a disjoint cover by compact open subsets, proving the claim. For 2, because of 1 we can reduce to the case where
f
is an open inclusion, or even a compact open inclusion of light profinite sets. Then the claim follows by definition of the functor
\delta_{S}
in Lemma
4.14
. Claim 3 follows formally from 1 and 2, given that
U\mapsto\underline{U}
obviously preseves finite limits. Finally, for claim 4, by descent we can reduce to the case when
K
is an open subset of an
L
as in the statement. Then
K
is Hausdorff, so the collection of compact Hausdorff subsets is closed under finite intersections, so by descent again we can reduce to the case
K=L
. Choose a light profinite set
S
with a surjection
f:S\twoheadrightarrow K
(e.g., take the spectral space of Example
6.14
part 1 and equip it with the constructible topology). It suffices to show that
\operatorname{Sh}(K)_{\leq d}
is the limit of
\operatorname{Sh}(-)_{\leq n}
over the Cech nerve of
f
for all
n\geq 0
. This is something that can be checked using the cohomological descent formalism set up in
[
Lur17
]
4.7.5. Namely, by
[
Lur09
]
7.3 the proper base change theorem holds for sheaves of anima on compact Hausdorff spaces, so it suffices to show that if
g:X\rightarrow Y
is a surjective map of compact Hausdorff spaces, then
g^{\ast}:\operatorname{Sh}(Y)_{\leq n}\rightarrow\operatorname{Sh}(X)_{\leq n}
is conservative and commutes with limits over
\Delta
. Conservativity follows from the fact that isomorphisms in
\operatorname{Sh}(Y)_{\leq n}
are tested on stalks, and commutation with limits over
\Delta
follows because we are truncated, so that limits over
\Delta
are finite limits hence commute with pullbacks.
∎

Remark 4.21
.

We recall from
[
Lur09
]
Thm. 7.2.3.6 that if
K
is a compact Hausdorff space of finite covering dimension, then
\operatorname{Sh}(K)
identifies with its Postnikov-completion. Thus, if
K
is additionally assumed second-countable, then we get
\operatorname{Sh}(K)\simeq\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(\underline{K})
. See also Proposition
6.15
for a similar statement with spectrum coefficients under a weaker hypothesis.

The profinite case
:

Now we turn to the profinite setting. This was notably considered by Barwick-Haine,
[
BH19
]
Example 3.3.10. We start with the unique map in
\mathcal{T}op^{L}

\delta:\operatorname{An}\rightarrow\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}

considered above. Then we restrict to the full subcategory
\operatorname{An}_{\pi}\subset\operatorname{An}
of
\pi
-finite anima (those anima
X
for which
\pi_{i}(X,x)
is finite for all
x\in X
and
i\in\mathbb{N}
, and
\ast
for
i
sufficiently large). Then we formally extend by countable filtered inverse limits to get a countable-limit-preserving functor

\widehat{\delta}:\operatorname{Pro}^{\aleph_{1}}(\operatorname{An}_{\pi})\rightarrow\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}.

Barwick-Haine proved the theorem that this functor is fully faithful. Actually, they proved not quite this statement, but its “pyknotic” variant. However the light condensed context is even easier to handle because countable pro-systems are simpler than arbitrary pro-systems. Just to be safe we’ll include a proof of this light variant: it follows from the following more general claim (which we’ll generalize even further below).

Lemma 4.22
.

Suppose
(X_{n})_{n}
is a tower of
\pi
-finite anima and that
A\in\operatorname{An}
is an anima such that for each
x\in\pi_{0}A
, the corresponding component
A_{x}\subset A
is
d
-truncated for some
d\in\mathbb{N}
(possibly depending on
x
). Then if
X=\varprojlim_{n}X_{n}
is the limit in
\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}
, we have

\varinjlim_{n}\operatorname{Map}(X_{n},A)\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\operatorname{Map}_{\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}}(X,A).

Here we implicitly use the fully faithful embedding
\delta:\operatorname{An}\rightarrow\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}
.

Proof.

By the Lemma which follows, every tower of
\pi
-finite anima admits a termwise surjective map from a tower of finite sets, such that the induced map on inverse limits (in
\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}
) is also surjective. It follows that any tower
(X_{n})
of
\pi
-finite anima admits a hypercover by towers of finite sets, such that on inverse limits we get a hypercover in
\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}
.

Now, we know the lemma in the case of towers of finite sets (it is a special case of the discussion of etale sheaves in Theorem
4.15
), so by this hypercover we reduce to the question of commuting a filtered colimit by a limit over
\Delta
. But in the truncated context this is no problem as limits over
\Delta
are finite. This handles the case of truncated
A
. In particular, the lemma holds for sets
A
. Then applying this to the set of components of our
A
, we deduce that
\operatorname{Map}_{\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}}(X,-)
commutes with the filtered colimit writing
A
as the union of its sub-anima with finitely many components, letting us reduce to the truncated case, already handled.
∎

We used the following simple lemma in the proof.

Lemma 4.23
.

Suppose
(X_{n})
is a tower of
\pi
-finite anima. Then there exists a tower of finite sets
(S_{n})
and a map of towers
(S_{n})\to(X_{n})
such that
S_{0}\to X_{0}
is surjective and

S_{n+1}\to S_{n}\times_{X_{n}}X_{n+1}

is surjective for all
n\geq 0
.

Moreover, for such a map of towers we have:

1.

Each
f_{n}:S_{n}\to X_{n}
is surjective.

2.

The induced map
\varprojlim_{n}S_{n}\to\varprojlim_{n}X_{n}
in
\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}
is surjective.

Proof.

Every
\pi
-finite anima has finite
\pi_{0}
, hence admits a surjection from a finite set. Moreover the class of
\pi
-finite anima is closed under pullbacks. Thus we can build
(S_{n})
inductively in the obvious way.

Claim 1 follows from the fact that surjections are closed under base change and composition. Claim 2 follows from repleteness, see Lemma
4.10
.
∎

Example 4.24
.

Suppose we take
A\in\operatorname{An}_{\pi}
. Then Lemma
4.13
implies that
\operatorname{Pro}^{\aleph_{0}}(\operatorname{An}_{\pi})\rightarrow\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}
is fully faithful. Call the essential image the
\infty
-category
\operatorname{ProfAn}^{light}
of
light profinite anima
. Note that the full subcategory of light profinite anima is closed under countable limits and finite disjoint unions.

We can also use Lemma
4.22
to study the notion of
\pi
-finite etale
map of light condensed anima. First, recall that a
\pi
-finite map of usual anima is a map
X\to Y
in
\operatorname{An}
such that each fiber is
\pi
-finite etale, or equivalently, it is locally on
Y
(with respect to the canonical Grothendieck topology on
\operatorname{An}
) isomorphic to projection off some
\pi
-finite anima. Then
\pi
-finite maps with target
Y\in\operatorname{An}
are classified by maps in
\operatorname{An}

Y\to\operatorname{An}^{\simeq}_{\pi},

where
\operatorname{An}^{\simeq}_{\pi}
is the groupoid core of the
\infty
-category of
\pi
-finite anima.

Definition 4.25
.

Let
Y\in\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}
. Say that a map
X\to Y
in
\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}
is
\pi
-finite etale
if and only if it is, locally on
Y
in the light condensed Grothendieck topology, isomorphic to projection off some
\pi
-finite anima.

In particular, by descent it follows that every
\pi
-finite etale map is indeed etale in the sense of Theorem
4.15
.

Lemma 4.26
.

1.

A composition or base-change of
\pi
-finite etale maps is
\pi
-finite etale. Moreover the class of
\pi
-finite etale maps is closed under taking the diagonal.

2.

For
Y\in\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}
,
\pi
-finite etale maps to
Y
are classified by maps

Y\to\operatorname{An}_{\pi}^{\simeq}

in
\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}
. (As usual, we implicitly use the fully faithful functor
\delta:\operatorname{An}\to\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}
from
4.14
.)

3.

If
Y\in\operatorname{ProfAn}^{light}
, then a map
X\to Y
in
\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}
is
\pi
-finite etale if and only if it is a pullback of a map between
\pi
-finite anima (in which case
X\in\operatorname{ProfAn}^{light}
as well).

4.

If again
Y\in\operatorname{ProfAn}^{light}
, then
\operatorname{ProfAn}^{light}_{/Y}
identifies with the countable pro-category of the
\infty
-category of
\pi
-finite etale maps to
Y
.

Proof.

For 1, the claims about base-change and closure under passage to the diagonal are obvious by descent. We will return to closure under composition after proving Claim 3.

Claim 2 (as well as Definition
4.25
) actually works in any
\infty
-topos. Namely if
\mathcal{C}
is an
\infty
-topos, then the anima of global sections of the constant sheaf of anima with value
\operatorname{An}^{\simeq}_{\pi}
on
\mathcal{C}
identifies with the anima of
\pi
-finite etale maps
X\to\ast
in
\mathcal{C}
(those maps which locally are isomorphic to projection off some
\pi
-finite anima). Indeed by descent theory
\pi
-finite etale maps
X\to\ast
are classified by global sections of the object

\bigsqcup_{[K]}B\underline{\operatorname{Aut}}(\underline{K})

of
\mathcal{C}
. Here
K
runs over a set of representatives for the isomorphism classes of
\pi
-finite anima,
\underline{K}
denotes the constant sheaf on
K
, and
\underline{\operatorname{Aut}}
means the internal automorphism group. In this language what we need to see is that
\underline{\operatorname{Aut}}(\underline{K})=\underline{\operatorname{Aut}(K)}
, i.e. that the sheaf of automorphisms of the constant sheaf on
K
identifies with the constant sheaf on the anima of automorphisms of
K
, when
K
is
\pi
-finite (caution that the statement is false e.g. for an infinite discrete set
K
). But this follows from the fact that
X\mapsto\underline{X}
, being a special case of pullback map of
\infty
-topoi, preserves finite limits and coproducts.

For 3, this follows from 2 and Lemma
4.22
, noting that
A=\operatorname{An}_{\pi}^{\simeq}
has the property that each component is
\pi
-finite. As for 4, it follows directly from 3 and Lemma
4.13
.

What remains is the claim (from 1) that
\pi
-finite etale maps are closed under composition. Assume given
X\to Y\to Z
where both maps are
\pi
-finite etale. Working locally we can assume
Z
is a light profinite set. By 3, the map
Y\to Z
is pulled back from a map of
\pi
-finite anima, and then so is
X\to Y
, and in this way we reduce to the setting of
\operatorname{An}
where it is clear from the long exact sequence of homotopy groups.
∎

The following lemma will also be convenient.

Lemma 4.27
.

Let
X\in\operatorname{ProfAn}^{light}
. Then there is a tower
(X_{n})_{n}
of
\pi
-finite anima and an isomorphism
X\simeq\varprojlim_{n}X_{n}
such that
X\rightarrow X_{n}
is surjective for all
n
.

Proof.

Start with any tower
(X^{\prime}_{n})
of
\pi
-finite anima with
X\simeq\varprojlim_{n}X^{\prime}_{n}
. Then set
X_{n}=\operatorname{im}(X\rightarrow X^{\prime}_{n})
. Here the image is in the sense of the
\infty
-topos
\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}
. However, it’s easy to see that if
A\in\operatorname{An}\subset\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}
and
B\subset A
is any subobject in
\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}
, then
B\in\operatorname{An}
as well (by descent, we reduce to
A=\ast
, whose only subobjects are
\ast
and
\emptyset
). Thus
(X_{n})_{n}
is a tower of
\pi
-finite anima, and
X=\varprojlim_{n}X_{n}
.
∎

For
Y\in\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}
, let
\operatorname{fEt_{\pi}}_{/Y}
denote the full subcategory of
\pi
-finite etale maps to
Y
, equipped with the Grothendieck topology inherited from
\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}
. The following theorem shows that, up to completeness issues,
\operatorname{fEt_{\pi}}_{/Y}
provides a defining site for
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(Y)
, for any light profinite anima
Y
.

Theorem 4.28
.

Let
S\in\operatorname{ProfAn}^{light}
. Consider the
\infty
-topos
\operatorname{Sh}(\operatorname{fEt_{\pi}}_{/S})
of sheaves of anima on the site
\operatorname{fEt_{\pi}}_{/Y}
with respect to the Grothendieck topology inherited from
\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}
. Then the natural comparison map

\operatorname{Sh}(\operatorname{fEt_{\pi}}_{/S})\rightarrow\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(S)

in
\mathcal{T}op^{L}
(left Kan extending the inclusion map
\operatorname{fEt_{\pi}}_{/S}\rightarrow\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(S)
) identifies the target as the Postnikov-completion of the source.

Proof.

Because
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(S)
is Postnikov-complete (Theorem
4.15
), it suffices to show that the comparison map induces an equivalence on
d
-truncations for all
d\geq 0
. Consider the following three
\infty
-categories:

1.

The
\infty
-category of
\operatorname{An}_{\leq d}
-valued sheaves on
\operatorname{fEt_{\pi}}_{/S}
, i.e.
\operatorname{Sh}(\operatorname{fEt_{\pi}}_{/S})_{\leq d}
.

2.

The
\infty
-category of
\operatorname{An}_{\leq d}
-valued sheaves on
\operatorname{ProfAn}^{light}_{/S}=\operatorname{Pro}^{\aleph_{0}}(\operatorname{fEt_{\pi}}_{/S})
which preserve countable filtered colimits (as functors
(\operatorname{ProfAn}^{light}_{/S})^{op}\to\operatorname{An}_{\leq d}
).

3.

The
\infty
-category of
\operatorname{An}_{\leq d}
-valued sheaves on
\operatorname{ProfSet}^{light}_{/S}
which preserve countable filtered colimits (as functors
(\operatorname{ProfSet}^{light}_{/S})^{op}\to\operatorname{An}_{\leq d}
), i.e.
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(S)_{\leq d}
.

We claim that restriction and left Kan extension identify 1 and 2, and restriction and right Kan extension identify 2 and 3. Assuming this, the composition of these equivalences restricts to the inclusion
\operatorname{fEt_{\pi}}_{/S}\rightarrow\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(S)
, so it agrees with the comparison map, finishing the proof.
For the claim about 1 and 2, we need to show that a functor
\mathcal{F}:(\operatorname{ProfAn}^{light}_{/S})^{op}\to\operatorname{An}_{\leq d}
preserving countable filtered colimits whose restriction to
\operatorname{fEt_{\pi}}_{/S}
is a sheaf is itself necessarily a sheaf. Finite disjoint unions correspond to maps from some object to a finite set, hence come from a finite stage in a defining filtered inverse limit, so the sheaf property for finite disjoint unions follows from the fact that finite products commute with filtered colimits. For surjections, using Lemma
4.27
we can write any surjection
T^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow T
in
\operatorname{ProfAn}^{light}_{/S}
as a sequential filtered limit of surjective maps in
\operatorname{fEt_{\pi}}_{/S}
, and the conclusion follows by commuting the (finite, by truncatedness) limit over
\Delta
with the filtered colimit. For the claim about 2 and 3, note that
\operatorname{ProfSet}^{light}_{/S}\subset\operatorname{ProfAn}^{light}_{/S}
is closed under pullbacks, and every
X\in\operatorname{ProfAn}^{light}_{/S}
admits a surjection from a
Y\in\operatorname{ProfSet}^{light}_{/S}
by Lemma
4.23
. By
[
Hoy15
]
Lemma C.3 this gives the claim if we remove the condition of preserving countable filtered colimits from both 2 and 3. Thus, to finish it suffices to show that if a sheaf
\mathcal{F}:(\operatorname{ProfAn}^{light}_{/S})^{op}\to\operatorname{An}_{\leq d}
is such that its restriction to
(\operatorname{ProfSet}^{light}_{/S})^{op}
preserves countable filtered colimits, then
\mathcal{F}
also preserves countable filtered colimits. For this, suppose given a tower
(X_{n})_{n}
over
S
of light profinite anima. Proceeding inductively using the fact that every
X\in\operatorname{ProfAn}^{light}_{/S}
admits a surjection from a
Y\in\operatorname{ProfSet}^{light}_{/S}
, we can make a hypercover of this tower by a tower of light profinite sets. Then the question amounts to commuting a filtered colimit with a limit over
\Delta
, which is handled as usual using truncatedness. ∎

Remark 4.29
.

For
S\in\operatorname{ProfAn}^{light}
, the Grothendieck topology on
\operatorname{fEt}^{\pi}_{/S}
inherited from
\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}
is simple to understand. In fact, more generally, the Grothendieck topology on
\operatorname{ProfAn}^{light}
can be described as follows: a sieve over
S\in\operatorname{ProfAn}^{light}
is covering if and only if it contains finitely many
\{f_{i}:S_{i}\rightarrow S\}_{i\in I}
such that for all
x:\ast\rightarrow S
, there is an
i\in I
such that
x
lifts along
f_{i}:S_{i}\rightarrow S
. Note that this generalizes the defining Grothendieck topology on
\operatorname{ProfSet}^{light}
. The proof is straightforward using that every
S\in\operatorname{ProfAn}^{light}
admits a surjective map from an
T\in\operatorname{ProfSet}^{light}
.

In practice, a truncated variant of this theorem is useful. Namely, for
d\geq 0
, consider the
\infty
-category of
d
-truncated
\pi
-finite anima,
\operatorname{An}^{\pi}_{\leq d}
. The induced functor
\operatorname{Pro}^{\aleph_{0}}(\operatorname{An}^{\pi}_{\leq d})\rightarrow\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}
is of course still fully faithful, and we call the essential image the full subcategory of
d
-truncated light profinite anima
.
1
1

1

There is in principle a question to be addressed, as to whether every light profinite anima which is
d
-truncated in the sense of the t-structure on
\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}
is actually a
d
-truncated light profinite anima in this sense. This is true and follows from Lemma
4.35
.

For a
d
-truncated light profinite anima
S=\varprojlim_{n}S_{n}
, we can consider the full subcategory
\operatorname{fEt^{\pi}_{\leq d-1}}_{/S}
of
\operatorname{fEt^{\pi}}_{/S}=\varinjlim_{n}\operatorname{An}^{\pi}_{/S_{n}}
consisting of the
(d-1)
-truncated maps
X\rightarrow S
, and equip it with the Grothendieck topology induced from
\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}
.

Then we have the following.

Corollary 4.30
.

Let
d\geq 0
. Suppose
S
is a
d
-truncated light profinite anima. Then the comparison morphism in
\mathcal{T}op^{L}

\operatorname{Sh}(\operatorname{fEt^{\pi}_{\leq d-1}}_{/S})\rightarrow\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(S)

exhibits the target as the Postnikov-completion of the source.

Proof.

By the theorem, it suffices to show that the comparison functor
\operatorname{Sh}(\operatorname{fEt^{\pi}_{\leq d-1}}_{/S})\rightarrow\operatorname{Sh}(\operatorname{fEt^{\pi}}_{/S})
is an equivalence. But
\operatorname{fEt^{\pi}_{\leq d-1}}_{/S}
is closed under finite limits, so by
[
Hoy15
]
Lemma C.3 for this it suffices to see that for every
X\rightarrow S
in
\operatorname{fEt^{\pi}}_{/S}
, there is a finite jointly surjective family of maps
\{X_{i}\rightarrow X\}_{i\in I}
such that
X_{i}\rightarrow S
is in
\operatorname{fEt^{\pi}_{\leq d-1}}_{/S}
for all
i\in I
. By base change we can assume
X,S\in\operatorname{An}^{\pi}
. Then take
I=\pi_{0}X
, take
X_{i}=\ast
for all
i\in I
, and let the map
X_{i}\rightarrow X
be any map hitting the
i^{th}
component.
∎

Example 4.31
.

In the case
d=0
, the
0
-truncated light profinite anima are exactly the light profinite sets
S
, and the
-1
-truncated finite etale maps to
S
are exactly the inclusions of compact open subsets. Thus when
d=0
this corollary recovers Theorem
4.14
.

Example 4.32
.

Let
G
be a light profinite group, written as the limit of a tower
(G_{n})_{n}
of finite groups. We then have

\ast/G\simeq\varprojlim_{n}\ast/G_{n}.

Indeed, by repleteness the map
\ast\rightarrow\varprojlim_{n}\ast/G_{n}
is surjective, so we can test on base change along it, and then we exactly reduce to
G\simeq\varprojlim_{n}G_{n}
.

Thus
\ast/G
is a
1
-truncated light profinite anima. A
0
-truncated finite etale map to
\ast/G
is the same thing as a finite set with a continuous
G
-action. Thus, in this case Corollary
4.30
shows that
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(\ast/G)
is the Postnikov completion of the “usual”
\infty
-topos of sheaves of anima on the site of finite continuous
G
-sets (see
[
CM21
]
4.1).

Remark 4.33
.

We recall that classically, the 1-topos of sheaves of sets on the site of finite sets with continuous
G
-action has an alternate description: it is the category of (arbitrary) sets with continuous
G
-action. The
\infty
-topos
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(\ast/G)
is already the direct
\infty
-categorical generalization of this. Indeed, by descent
\operatorname{Sh}_{v}(\ast/G)
identifies with the
\infty
-category of light condensed anima with
G
-action, and by definition
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(\ast/G)
is the full subcategory where the underlying light condensed anima is “discrete”, i.e. etale over
\ast
.

We can also deduce the following convenient “continuity” result.

Proposition 4.34
.

Let
S
be a light profinite anima, and let

\ldots\to X_{n}\to X_{n-1}\to\ldots\to X_{0}\to S

be a tower of light profinite anima over
S
, with limit
X=\varprojlim X_{n}
. Then if
\mathcal{F}\in\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(S)
is
d
-truncated for some
d
, we have

\Gamma(X;\mathcal{F})=\varinjlim_{n}\Gamma(X_{n};\mathcal{F}).

Proof.

By Lemma
4.26
, the (finitary) site
\operatorname{fEt}^{\pi}_{/X}
is the filtered colimit of the
\operatorname{fEt}^{\pi}_{/X_{n}}
. Thus the analog of this result for sheaves on the
\pi
-finite etale sites follows from
[
CM21
]
Lemma 3.3. Then we deduce the claim from Theorem
4.28
.
∎

To conclude this section, we prove a general “
\operatorname{lim}^{1}
-vanishing result” in the setting of light profinite anima, showing that Postnikov truncations can be taken termwise.

Proposition 4.35
.

Suppose
(X_{n})_{n}
is a tower of light profinite anima, with limit
X=\varprojlim_{n}X_{n}
in
\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}
. Then for all
d\geq-1
, the map

\tau_{\leq d}X\rightarrow\varprojlim_{n}\tau_{\leq d}X_{n}

is an isomorphism in
\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}
.

More generally, if
(f_{n}):(X_{n})\rightarrow(Y_{n})
is a map of towers of light profinite anima, then taking the inverse limit in
\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}
commutes with relative Postnikov truncation.

Proof.

Let us immediately handle the more general relative case. By Lemma
4.23
, we can write
X_{0}\to Y_{0}
as the sequential inverse limit of a map of
\pi
-finite anima. Then we do the same for
Y_{1}\to Y_{0}
, but using the same tower for
Y_{0}
as before (again this is possible by Lemma
4.23
). Then we do the same again for
X_{1}\to Y_{1}\times_{Y_{0}}X_{0}
, using the tower on the target coming from what we’ve already constructed. Continuing in this manner, we write our map of towers as a sequential inverse limit of maps of towers of
\pi
-finite anima. Thus we can reduce our claim to the case where all
X_{n},Y_{n}
are
\pi
-finite anima.

Consider the factorization

X_{n}\rightarrow\tau_{\leq d}f_{n}\rightarrow Y_{n}

of
f_{n}
into a
d+1
-connective map followed by a
d
-truncated map. Taking inverse limits, we get

X\rightarrow\varprojlim_{n}\tau_{\leq d}f_{n}\rightarrow Y.

The second map is
d
-truncated because a limit of
d
-truncated maps is
d
-truncated. Thus it suffices to show that the first map is
d+1
-connective, or in other words, a limit of a tower of
d+1
-connective maps of
\pi
-finite anima is
d+1
-connective. Choose a tower finite sets with a surjection to the target tower such that the map on inverse limits is still a surjection (using Lemma
4.23
). Then by base change, we reduce to the case where the target tower consists of finite sets.

When
d=-1
, we need to show that if our map
(X_{n})\rightarrow(S_{n})
(with
S_{n}
a tower of finite sets and
X_{n}
a tower of
\pi
-finite anima) is termwise surjective, then
\varprojlim_{n}X_{n}\rightarrow\varprojlim_{n}S_{n}
is also surjective. Choosing also a tower of finite sets surjecting onto
(X_{n})
, we reduce to the case that both
(X_{n})
and
(S_{n})
are towers of finite sets. Then it is a consequence of the fact that a filtered inverse limit of nonempty finite sets is nonempty (by a standard compactness argument).

For
d\geq 0
, we proceed inductively. Suppose
(X_{n})\rightarrow(S_{n})
is
d+1
-connective, in particular it induces
\pi_{0}(X_{n})\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}S_{n}
. Using this we can define, proceeding inductively on the tower, a section of
(X_{n})\rightarrow(S_{n})
, giving in particular a section
\sigma:S\rightarrow X
on inverse limits. By the
d=-1
case,
\sigma
is surjective. On the other hand,
S\times_{X}S\rightarrow S
(the fiber product being formed using the map
\sigma
) is the inverse limit of a tower of
d
-connective maps of
\pi
-finite anima. (We have in essence passed to the based loop spaces on fibers.) Thus by induction
S\times_{X}S\rightarrow S
is
d
-connective, so
X\rightarrow S
is
d+1
-connective as desired.
∎

5
Light condensed anima attached to sheaves on manifolds

Above, we briefly discussed the light condensed sets
\underline{X}
associated to topological spaces
X
which are “locally compact” in the slightly nonstandard sense that each point admits a second-countable compact Hausdorff neighborhood. We showed that for such an
X
, the category
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(X)
of etale sheaves on
X
identifies with the Postnikov completion of the category of sheaves of anima on the topological space
X
.

This result implies in particular to
F
-manifolds if
F
is a
local field
(a non-discrete locally compact field). That case is actually even simpler: any compact subset of
F^{d}
has finite covering dimension (less than or equal to
0
if
F
is nonarchimedean,
d
if
F=\mathbb{R}
, and
2d
if
F\simeq\mathbb{C}
.) Thus
\operatorname{Sh}(M)
is Postnikov-complete by
[
Lur09
]
Cor. 7.2.3.7, and so the result of Lemma
4.20
shows that

\operatorname{Sh}(M)\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(\underline{M})

for any
F
-manifold
M
: etale sheaves on
F
-manifolds identify with usual sheaves.

In this paper we are not directly interested in
F
-manifolds; rather our purported focus is on cohomology of
F
-Lie groups
G
. For this it is convenient to pass from
G
to some version of the classifying stack
BG=\ast/G
. For some of our arguments, we will also have to consider more general quotient stacks. To form such a quotient
M/G
, we could of course consider the underlying light condensed sets of
G
and
M
and then form this quotient in light condensed anima. But for some purposes we’ll want to remember the
F
-analytic structure on such an object. For this we make the following standard definitions.

Definition 5.1
.

Let
F
be a local field. A
sheaf on
F
-manifolds
is a sheaf (of anima) on the category
\operatorname{Man}_{F}
with respect to the Grothendieck topology generated by open covers.

Remark 5.2
.

Since
\operatorname{Man}_{F}
is not a small category, in principle there are cardinality issues in this definition. But since every manifold by definition admits an open cover by open subsets of some
F^{d}
, it follows from
[
Hoy15
]
Lemma C.3 that we could equivalently restrict to the full subcategory of
\operatorname{Man}_{F}
consisting of such open sets and the sheaf category would stay the same. In particular
\operatorname{Sh}(\operatorname{Man}_{F})
is an
\infty
-topos.

Moreover,
\operatorname{Sh}(\operatorname{Man}_{F})
is hypercomplete. This follows from the fact that
F
-manifolds have local finite covering dimension and
[
Lur09
]
Thm. 7.2.3.6. Note that every
\mathcal{F}\in\operatorname{Sh}(\operatorname{Man}_{F})
admits a hypercover by
F
-manifolds, or even by disjoint unions of open polydisks.

Now we describe the “forgetful functor” from sheaves on
F
-manifolds to light condensed anima.

Proposition 5.3
.

Let
F
be a local field.

1.

There is a unique colimit-preserving functor

\operatorname{Sh}(\operatorname{Man}_{F})\rightarrow\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}

whose restriction to
\operatorname{Man}_{F}
is the functor
M\mapsto\underline{M^{top}}:\operatorname{Man}_{F}\rightarrow\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}
, where
M^{top}
denotes the underlying topological space of
M
. By abuse of notation, denote this functor also by
X\mapsto\underline{X}
(or sometimes, if we’re being really abusive, just
X\mapsto X
with the context being implicit).

2.

This functor
X\mapsto\underline{X}
preserves pullbacks of diagrams of the form
X\rightarrow S\leftarrow Y
whenever
X\to S
can, locally on
S
, be written as a colimit of submersions of manifolds
X_{i}\to S
.

Proof.

Part 1 follows formally from Lemma
4.20
. For part 2, we can use that colimits distribute over pullbacks in any
\infty
-topos to reduce to the claim that pullbacks in
\operatorname{Man}_{F}
by submersions are representable and commute with the forgetful functor to condensed sets.
∎

Remark 5.4
.

A special case of 2 is that
X\mapsto\underline{X}
preserves finite products. In particular, it sends group objects to group objects. Moreover, if
G
is a group object in
\operatorname{Sh}(\operatorname{Man}_{F})
, then

\underline{\ast/G}=\ast/\underline{G}

by commutation with colimits and finite products.

Remark 5.5
.

It seems likely that
X\mapsto\underline{X}
commutes with arbitrary pullbacks, hence gives a morphism in
\mathcal{T}op^{L}
. But we won’t need this.

Up until now we’ve done things uniformly in
F
. But of course there are major structural differences between
F
-manifolds for archimedean and non-archimedean
F
. One such difference is that when
F
is archimedean, every
F
-manifold is locally contractible. Local contractibility gives a good theory of locally constant sheaves, see
[
Lur17
]
App. A, and relatedly shows that every
F
-manifold has an “underlying anima”. We now give an expression of this phenomenon in the setting of etale sheaves. There is no real loss in restricting to
F=\mathbb{R}
, so we will.

Lemma 5.6
.

1.

For
X\in\operatorname{Sh}(\operatorname{Man}_{\mathbb{R}})
, there is an
|X|\in\operatorname{An}
and a map
\underline{X}\rightarrow|X|
in
\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}
such that the pullback functor
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(|X|)\rightarrow\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(\underline{X})
is fully faithful.

2.

Given
\underline{X}\rightarrow|X|
as in 1, for arbitrary
A\in\operatorname{An}
we have

\operatorname{Map}(|X|,A)\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\operatorname{Map}(\underline{X},A).

In particular,
X\mapsto|X|
is functorial in
\underline{X}
.

3.

For
X\in\operatorname{Sh}(\operatorname{Man}_{\mathbb{R}})
, the essential image of the fully faithful pullback functor
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(|X|)\rightarrow\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(\underline{X})
from 1 consists of the locally constant sheaves, meaning those sheaves
\mathcal{F}\in\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(\underline{X})
such that there exists a cover
\{f_{i}:X_{i}\rightarrow X\}_{i\in I}
in
\operatorname{Sh}(\operatorname{Man}_{\mathbb{R}})
such that
f_{i}^{\ast}\mathcal{F}
is pulled back along
X_{i}\rightarrow\ast
, for all
i\in I
.

4.

The functor
X\mapsto|X|:\operatorname{Sh}(\operatorname{Man}_{\mathbb{R}})\rightarrow\operatorname{An}
is uniquely characterized as follows: it preserves all colimits, and on the full subcategory of open polydisks it identifies with the constant functor with value
\ast\in\operatorname{An}
. More precisely, the full subcategory of
\operatorname{Fun}(\operatorname{Sh}(\operatorname{Man}_{\mathbb{R}}),\operatorname{An})
spanned by the functors satisfiying these two conditions is equivalent to the terminal
\infty
-category
\ast
.

5.

The functor
X\mapsto|X|
preserves finite products.

Proof.

Note that 2 follows from 1, since mapping to
A
is the same as giving a section of the etale projection map
A\times(-)\rightarrow(-)
. It follows that collection of
X
satisfying 1 is closed under colimits. Thus (using hypercovers) we can reduce to the case where
X
is an open polydisk. Then we claim
|X|=\ast
works. Indeed, this follows by contractibility, see
[
Lur17
]
A.2.

For 3, note that we just showed that if a sheaf is in the essential image, then it is a constant sheaf on any open polydisk, hence it is locally constant. For the converse, suppose
\mathcal{F}\in\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(\underline{X})
is locally constant. Then
\mathcal{F}
locally comes from
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(|X|)
. But the property of lying in
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(|X|)\subset\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(X)
is a local one thanks to the colimit preservation of
X\mapsto|X|
, so
\mathcal{F}
comes from
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(|X|)
as required.

For 4, note that by hypercompleteness that every colimit-preserving functor out of
\operatorname{Sh}(\operatorname{Man}_{\mathbb{R}})
is the left Kan extension of its restriction to open polydisks. But for any
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
, the full subcategory of functors
\mathcal{C}\to\operatorname{An}
spanned by the constant functor with value
\ast
is equivalent to
\ast
, whence the claim.

For 5, since colimits distribute finite products (in any
\infty
-topos, so in particular in
\operatorname{Sh}(\operatorname{Man}_{\mathbb{R}})
and in
\operatorname{An}
), we can reduce to the case of open polydisks, which is obvious because
\ast\times\ast=\ast
.
∎

Remark 5.7
.

We can interpret
|X|
as the “homotopy type”, or “underlying anima”, of
X
. Claim 4 makes it easy to identify this model for the homotopy type with any other one. For example, given
M\in\operatorname{Man}_{\mathbb{R}}
we can consider the usual Kan complex
\operatorname{Sing}(M)
of singular simplices on
M
(so
\operatorname{Sing}(M)_{n}=\operatorname{Cont}(\Delta^{n},M)
), and its realization
\varinjlim_{\Delta^{op}}\operatorname{Sing}(M)
in
\operatorname{An}
. The functor

M\mapsto\varinjlim_{\Delta^{op}}\operatorname{Sing}(M)

from
\operatorname{Man}_{F}
to
\operatorname{An}
is a cosheaf, because of the standard Mayer-Vietoris property for binary covers and the fact that
\operatorname{Sing}(-)
preserves filtered unions of open subsets. Thus it extends uniquely to a colimit-preserving functor
\operatorname{Sh}(\operatorname{Man}_{F})\rightarrow\operatorname{An}
. On the other hand, its value on an open polydisk is
\ast
by contractibility, so we deduce that the usual singular simplicial Kan complex of an
F
-manifold
M
gives a model for the abstract
|M|
considered in Lemma
5.6
. Note, however, that the map
\underline{M}\rightarrow|M|
, which is a fundamental structure, is not at all clear from the perspective of the singular simplicial complex.

This gives the following.

Theorem 5.8
.

Let
X\in\operatorname{Sh}(\operatorname{Man}_{\mathbb{R}})
with underlying anima
|X|
as defined above. The functor

\operatorname{An}_{/|X|}\rightarrow\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(\underline{X}),

arising from pull back of etale sheaves under the map
\underline{X}\rightarrow|X|
, is fully faithful. The essential image consists of those
\mathcal{F}\in\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(\underline{X})
such that there is a cover
\{f_{i}:X_{i}\rightarrow X\}_{i\in I}
in
\operatorname{Sh}(\operatorname{Man}_{\mathbb{R}})
for which the pullback
f_{i}^{\ast}\mathcal{F}\in\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(\underline{X_{i}})
is constant for all
i\in I
.

Proof.

This follows by combining Lemmas
5.6
and
4
.
∎

Corollary 5.9
.

Let
G
be an
\mathbb{R}
-Lie group with underlying group anima
|G|
. Then

\operatorname{An}_{/(\ast/|G|)}\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(\underline{\ast/G}).

In other words, an etale sheaf on
\ast/G
is the same as an etale sheaf on
\ast/|G|
which is the same thing as an anima with a
|G|
-action.

Proof.

The map
\ast\rightarrow\ast/G
gives a cover such that, tautologically, any sheaf is constant on pullback along it. Thus this follows from Theorem
5.8
.
∎

Example 5.10
.

We have
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(\ast/\mathbb{R})\overset{\sim}{\leftarrow}\operatorname{An}
via pullback, so the theory of etale sheaves on
\ast/\mathbb{R}
is trivial… Or is it? [Cue dramatic music.] It turns out that the six functor formalism we consider will do something nontrivial even in this situation, and this is actually a crucial phenomenon, see Section
11
.

It is convenient to make the following standard definition, as a reasonable category in which all our examples of interest will live.

Definition 5.11
.

Let
F
be a local field. An
F
-analytic smooth Artin stack
is an
X\in\operatorname{Sh}(\operatorname{Man}_{F})
such that there exists a surjective representable submersion
M\twoheadrightarrow X
with
M\in\operatorname{Man}_{F}
.

Remark 5.12
.

A representable submersion is surjective in the sense of
\infty
-topos theory if and only if on pullback to any manifold it is surjective as a map of sets. This follows because a submersion has open image, and a surjective submersion has local sections (by the implicit function theorem).

Example 5.13
.

1.

Suppose
G
is an
F
-Lie group. The
classifying stack

\ast/G
, also denoted
BG
, is an
F
-analytic smooth Artin stack.

2.

More generally, suppose
G
is an
F
-Lie group acting on an
F
-manifold
M
. Then the quotient
M/G
is an
F
-analytic smooth Artin stack, with cover
M\to M/G
. This kind of example is called a
quotient stack
.

3.

Even more generally, if
N\in\operatorname{Man}_{F}
, and
G\to N
is an
F
-Lie group over
N
(a group object in submersions with target
N
), then for any
F
-manifold
M\to N
mapping to
N
(not necessarily by a submerison) with an action by
G
in the slice category of manifolds over
N
, the quotient
M/G
is an
F
-analyic smooth Artin stack. We’ll call this a
relative quotient stack
.

In the
p
-adic case, we will have to make a study of families of Lie groups. Recall that Lazard showed that every
p
-adic Lie group
G
admits a compact open subgroup
H
which is a
uniform pro-
p
-group
. Here we use the language of
[
Dix+03
]
instead of
[
Laz65
]
, and refer to the nice lecture notes
[
Pst
]
for more on this classical situation.

In particular, we recall the following properties of uniform pro-
p
-groups
H
:

1.

The subset of
p^{n}
-powers
H^{p^{n}}\subset H
is an open normal subgroup, and
\cap_{n}H^{p^{n}}=\{e\}
.

2.

Each
H^{p^{n}}/H^{p^{n+1}}
is abelian, hence an
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-vector space, and has dimension
d
where
d=\operatorname{dim}(H)=\operatorname{dim}(G)
;

3.

For the continuous group cohomology of
H
with constant
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-coefficients, we have:

(a)

The natural map
H^{1}(H;\mathbb{F}_{p})\to\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}(H/H^{p},\mathbb{F}_{p})
is an isomorphism.

(b)

For all
x\in H^{1}(H;\mathbb{F}_{p})
, we have
x^{2}=0
in
H^{2}(H;\mathbb{F}_{p})
, and the induced map

\Lambda^{i}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}H^{1}(H;\mathbb{F}_{p})\to H^{i}(H;\mathbb{F}_{p})

is an isomorphism for all
i\geq 0
.

Now we go to the in-families version. If
M
is a
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-manifold, recall that a
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-Lie group over
M
is a group object in the slice category of submersions with target
M
.

Lemma 5.14
.

Let
n,m\geq 0
and let
\varphi:U\to\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{m}
be a
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-analytic map from an open subset
U\subset\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{n}
containing
0
such that:

1.

\varphi(0)=0
;

2.

the
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-linear map
d\varphi|_{0}:\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{n}\to\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{m}
sends
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{n}
inside
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{m}
.

Then there is an
N\geq 0
such that:

1.

p^{N}\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{n}\subset U
;

2.

The power series expansion of
\varphi
at the origin converges on
p^{N}\mathbb{Z}_{p}
;

3.

If we re-coordinatize both source and target by replacing the old coordinate
X
with the new coordinate
X^{\prime}=p^{N}X
, then each coefficient in the Taylor expansion of
\varphi
at the origin lies in
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
;

4.

With respect to the new coordinates,
\varphi(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{n})\subset\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{m}
.

Proof.

We can assume
m=1
by working coordinate-wise. It is clearly no problem to ensure 1 and 2. In fact if we write the power series expansion of
\varphi
as

\varphi(X)=\sum_{i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{n}}c_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}}x_{1}^{i_{1}}\ldots x_{n}^{i_{n}},

then by definition of locally analytic we can ensure that

\sum_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}}|c_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}}|p^{-N(i_{1}+\ldots+i_{n})}<\infty,

and in particular for all but finitely many
i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}
we have
c_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}}p^{N(i_{1}+\ldots+i_{n})}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. Since the constant term vanishes, by increasing
N
we can even ensure this for all
i_{1},\ldots,i_{N}
. If we make the re-coordinatizations
X^{\prime}=p^{M}X
on both source and target, this changes
c_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}}
to

c^{\prime}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}}=\frac{1}{p^{M}}p^{M(i_{1}+\ldots+i_{n})}c_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}}.

The linear terms are unchanged, but we assumed those are in
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
anyway. Thus if we take
M=2N
we get all the coefficients to lie in
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, meaning we can ensure 1, 2, and 3. But then 4 follows because
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
is closed under convergent sums in
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
.

∎

Proposition 5.15
.

Let
p
be a prime, let
M
be a compact Hausdorff
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-manifold, and let
G\to M
be a
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-Lie group over
M
. Then there exists a compact Hausdorff open sub-group object
H\subset G
over
M
such that for all
m\in M
, the fiber
H_{m}
is a uniform pro-
p
-group.

Proof.

Note that because
M
is profinite, we are free to work locally on
M
; the gluing back up will be trivial. In particular we can assume
M=\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{e}
for some
e\geq 0
. Using the implicit function theorem, again working locally on
M
, we can write
G\to M
in local coordinates around the identity section as the projection

\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{d}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{e}\to\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{e},

such that the identity section corresponds to
t\mapsto(0,t)
. Now consider the map
\varphi:G\times_{M}G\to G
defined by
(g,h)\mapsto gh^{-1}
. Note that an open subset
H\subset G
containing the identity section is a sub-group object if and only if
\varphi(H\times_{M}H)\subset H
. Restricting to our coordinate chart
\varphi
takes the form

\varphi:\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{d}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{d}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{e}\to G,

lying over
M=\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{e}
. By continuity there is an
M\geq 0
such that
\varphi
sends
p^{M}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{d+d}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{e})
inside our coordinate chart
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{d}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{e}
again. Now, we have
\varphi(0)=0
, and

d\varphi|_{0}(X,Y,Z)=X-Y+Z,

Thus we can apply Lemma
5.14
to conclude that there is some
N\geq 0
such that after changing all the coordinates via
T^{\prime}=p^{N}T
, we have

\varphi(\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{d}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{d}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{e})\subset\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{d}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{e},

and in fact the power series expansion of
\varphi
at
0
has
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-coefficients.

It follows that
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{d}\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{e}
defines a compact open subgroup object of
G
. Moreover, if we specialize to the fiber over any
t\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{e}
, we get that the group law is defined by a (convergent) formal group law with
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-coefficients. By
[
Dix+03
]
8.4, then, if we further restrict to
(2p\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{d})\times\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{e}
, then this gives a uniform pro-
p
-group (and still a compact open subgroup object).
∎

Now let us describe some consequences for the associated light condensed anima.

Proposition 5.16
.

Let
M
be a compact Hausdorff
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-manifold, let
G\to M
be a group object in sumbersions over
M
, and let
H\subset G
be a compact Hausdorff open sub-group object such that each fiber
H_{x}
is a uniform pro-
p
-group (whose existence is guaranteed by the previous result). Let
BH\in\operatorname{Sh}(\operatorname{Man}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}})
denote the classifying stack of
H
relative to
M
, and similarly for
H^{p^{n}}
or
G
.

Then:

1.

For
n\geq 1
, the subset
H^{p^{n}}\subset G
is also a compact open sub-group object.

2.

\underline{BH}\to\underline{BG}
is etale, as is
\underline{BH^{p^{n}}}\to\underline{BH}
for all
n\geq 0
.

3.

\underline{BH}=\varprojlim_{n}\underline{B(H/H^{p^{n}})}
.

4.

\underline{BH}
is a 1-truncated light profinite anima.

Proof.

By definition
H^{p^{n}}\subset H
is the image of the
p
-power map
H\to H
, hence it is compact. It is a sub-group object because this is so fiberwise (
5
). Then
H/H^{p^{n}}
is the quotient of a compact Hausdorff space by a closed equivalence relation, hence compact Hausdorff. The map
H/H^{p^{n}}\to M
of compact Hausdorff spaces has finite fibers, and the cardinality of the fibers is a locally constant function of the base (by
5
); it follows that
H/H^{p^{n}}\to M
is finite etale (a finite covering map), and a fortiori
H^{p^{n}}\subset H
is open, proving 1.

Next we claim that if
G^{\prime}\subset G
is any open sub-group object, the quotient
G/G^{\prime}\to M
is etale. Indeed because
G\to M
is a submersion it admits local sections through any given point, hence so does
G/G^{\prime}\to M
. The conclusion follows easily. In this situation we have that
BG^{\prime}\to BG
is a pullback of
G/G^{\prime}\to M
, hence it is also etale. Moreover this pullback is preserved by the functor to light condensed anima by Lemma
5.3
, and we deduce 2.

Now consider 3. By repleteness
M
surjects onto the target, so we can test this map being an isomorphism on base change to
M
. Thus we reduce to showing

\varprojlim_{n}\underline{BH^{p^{n}}}\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\underline{M}.

Using the same trick again with repleteness and pullbacks, we further reduce to showing
M\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\varprojlim_{n}H^{p^{n}}
. But this is a map of compact Hausdorff spaces and it’s a bijection as we see looking fiberwise.

Finally, for 4, given 3 it suffices to show that each
\underline{B(H/H^{p^{n}})}
is a 1-truncated light profinite anima. But we saw above that
H/H^{p^{n}}\to M
is finite etale. It follows that as a group object it is locally constant with finite fibers, and the claim follows.
∎

Thus, in particular, for any
M\in\operatorname{Man}_{F}
and any Lie group object
G\to M
over
M
, the relative
BG
has the following property: etale locally, the associated light condensed anima is light profinite. More generally one can deduce that the same claim holds for any quotient stack or relative quotient stack. I conjecture that this actually holds for all
p
-adic analytic smooth Artin stacks:

Conjecture 5.17
.

Let
X
be a
p
-adic analytic smooth Artin stack. Then étale locally, the associated light condensed anima is a light profinite anima.

If true, this would imply that every
p
-adic analytic smooth Artin stack is “
!
-good” in the sense of Theorem
8.10
.

6
Interlude on descendability in sheaf categories

For
\mathcal{C}\in\operatorname{CAlg}(\operatorname{Pr}_{st}^{L})
, or equivalently
\mathcal{C}
a symmetric monoidal presentable stable
\infty
-category where the tensor product distributes over colimits, Mathew in
[
Mat16
]
,
[
Mat18
]
defined and studied the condition of
descendability
on a commutative algebra object
A\in\operatorname{CAlg}(\mathcal{C})
. This notion has important technical relevance in the construction of six functor formalisms, so we will prove some results giving criteria for descendability in (certain) sheaf categories.

We start with a review of some aspects of Mathew’s theory. For the basic definition, actually, the commutative algebra structure on
A
is not relevant.

Definition 6.1
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a symmetric monoidal stable
\infty
-category and let
A\in\mathcal{C}
.

1.

Say a map
f:X\to Y
in
\mathcal{C}
is
A
-null
if the induced map
X\otimes A\to Y\otimes A
in
\mathcal{C}
is null, i.e. homotopic to
0
.

2.

For
d\geq 0
, say that
A
is
descendable of index
\leq d
if whenever
X_{0}\to X_{1}\to\ldots\to X_{d}
is a sequence of
d
composable
A
-null maps, the composition
X_{0}\to X_{d}
is null.

3.

Say that
A
is descendable if if is descendable of index
\leq d
for some
d\geq 0
.

Example 6.2
.

Suppose
\mathcal{C}=\operatorname{D}(k)
for a field
k
. Then
A\in\mathcal{C}
is descendable if and only if
A\neq 0
, in which case it is descendable of index
\leq 1
. Indeed, every nonzero
A\in\operatorname{D}(k)
admits
k[n]
as a summand for some
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Remark 6.3
.

If
A\in\mathcal{C}
is descendable, then
-\otimes A:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}
is conservative, because
X\otimes A=0
means that
\operatorname{id}_{X}
is
A
-null. The converse fails: in
\mathcal{C}=\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Z})
, the object
A=\mathbb{Z}[1/p]\times\mathbb{F}_{p}
satisfies that
-\otimes A
is conservative, but it is not descsendable, because

\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z}\overset{p}{\to}\mathbb{Z}/p^{2}\mathbb{Z}\overset{p}{\to}\ldots

gives an infinite sequence of
A
-null maps where no composition is null.

Remark 6.4
.

In verifying the definition of descendability for an
A\in\operatorname{CAlg}(\mathcal{C})
, it is enough to consider only a certain basic instance of a composition of
A
-null maps. Namely, set
I=\operatorname{Fib}(1\to A)
, and denote by
\alpha:I\to 1
the canonical map. Note that
\alpha
is
A
-null. Define inductively a sequence of maps

\alpha_{d}:I^{\otimes d}\to 1

by taking
\alpha_{0}
to be the identity map
1\to 1
, and taking
\alpha_{d}
for
d\geq 1
to be the composition

\alpha_{d}:I^{\otimes d}\overset{\operatorname{id}\otimes\alpha}{\longrightarrow}I^{\otimes d-1}\overset{\alpha_{d-1}}{\longrightarrow}1.

Then
A
is descendable of index
\leq d
if and only if
\alpha_{d}
is null. This criterion in particular shows that symmetric monoidal functors send descendable algebras of index
\leq d
to descendable algebras of index
\leq d
.

How to prove descendability in a given case? Sometimes one can use devissage to reduce complicated cases to some more basic ones, a nice example being given in
[
BS17
]
11.2. But in the end, one is faced with the problem of proving that some
\alpha_{d}
map is null. Sometimes, one can proceed by finding an auxiliary property
\mathcal{P}
of maps, such that every sufficiently long composition of
A
-null maps satisfies
\mathcal{P}
, and any sufficiently long composition of maps satisfying
\mathcal{P}
is null. One model for this is Bousfield’s argument in the context of spectra (see
[
Bou79
]
Section 6), where the relevant property
\mathcal{P}
is that of being a
phantom map
. We would like to place Bousfield’s argument in a more general context, starting with the following definition.

Definition 6.5
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a presentable stable
\infty
-category. Say that a map
f:X\to Y
is
phantom
if for every colimit-preserving functor
F:\mathcal{C}\to\operatorname{Sp}
, the induced map of abelian groups

\pi_{0}F(X)\to\pi_{0}F(Y)

is zero.

Example 6.6
.

If
\mathcal{C}
is compactly generated, then then the colimit preserving functors
F:\mathcal{C}\to\operatorname{Sp}
are exactly the filtered colimits of functors of the form
\operatorname{map}(c,-)
for some
c\in\mathcal{C}^{\aleph_{0}}
. It follows that the above definition recovers the usual one: a map
f:X\to Y
is phantom if and only if for every compact object
c
, the induced map
[c,X]\to[c,Y]
is zero.

Example 6.7
.

More generally, suppose
\mathcal{C}
is
dualizable
. Then every colimit-preserving functor
\mathcal{C}\to\operatorname{Sp}
is a filtered colimit of functors of the form

\operatorname{map}_{cpct}(c,-)=\operatorname{map}(c,\hat{y}(-))

for
c\in\mathcal{C}
. Here
\hat{y}:\mathcal{C}\to\operatorname{Ind}(\mathcal{C})
is the
left
adjoint to the colimit functor, which exists when
\mathcal{C}
is dualizable. It follows that in the dualizable case, Definition
6.5
agrees with that given in
[
Efi25
]
App. E: a map
f:X\to Y
is phantom if and only if for every
c\in\mathcal{C}
and every compact map
g:c\to X
, we have
g\circ f\sim 0
.

Lemma 6.8
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a presentable stable
\infty
-category.

1.

Any shift or retract of a phantom map is phantom.

2.

Phantom maps form a 2-sided ideal: they are closed under addition and under composition on either side with an arbitrary map.

3.

If
A:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
is a colimit-preserving functor to another presentable stable
\infty
-category, then
A
sends phantom maps to phantom maps.

Proof.

For closure under shifts note that since the class of colimit-preserving functors is closed under shifts, we could replace the condition that
\pi_{0}F(X)\to\pi_{0}F(Y)
should be
0
in the definition with the condition that
F(X)\to F(Y)
should be zero on all homotopy groups. The rest of the properties are straightforward from the definition.
∎

We can attempt to bridge the gap between phantom maps and nullhomotopic maps using the following defintion.

Definition 6.9
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a presentable stable
\infty
-category, let
X\in\mathcal{C}
, and let
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Say
X
is a
ghostbuster of index
\leq n
if for any composable sequence
X_{0}\to X_{1}\to\ldots\to X_{n}
of
n
consecutive phantom maps in
\mathcal{C}
and any map
X\to X_{0}
the composition
X\to X_{n}
is homotopic to
0
.

Lemma 6.10
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a presentable
\infty
-category.

1.

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the collection of ghostbusters of index
\leq n
is closed under shifts, retracts, and arbitrary direct sums.

2.

Suppose
X\to Y\to Z
is a cofiber sequence in
\mathcal{C}
. If
X
is ghostbuster of index
\leq n
and
Z
is a ghostbuster of index
\leq m
, then
Y
is a ghostbuster of index
\leq n+m
.

3.

Any compact object
X\in\mathcal{C}
is a ghostbuster of index
\leq 1
.

4.

Suppose given another presentable stable
\infty
-category
\mathcal{D}
and functors
A:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
and
B:\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{C}
such that
A
preserves colimits,
B(0)=0
, and
B\circ A\simeq\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{C}}
. Then for
X\in\mathcal{C}
, we have the implication
A(X)\in\mathcal{D}
is a ghostbuster of index
\leq n

\Rightarrow

X\in\mathcal{C}
is a ghostbuster of index
\leq n
.

Proof.

Part 1 is straightforward using the remark that the class of phantom maps is closed under shifts. For part 2, it suffices to show that if we have maps
A\to B\to C
in
\mathcal{C}
such that
[X,A]\to[X,B]
is
0
and
[Z,B]\to[Z,C]
is
0
, then
[Y,A]\to[Y,C]
is
0
. But this is clear. Part 3 follows from the definition of phantom, as
\operatorname{map}(X,-)
preserves colimits when
X
is compact. For part 4, assume
A(X)
is a ghostbuster of index
n
, let
X_{0}\to\ldots\to X_{n}
be a string of
n
composable phantom maps in
\mathcal{C}
, and let
X\to X_{0}
be arbitary. As
A
preserves colimits, it sends phantom maps to phantom maps by Lemma
6.8
. We deduce that
A(X)\to A(X_{0})\to\ldots A(X_{n})
is homotopic to
0
. Applying
B
we get the conclusion.
∎

Example 6.11
.

Let
A
be an associative ring, and let
M
be a left
A
-module which has projective dimension
n
. Then
M\in\operatorname{D}(A)
is a ghostbuster of index
\leq n+1
. Indeed, a projective module is a ghostbuster of index
1
by Lemma
6.10
part 3 , so this follows by induction Lemma
6.10
parts 1 and 2.

It turns out that ghostbusters of small index are prevalent under countability conditions. Under a countability condition on the object, we can say the following.

Example 6.12
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a presentable stable
\infty
-category.

1.

Suppose
\mathcal{C}
is compactly generated. Then any
\aleph_{1}
-compact object is a ghostbuster of index
2
. Indeed, an
\aleph_{1}
-compact object is a sequential colimit of compact objects, so by the Milnor sequence it fits into a cofiber sequence with direct sums of compact objects.

2.

More generally, suppose
\mathcal{C}
is dualizable (see
[
Efi25
]
Section 1). Then again any
\aleph_{1}
-compact object is a ghostbuster of index
2
. Indeed, consider the functor
B:\operatorname{Ind}(\mathcal{C}^{\aleph_{1}})\to\mathcal{C}
of taking colimits and its left adjoint
A
. Then
A
is fully faithful, so
B\circ A\simeq\operatorname{id}
. Also, since
B
preserves colimits,
A
sends
\aleph_{1}
-compact objects to
\aleph_{1}
-compact objects. Thus Lemma
6.10
part 4 reduces the dualizable case to the compactly generated case.

A decidedly deeper fact, due to Neeman (
[
Nee97
]
), is that under a countability assumption on
\mathcal{C}
itself
every
object is a ghostbuster of index 2:

Theorem 6.13
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{C}
is a presentable stable
\infty
-category which satisfies the following countability criterion: there is a countable stable
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}_{0}
(meaning the set of isomorphism classes of objects in
\mathcal{C}_{0}
is countable, and the set of homtopy classes of maps between any
X,Y\in\mathcal{C}_{0}
is countable) such that
\mathcal{C}
is a retract, in
\operatorname{Pr}^{L}_{st}
, of
\operatorname{Ind}(\mathcal{C}_{0})
. Then every
X\in\mathcal{C}
is a ghostbuster of index 2.

Proof.

Again by Lemma
6.10
part 4 we reduce to the case
\mathcal{C}=\operatorname{Ind}(\mathcal{C}_{0})
. But in that case, by
[
Nee97
]
Thm. 5.1 and Lemma 4.1, every object is the cofiber of a map between direct sums of compact objects, so this follows from Lemma
6.10
.
∎

Example 6.14
.

1.

Suppose
X
is a second-countable compact Hausdorff space. Then
\operatorname{Sh}(X;\operatorname{Sp})
satisfies the countability criterion of Theorem
6.13
, and hence every object in
\operatorname{Sh}(X;\operatorname{Sp})
is a ghostbuster of index 2. Indeed, we can prove this by the following variant of the argument in
[
Lur09
]
7.3.4. Let
\mathcal{K}
denote a countable collection of compact subsets of
X
which forms a basis for the topology, closed under finite intersection and finite union, and view
\mathcal{K}
as a poset. Let
X_{\mathcal{K}}
denote the spectral space whose poset of quasicompact open subsets identifies with
\mathcal{K}
, via a fixed bijection which we will denote
K\in\mathcal{K}\mapsto[K]\subset X_{\mathcal{K}}
. There is a unique continuous map
\pi:X_{\mathcal{K}}\to X
such that for
U\subset X
open, we have

\pi^{-1}(U)=\cup_{K\subset U}[K].

For the induced pullback
\pi^{\ast}
and pushforward
\pi_{\ast}
functors for sheaves of anima, we have that:

(a)

(\pi^{\ast}\mathcal{F})([K])=\varinjlim_{K\subset U}\mathcal{F}(U)
, where
U
runs over open neighborhoods of
K
. Indeed, this describes the presheaf pullback, but one checks it’s a sheaf.

(b)

\operatorname{id}\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\pi_{\ast}\pi^{\ast}
. Indeed, this follows readily from (a) and the description of
\pi_{\ast}
coming from the definition of
\pi
.

(c)

\pi_{\ast}
commutes with filtered colimits and Postnikov truncations. Indeed, by (b)
\pi^{\ast}
is conservative, and as
\pi^{\ast}
preserves colimits and Postnikov truncation on general grounds we reduce to checking that
\pi^{\ast}\pi_{\ast}
preserves filtered colimits and Postnikov truncations, which is clear from (a).

It follows that on the level of sheaves of spectra we have the same properties, and
\pi_{\ast}
commutes with arbitrary colimits. Thus
\operatorname{Sh}(X;\operatorname{Sp})
is a retract of
\operatorname{Sh}(X_{\mathcal{K}};\operatorname{Sp})
in
\operatorname{Pr}^{L}_{st}
. To finish, we note that
\operatorname{Sh}(X_{\mathcal{K}};\operatorname{Sp})
is compactly generated by the Yoneda images of the
[K]
, and that Homs in all degrees between these are countable, for example by writing the site of quasicompact opens as a countable filtered colimit of finitary sites associated to finite posets.

2.

Let
X=\varprojlim X_{n}
be a 1-truncated light profinite anima (see
4
). Suppose that
X
has finite cohomological dimension in the sense that there is a
d\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
M\in\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(X;\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Z}))
in degree
0
, we have
\Gamma(X;M)\in\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Z})_{\geq-d}
. Then
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(X;\operatorname{Sp})
satisfies the countability criterion of Theorem
6.13
, and hence every object is a ghostbuster of index
2
.

Indeed, Corollary
4.30
shows that
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(X;\operatorname{Sp})
identifies with the left-completion of the
\infty
-category of sheaves of spectra on the finite etale site of
X
. Now note that the cohomological dimension estimate passes to any finite etale
f:Y\to X
because
f_{\ast}
is t-exact (check on pullback to light profinite sets). Then from
[
CM21
]
Cor. 2.20 we deduce that for sheaves of spectra on the finite etale site of
X
, the hypercomplete full subcategory is already left-complete and hence identifies with
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(X;\operatorname{Sp})
. Moreover
[
CM21
]
Prop. 2.28 gives that this category is compactly generated by the
\mathbb{S}[h_{Y\to X}]
as
Y\to X
runs over the finite etale maps to
X
. To finish we note that homs between these are countable as one sees by reducing to cohomology using the Postnikov tower.

We will be more concerned with a variant of part 1 of this example in the setting of etale sheaves. Recall from Lemma
4.20
that if
X
is a second-countable compact Hausdorff space, then
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(\underline{X})
identifies with the Postnikov completion of
\operatorname{Sh}(X)
. It follows that
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(X;\operatorname{Sp})
identifies with with the left completion of
\operatorname{Sh}(X;\operatorname{Sp})
equipped with its natural t-structure. (See Section
7
for the notion of etale sheaves with coefficients.) Using this we show the following.

Proposition 6.15
.

Let
X
be a second-countable compact Hausdorff space which has finite cohomological dimension, in the sense that there is a
d\geq 0
such that for any sheaf of abelian groups
\mathcal{A}
on
X
, we have
H^{i}(X;\mathcal{A})=0
for
i>d
.

Then
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(X;\operatorname{Sp})
satisfies the countability hypothesis from Theorem
6.13
, so the composition of any two phantom maps is
0
.

Proof.

We recall from Lemma
4.20
that
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(X;\operatorname{Sp})=\widehat{\operatorname{Sh}}(X;\operatorname{Sp})
, the left completion of sheaves of spectra on
X
. By the commutation with Postnikov towers in Example
6.14
part 1, we deduce that
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(X;\operatorname{Sp})
is, via
\pi_{\ast}
and
\pi^{\ast}
, a retract of the left completion
\widehat{\operatorname{Sh}}(X_{\mathcal{K}};\operatorname{Sp})
of
\operatorname{Sh}(X_{\mathcal{K}};\operatorname{Sp})
. Moreover
\pi_{\ast}
still commutes with colimits on the level of left-completions: it suffices to show preservation of direct sums, but this follows again from the t-exactness. Thus it suffices to show that
\widehat{\operatorname{Sh}}(X_{\mathcal{K}};\operatorname{Sp})
is compactly generated and the full subcategory of compact objects is countable.

Since again
\pi_{\ast}
is t-exact, we deduce that
X_{\mathcal{K}}
is also of cohomological dimension
\leq d
. But moreover, the cohomological dimension hypothesis passes to any closed subset
K
of
X
, because the pushforward from a closed subset is
t
-exact. Since
K_{\mathcal{K}^{\prime}}=[K]\subset X_{\mathcal{K}}
where
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
denotes the set of elements of
\mathcal{K}
which lie in
K
, we deduce in fact that for all
K\in\mathcal{K}
, the quasicompact open subset
[K]\subset X_{\mathcal{K}}
has cohomological dimension
\leq d
. Then from
[
CM21
]
Cor 2.20 and Prop 2.28 we deduce that the left completion of
\operatorname{Sh}(X_{\mathcal{K}};\operatorname{Sp})
agrees with the hypercompletion, and the
\mathbb{S}[h_{[K]}]
form a set of compact generators. To finish we need to see that Hom’s between shifts of these compact generators are countable. Replacing
X
by
K
, we reduce to showing that
\Gamma(X_{\mathcal{K}};\varprojlim_{d}\tau_{\leq d}\mathbb{S}[h_{[K]}])
has countable homotopy groups for any
K\in\mathcal{K}
. By the cohomological dimension hypothesis this limit stabilizes in any degree, and then we can again write the site of quasicompact opens as a countable filtered colimit of finitary sites associated to finite posets to conclude.
∎

Remark 6.16
.

There do exist second-countable compact Hausdorff spaces which have infinite covering dimension, but finite cohomological dimension, see
[
Dra05
]
Section 7. (See also
[
Gro57
]
for the equality of the notion of cohomological dimension used in
[
Dra05
]
and the one we use here based on sheaf cohomology.)

We have just seen that phantom maps are often close enough to being nullhomotopic. For this to be useful we also need to give criteria for proving that a given map is phantom. Here is a helpful statement for that.

Lemma 6.17
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a stable
\infty
-category equipped with a t-structure
(\mathcal{C}_{\geq 0},\mathcal{C}_{\leq 0})
. Denote the heart by
\mathcal{C}^{\heartsuit}
, the homotopy group functors by
\pi^{\heartsuit}_{d}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}^{\heartsuit}
for
d\in\mathbb{Z}
, and the truncation functors by
\tau_{\geq d},\tau_{\leq d}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}
for
d\in\mathbb{Z}
. Suppose
F:\mathcal{C}\to\operatorname{Sp}
is an exact functor (in the sense of preserving finite limits and colimits, not in the sense of the t-structures) which satisfies the following conditions:

1.

F(\mathcal{C}^{\heartsuit})\subset\operatorname{Sp}_{[-n,0]}

for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
;

2.

\varinjlim_{d}F(\tau_{\geq-d}X)\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}F(X)\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\varprojlim_{d}F(\tau_{\leq d}X)
for all
X\in\mathcal{C}
.

Then if
X_{0}\to\ldots\to X_{n+1}
is any sequence of
n+1
composable maps in
\mathcal{C}
, each of which induces the
0
map on
\pi_{d}^{\heartsuit}
for all
d\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have that the composition

\pi_{0}F(X_{0})\to\ldots\to\pi_{0}F(X_{n})

is
0
.

Proof.

Let us first show that if
X\in\mathcal{C}
, then
\pi_{0}F(\tau_{\geq n+1}X)=0
and
\pi_{0}F(\tau_{\leq-1}X)=0
. Let
\alpha\in\pi_{0}F(\tau_{\geq n+1}X)
. By 1, the image of
\alpha
in
\pi_{0}F(\pi^{\heartsuit}_{n+1}[n+1])
is 0, hence
\alpha
lifts to some
\alpha_{1}\in\pi_{0}F(\tau_{\geq n+2}X)
, which by the same reasoning lifts to
\alpha_{2}\in\pi_{0}F(\tau_{\geq n+3})
. Continuing in this way, we find that
\alpha
lifts to a class in
\pi_{0}\varprojlim_{n}F(\tau_{\geq n}X)
. But this group is
0
by 2, hence
\alpha=0
as desired. Now let
\alpha\in\pi_{0}F(\tau_{\leq-1}X)
. By 2,
\alpha
comes form some class in
\pi_{0}F(\tau_{[-N,-1]}X)
, so it suffices to show that this group vanishes. Here we can use descending induction on
N
and 1 to handle the claim.

Now suppose
X\to Y
is a map in
\mathcal{C}
which induces
0
on
\pi^{\heartsuit}_{0}
. By the above vanishing of
\pi_{0}F(\tau_{\leq-1}X)
, any
\alpha\in\pi_{0}F(X)
lifts to
\alpha^{\prime}\in\pi_{0}F(\tau_{\geq 0}X)
. Since
\tau_{\geq 0}X\to\tau_{\geq 0}Y\to\pi^{\heartsuit}_{0}Y
is
0
, the image of
\alpha^{\prime}
in
\pi_{0}F(\tau_{\geq 0}Y)
lifts to
\pi_{0}F(\tau_{\geq 1}Y)
. If
Y\to Z
is a further map which is
0
on
\pi^{\heartsuit}_{1}
, applying the same argument to this lift in
\pi_{0}F(\tau_{\geq 1}Y)
we get that the image of
\alpha^{\prime}
in
\pi_{0}F(\tau_{\geq 0}Z)
lifts to
\pi_{0}F(\tau_{\geq 2}Z)
. Continuing in this manner, we deduce that if
X\to Y
is a composition of
d+1
many maps each of which is
0
on
\pi^{\heartsuit}_{\ast}
, then the image of
\alpha^{\prime}
in
\pi_{0}F(\tau_{\geq 0}Y)
lifts to
\pi_{0}F(\tau_{\geq d+1}Y)
. But this group is
0
by the previous paragraph, whence the conclusion.
∎

To proceed further we recall a class of presentable stable
\infty
-categories
\mathcal{C}
which are canonically self-dual, permitting an efficient classification of the colimit-preserving functors
\mathcal{C}\to\operatorname{Sp}
(such as is needed to understand phantom maps). The definition is due to
[
GR19
]
, 1.9.

Definition 6.18
.

Let
\mathcal{C}\in\operatorname{CAlg}(\operatorname{Pr}_{st}^{L})
. Say that
\mathcal{C}
is
rigid
if the following conditions are satisfied:

1.

The unit object
1\in\mathcal{C}
is compact.

2.

The multiplication map
m:\mathcal{C}\otimes\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}
in
\operatorname{CAlg}(\operatorname{Pr}^{L}_{st})
has a right adjoint which preserves colimits and satisfies the projection formula.

If
\mathcal{C}
is rigid, then by
[
GR19
]
9.2,
\mathcal{C}
is canonically self-dual in
\operatorname{Pr}_{st}^{L}
via the coevaluation pairing given by the composition

\operatorname{Sp}\overset{1}{\rightarrow}\mathcal{C}\overset{m^{R}}{\rightarrow}\mathcal{C}\otimes\mathcal{C}

where the first map is the unit and the second map is the right adjoint
m^{R}
to the functor
m:\mathcal{C}\otimes\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}
. The evaluation pairing can be described as follows: if we define
\Gamma:\mathcal{C}\to\operatorname{Sp}
to be the functor
\Gamma=\operatorname{map}(1,-)
or in other words the right adjoint to the functor
\operatorname{Sp}\to\mathcal{C}
classifying the unit, then the evaluation is given by the composition

\mathcal{C}\otimes\mathcal{C}\overset{m}{\rightarrow}\mathcal{C}\overset{\Gamma}{\rightarrow}\operatorname{Sp}.

In particular, and this is the key point for us, every colimit-preserving functor
F:\mathcal{C}\to\operatorname{Sp}
is of the form

F(-)\simeq\Gamma(X\otimes-)

for some (unique)
X\in\mathcal{C}
.

Example 6.19
.

1.

Suppose
\mathcal{C}\in\operatorname{CAlg}(\operatorname{Pr}^{L}_{st})
is compactly generated. Then
\mathcal{C}
is rigid if and only if for
X\in\mathcal{C}
, we have that
X
is compact
\Leftrightarrow

X
is dualizable. (Or equivalently if
1\in\mathcal{C}
is compact, and
\mathcal{C}
is generated by objects which are both compact and dualizable.) See
[
GR19
]
Lemma 9.1.5.

2.

For a compact Hausdorff space
X
, the
\infty
-category
\operatorname{Sh}(X;\operatorname{Sp})
is rigid. This follows formally from the proper base-change theorem of
[
Lur09
]
7.3.1.18 and the Kunneth property
\operatorname{Sh}(X;\operatorname{Sp})\otimes\operatorname{Sh}(X;\operatorname{Sp})\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\operatorname{Sh}(X\times X;\operatorname{Sp})
of
[
Lur09
]
7.3.1.11.

3.

Suppose
X=\varprojlim X_{n}
is a 1-truncated light profinite anima such that
X
has finite cohomological dimension. Then the
\infty
-category
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(X;\operatorname{Sp})
is rigid.

Indeed, recall from Example
6.14
that
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(X;\operatorname{Sp})
is compactly generated by the
f_{\natural}\mathbb{S}
where
f_{\natural}
is the left adjoint to
f^{\ast}
for
f:Y\to X
finite etale. By example 1, to finish the proof it suffices to show that these are dualizable. In fact this holds generally for an arbitrary finite etale map. Indeed by descent we can reduce to the case where the target is light profinite set, and then it is obvious because such finite etale maps are split.

4.

If
R
is any
E_{\infty}
-ring, then
D(R)
is rigid. Indeed, the unit is compact and dualizable and generates, so this follows from 1.

5.

If
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}\in\operatorname{CAlg}(\operatorname{Pr}^{L}_{st})
are both rigid, then so is
\mathcal{C}\otimes\mathcal{D}
. Indeed the projection formula is stable under tensor product.

We will want to use the following etale sheaf variant of part 2 of the above example.

Proposition 6.20
.

Suppose
X
is a second-countable compact Hausdorff space of finite cohomological dimension. Then
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(\underline{X};\operatorname{Sp})
is rigid.

Proof.

First we note that for a map
f:X\to Y
of compact Hausdorff spaces such that the fibers have uniformly bounded cohomological dimension
\leq d
, the proper base change theorem holds for the left completion
\widehat{Sh}(-;\operatorname{Sp})
. Indeed, for a truncated sheaf of spectra
\mathcal{F}
on
X
, the pushforward
f_{\ast}\mathcal{F}
is also truncated, and by proper base-change and the cohomological dimension hypothesis we deduce that
\tau_{\leq n}f_{\ast}\mathcal{F}\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\tau_{\leq n}f_{\ast}\tau_{\leq N}\mathcal{F}
for
N>n+d
. Thus for an object
(\mathcal{F}_{n})_{n}
in the left-completion
\widehat{\operatorname{Sh}}(X;\operatorname{Sp})
, the left-complete pushforward
f_{\ast}((\mathcal{F}_{n})_{n})
identifies with the object
(\tau_{\leq n}f_{\ast}\mathcal{F}_{n+d+1})_{n}
, and so the conclusion follows again from (noncomplete) proper base-change.

Thus rigidity of
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(\underline{X})=\widehat{\operatorname{Sh}}(X;\operatorname{Sp})
will follow provided we can show that we can show the Kunneth property, namely that if
X
and
Y
are compact Hausdorff spaces of finite cohomological dimension, then

\widehat{\operatorname{Sh}}(X;\operatorname{Sp})\otimes\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}}(Y;\operatorname{Sp})\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\widehat{\operatorname{Sp}}(X\times Y;\operatorname{Sp}).

For this, as in the proof of Proposition
6.15
it suffices to prove the following spectral space variant: if
X,Y
are spectral spaces with a basis of quasi-compact open subsets of uniformly bounded cohomological dimension, then
