\widehat{\operatorname{Sh}}(X;\operatorname{Sp})\otimes\widehat{\operatorname{Sh}}(Y;\operatorname{Sp})
identifies with
\widehat{\operatorname{Sh}}(X\times Y;\operatorname{Sp})
. Note that the non-complete variant holds, for example by writing
X
and
Y
as filtered inverse limits of finite posets and using
[
CM21
]
Lemma 3.3. Looking at
\mathbb{Z}
-coefficients, it follows in particular that the uniform boundedness of cohomological dimension passes to
X\times Y
. Now we compare compact generators. The objects
\mathbb{S}[h_{U}]
,
\mathbb{S}[h_{V}]
, and
\mathbb{S}[h_{U\times V}]
give compact generators of respectively
\widehat{\operatorname{Sh}}(X;\operatorname{Sp})
,
\widehat{\operatorname{Sh}}(Y;\operatorname{Sp})
, and
\widehat{\operatorname{Sh}}(X\times Y;\operatorname{Sp})
by
[
CM21
]
Prop 2.28, where
U,V
run over quasicompact basic opens in
X,Y
respectively. Note that the mapping spectra between these compact generators are all bounded below, by Postnikov completeness and the finite cohomological dimension. Thus we can check our Kunneth property after base-change to
D(\mathbb{Z})
. But then there is no distinction between hypercomplete = Postnikov-complete sheaves and arbitrary sheaves because the generators are truncated and hence automatically hypercomplete, see
[
CM21
]
Prop. 2.28.
∎

Putting things together, we get the following criterion for descendability.

Theorem 6.21
.

Let
X
be a light condensed anima. Suppose that the following hypotheses are satisfied:

1.

\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(X;\operatorname{Sp})
is rigid and satisfies the countability hypothesis from Theorem
6.13
.

2.

X
has finite cohomological dimension: there is a
d\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
M\in\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(X;\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Z}))
concentrated in degree
0
, we have
\Gamma(M)\in\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Z})_{\geq-d}
.

Then for any
A\in\operatorname{CAlg}(\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(X;\operatorname{Sp}))
, the following conditions are equivalent:

1.

A
is descendable;

2.

there is an
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all maps
x:\ast\to X
, the pullback
x^{\ast}A\in\operatorname{CAlg}(\operatorname{Sp})
is descendable of index
\leq N
.

Proof.

If condition 1 holds, then
A
is descendable of index
\leq N
for some
N
. This condition is preserved by symmetric monoidal functors, so condition 2 holds. For the converse, we use the usual t-structure on
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(X;\operatorname{Sp})
coming from sheafified Postnikov towers. The heart is the category of etale sheaves of abelian groups. Consider the following classes of maps:

1.

The
A
-null maps.

2.

The maps inducing
0
on homotopy groups (with respect to the t-structure).

3.

The maps
f
such that for all
X\in\mathcal{C}
, the map
f\otimes X
is
0
on homotopy groups.

4.

The phantom maps.

5.

The nullhomotopic maps.

We first claim that any
N
-fold composition of
A
-null maps induces
0
on homotopy groups. As pullbacks are t-exact and detect being
0
on abelian group sheaves, this follows from hypothesis 2. Since the class of
A
-null maps is closed under
-\otimes X
, we even see that any
N
-fold composition of
A
-null maps satisfies the stronger condition 3 just above. Next we claim that any
d+1
-fold composition of maps satisfying 3 is phantom. By
6
, all colimit-preserving functors to spectra are of the form
\Gamma(X\otimes-)
for some
X\in\mathcal{C}
, so for this it suffices to show that a
d+1
-fold composition of maps which induce
0
on homotopy groups gets sent to a null map by
\Gamma
. For that we can invoke Lemma
6.17
. Since
\Gamma
preserves colimits (because the unit is compact) and the t-structure is right complete, we have
\Gamma(X)=\varinjlim_{d}\Gamma(\tau_{\geq-d}X)
. On the other hand,
\Gamma
also preserves limits (because it is a right adjoint) and the t-structure is left-complete, so we have
\Gamma(X)=\varprojlim_{d}\Gamma(\tau_{\leq d}X)
. Finally, the last hypothesis in Lemma
6.17
is satisfied by our cohomological dimension assumption. Thus all the hypotheses of Lemma
6.17
are satisfied and we get the desired conclusion. Finally, we claim that any 2-fold composition of phantom maps is null. This follows from Theorem
6.13
and the countability hypothesis.

In total, we have seen that any
2N(d+1)
-fold composition of
A
-null maps is null, hence
A
is descendable.
∎

Remark 6.22
.

Let
p
be a prime. There is also a
p
-complete analog of Theorem
6.21
, but since
\operatorname{Sp}_{\widehat{p}}
is not itself rigid we have to state it a bit differently. We can use the concept of a
semi-rigid
category from
[
Ari+22
]
App. C (also called
locally rigid
in
[
Efi25
]
and
[
Ram24
]
). This is a
\mathcal{C}\in\operatorname{CAlg}(\operatorname{Pr}^{L}_{st})
which satisfies condition 2 in Definition
6.18
and is moreover dualizable as an object in
\operatorname{Pr}^{L}_{st}
. Any
\mathcal{C}\in\operatorname{CAlg}(\operatorname{Pr}^{L}_{st})
which is compactly generated by dualizable objects is semi-rigid, and if
\mathcal{C}
is semi-rigid and
p
-complete and
1/p
is compact, then
\mathcal{C}
is self-dual via
\Gamma(-)[p^{\infty}]
, as follows from
[
Ram24
]
Cor. 4.7.2. (This applies in particular to
\mathcal{C}=\operatorname{Sp}_{\widehat{p}}
.)

Then if we assume
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(X;\operatorname{Sp}_{\widehat{p}})
is semi-rigid and
X
has finite (mod
p
) cohomological dimension, we see from a Postnikov tower argument that
1/p
is compact, and then we can run a similar argument as in the above proof to deduce once more that (uniform) descendability is detected on pullback to points.

Remark 6.23
.

The following criterion can be useful for establishing finite cohomological dimension. Suppose
X
is a light condensed anima which admits a hypercover by light profinite sets (for example,
X
could be a second-countable compact Hausdorff space or a light profinite anima). Suppose further that we have a map
f:X\to S
to a light profinite set, and for every
s:\ast\to S
the fiber
X_{s}
has finite cohomological dimension
\leq d
, with
d
being independent of
s
. Then
X
has cohomological dimension
\leq d
.

Indeed, for a truncated
\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Z})
-valued sheaf
\mathcal{F}
we can deduce base-change for
f_{\ast}\mathcal{F}
by using the hypercover and the fact that base-change for lower-
\ast
holds for maps of light profinite sets. Then we can make use of the fact that pullbacks to points are t-exact (as always) and jointly detect isomorphisms (Remark
4.18
) to conclude.

The same remark applies to mod
p
cohomological dimension.

7
The six functor formalism for etale sheaves

In this section we produce a six functor formalism for etale sheaves on light condensed anima, following
[
HM24
]
. Recall that in the set-up of that article, a six functor formalism consists of the following data:

1.

An
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
which has all finite products;

2.

A collection
E
of morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
which is
geometric
, i.e. closed under composition, closed under base-change, contains all isomorphisms, and is closed under passing to diagonals.

3.

A lax symmetric monoidal functor
M:\operatorname{Span}_{E}(\mathcal{C})\to\operatorname{Pr}^{L}
.

Here
\operatorname{Span}_{E}(\mathcal{C})
is a certain natural
\infty
-category whose objects are the objects in
\mathcal{C}
and where morphisms from
X
to
Y
are spans

X\leftarrow T\rightarrow Y

with
T\rightarrow Y
in
E
, and where composition is induced by pullbacks of spans. This
\infty
-category
\operatorname{Span}_{E}(\mathcal{C})
carries a symmetric monoidal structure induced by the product in
\mathcal{C}
, and we view
\operatorname{Pr}^{L}
as symmetric monoidal via the Lurie tensor product.

Thus a six functor formalism assigns a presentable
\infty
-category
M(X)
to every
X\in\mathcal{C}
and a colimit-preserving functor
f^{\ast}:M(Y)\to M(X)
to every
f:X\to Y
in
\mathcal{C}
and a colimit-preserving functor
g_{!}:M(X)\to M(Y)
to every map
g:X\to Y
in
E
. The functoriality of
M
implies that
(-)^{\ast}
and
(-)_{!}
are separately functorial, and (crucially) that the formation of
g_{!}
commutes with
f^{\ast}
in a base-change square. The symmetric monoidality gives the exterior product operation:

M(X)\otimes M(Y)\to M(X\times Y),

such that composing with pullback along the diagonal promotes each
M(X)
to a presentably symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category. We refer to
[
HM24
]
for more details on all of this.

Generally, one produces six functor formalisms in a multi-step procedure. First one starts with the situation in which
g_{!}
can be taken to be
g_{\ast}
, and hence the whole data is in principle determined just by the underlying functor
M^{\ast}:\mathcal{C}^{op}\to\operatorname{CAlg}(\operatorname{Pr}^{L})
by some fancy procedure of passing to adjoints. Then, in the subsequent steps, one applies various formal procedures allowing to enlarge the class of maps
E
allowed in the formalism. In
[
HM24
]
a convenient result is proved which axiomatizes this procedure in a way which is sufficient for our applications.

We are primarily interested in etale sheaves of
p
-complete spectra , but for extra flexibility we consider a more general set-up. Following
[
Lur18
]
21.1.2, we single out a class of reasonable “coefficient categories” which includes all examples of interest.

Definition 7.1
.

Suppose
\mathcal{C}
is a compactly assembled presentable
\infty
-category. For
X\in\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}
, define

\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(X;\mathcal{C})=\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(X)\otimes\mathcal{C}

and

\operatorname{Sh}_{v}(X;\mathcal{C})=\operatorname{Sh}_{v}(X)\otimes\mathcal{C}.

Remark 7.2
.

By
[
Lur18
]
1.3.1.6 this is consistent notation: if
\mathcal{X}
is an
\infty
-topos which identifies with the
\infty
-topos of hypercomplete sheaves of anima on some site, then
\mathcal{X}\otimes\mathcal{C}
identifies with the
\infty
-category of hypercomplete
\mathcal{C}
-valued sheaves on that same site. But we use the tensor product perspective to keep better track of the functoriality.

The reason we restrict to compactly assembled
\mathcal{C}
is because of the following favorable properties.

Lemma 7.3
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a compactly assembled presentable
\infty
-category.

1.

The functor

\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(X;\mathcal{C})\to\operatorname{Sh}_{v}(X;\mathcal{C}),

gotten by tensoring the inclusion
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(X;\mathcal{C})\to\operatorname{Sh}_{v}(X;\mathcal{C})
with
\mathcal{C}
, is fully faithful.

2.

The functors
X\mapsto\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(X;\mathcal{C})
and
X\mapsto\operatorname{Sh}_{v}(X;\mathcal{C})
from
\operatorname{CondAn}^{op}
to
\operatorname{Pr}^{L}
preserve arbitrary limits.

3.

Let
X\in\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}
. A map
\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{G}
in
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(X;\mathcal{C})
is an isomorphism if and only if
x^{\ast}\mathcal{F}\to x^{\ast}\mathcal{G}
is an isomorphism for all
x:\ast\to X
.

Proof.

Because every compactly assembled presentable
\infty
-category is a retract in
\operatorname{Pr}^{L}
of a compactly generated
\infty
-category (
[
Lur18
]
21.1.2), we can reduce to the compactly generated case, so assume
\mathcal{C}
compactly generated. Note that if
\mathcal{X}
is a presentable
\infty
-category, then

\mathcal{X}\otimes\mathcal{C}\simeq\operatorname{Fun}^{rex}((\mathcal{C}^{\aleph_{0}})^{op},\mathcal{X}),

where
\operatorname{Fun}^{rex}
means the
\infty
-category of functors which preserve finite limits. Indeed we have
\mathcal{C}\simeq\operatorname{Fun}^{rex}((\mathcal{C}^{\aleph_{0}})^{op},\operatorname{An})
by sending
c\in C
to the functor
c_{0}\mapsto\operatorname{Map}(c_{0},c)
. Using that
\mathcal{X}
is tensored over
\operatorname{An}
, this produces an obvious comparison functor
\mathcal{X}\otimes\mathcal{C}\to\operatorname{Fun}^{rex}((\mathcal{C}^{\aleph_{0}})^{op},\mathcal{X})
. It is an isomorphism by
[
Lur17
]
Prop. 4.8.1.17. But note that from this definition of the comparison functor, we see that it is functorial in maps between presentable
\infty
-categories
\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
which preserve colimits and finite limits. Every pullback map of
\infty
-topoi is of this form by definition, and thus we reduce all three claims 1,2,3 to the case
\mathcal{C}=\operatorname{An}
. Then claims 1 and 2 follow from the definition, while 3 is given by Remark
4.18
.
∎

Remark 7.4
.

If
\mathcal{C}
is compactly generated, then the essential image of the fully faithful functor in 1 consists of those
\mathcal{C}
-valued
v
-sheaves
\mathcal{F}
such that for all compact objects
c\in\mathcal{C}
, the
v
-sheaf of anima
\operatorname{Map}(c,\mathcal{F})
lies in
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(X)
. For example, a
v
-sheaf of
p
-complete spectra
\mathcal{F}
is etale if and only if
\mathcal{F}/p
is etale as a
v
-sheaf of spectra, which is if and only if
\Omega^{\infty}(\mathcal{F}/p[n])
is etale as a
v
-sheaf of anima for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, or again (see Lemma
4.17
) if and only if each homotopy group sheaf of
\mathcal{F}/p
is etale as an abelian group valued sheaf. This follows because
\mathbb{S}/p
is a compact generator of
\operatorname{Sp}_{\widehat{p}}
.

In the abstract context, our “base category” for our six functor formalism will therefore be some
\mathcal{R}\in\operatorname{CAlg}(\operatorname{Pr}^{L})
which is compactly assembled (as an underlying
\infty
-category).

Following the procedure outlined above, a sufficient supply of proper maps to start out with is guaranteed by the following simple result.

Lemma 7.5
.

Let
f:T\to S
be a map of light profinite sets. Then the right adjoint
f_{\ast}:\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(T;\operatorname{Sp})\to\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(S;\operatorname{Sp})

to the pullback of etale sheaves of spectra preserves all colimits, satisfies the projection formula, and commutes with base-change (along any map
S^{\prime}\to S
in
\operatorname{ProfSet}^{light}
).

Proof.

By the comparison of etale sheaves on light profinite sets with sheaves on the underlying compact Hausdorff space (Lemma
4.20
), this follows from Lurie’s proper base change theorem (
[
Lur09
]
7.3).
∎

Remark 7.6
.

Lurie’s proper base change theorem is of course quite elementary in the context of profinite sets. See
[
HM24
]
3.5 for another perspective, by identifying sheaves of spectra on a profinite set
S
with modules over the ring
\Gamma(S;\mathbb{S})
. Yet another perspective, that of
[
Sch22
]
, is the following: it is tautological from slice topos formalism that the pushforward of
v
-sheaves commutes with pullbacks and satisfies the projection formula; thus to see that the etale pushforward does as well, it suffices to show that the pushforward of
v
-sheaves sends etale sheaves to etale sheaves. This is a simple consequence of the criterion of Theorem
4.15
.

Remark 7.7
.

For any
\mathcal{R}\in\operatorname{CAlg}(\operatorname{Pr}_{st}^{L})
, if we tensor this lemma with
\mathcal{R}
we deduce the analogous lemma for
\mathcal{R}
-valued etale sheaves.

From this result and
[
HM24
]
Prop. 3.3.3, we get a (completely explicit) six functor formalism on
\operatorname{ProfSet}^{light}
with
E
the class of all maps such that:

1.

The underlying functor
M^{\ast}:(\operatorname{ProfSet}^{light})^{op}\to\operatorname{CAlg}(\operatorname{Pr}^{L})
is
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(-;\mathcal{R})
(for any
\mathcal{R}\in\operatorname{CAlg}(\operatorname{Pr}^{L}_{st})
compactly assembled).

2.

For all maps
f:X\to Y
in
\operatorname{ProfSet}^{light}
, the functor
f_{!}
identifies with
f_{\ast}
, and the base-change isomorphisms for
f_{!}
(coming from the six functor formalism) and for
f_{\ast}
(coming from adjunction) agree.

There is a more precise version of 2: in this six functor formalism every map is
proper
in the sense of
[
HM24
]
Def. 4.6.1 (we will also review this notion below). It follows by
[
DK24
]
that this is the unique six functor formalism on
\operatorname{ProfSet}^{light}
such that 1 holds and every map is proper.

Now we apply
[
HM24
]
Thm. 3.4.11 to extend this six functor formalism to all of
\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}
. In the statement we make use the notion of a
!
-cover
associated to a given six functor formalism

M:\operatorname{Span}_{E}(\mathcal{C})\to\operatorname{Pr}^{L}.

The definition is as follows: a collection of maps
F=\{f_{i}:X_{i}\to X\}_{i\in I}
in
\mathcal{C}
with common target is a
!
-cover
(called a universal
!
-cover in
[
HM24
]
Def. 3.4.6) if:

1.

Each
f_{i}
lies in
E
;

2.

For any map
X^{\prime}\to X
in
\mathcal{C}
, if
F^{\prime}:=\{f^{\prime}_{i}:X^{\prime}_{i}\to X^{\prime}\}_{i\in I}
denotes the pullback, then the co-presheaf
M_{!}:E\to\operatorname{Pr}^{L}
satisfies the cosheaf condition for the sieve on
E_{/X^{\prime}}
generated by
F^{\prime}
. Here
E
is the wide subcategory of
\mathcal{C}
corresponding to
E
, and
M_{!}
is the functor given by the
!
-pushforwards from the six functor formalism.

Theorem 7.8
.

Let
\mathcal{R}\in\operatorname{CAlg}(\operatorname{Pr}_{st}^{L})
such that
\mathcal{R}
is compactly assembled. Then there exists a minimal geometric class of maps
E
of light condensed anima satisfying the following conditions:

1.

There is a unique six functor formalism
M:\operatorname{Span}_{E}(\operatorname{CondAn}^{light})\to\operatorname{Pr}^{L}
equipped with an identification of the underlying functor
M^{\ast}:(\operatorname{CondAn}^{light})^{op}\to\operatorname{CAlg}(\operatorname{Pr}^{L})
with
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(-;\mathcal{R})
such that
E
contains all maps between light profinite sets, and all maps between light profinite sets are
proper
.

2.

Suppose that
f:X\to Y
is a map of light condensed anima such for all light profinite sets
S
and all maps
S\to Y
, the pullback
X\times_{Y}S\to S
lies in
E
. Then
f
lies in
E
.

3.

Suppose that
f:X\to Y
is a map of light condensed anima which,
!
-locally on source or target (with respect to the six functor formalism
M
from 1), lies in
E
. Then
f
lies in
E
.

4.

If
f:X\to Y
lies in
E
and
Y\in\operatorname{ProfSet}^{light}
, then
X
admits a
!
-cover by light profinite sets.

By definition, we will say that a map
f
of light condensed anima is
\mathcal{R}
-
!
-able
(or
\mathcal{R}
-fine
, in the language of
[
HM24
]
) if it lies in this class
E
.

Proof.

By Lemma
7.3
,
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(-;\mathcal{R}):(\operatorname{CondAn}^{light})^{op}\to\operatorname{CAlg}(\operatorname{Pr}^{L})
is right Kan extended from
\operatorname{ProfSet}^{light}
. Thus condition 1 is equivalent to the following condition: there is a unique six functor formalism
M:\operatorname{Span}_{E}(\operatorname{CondAn}^{light})\to\operatorname{Pr}^{L}
such that
E
contains all maps of light profinite sets,
M^{\ast}
preserves limits, and
M
is equipped with an isomorphism to the basic six functor formalism discussed above on restriction to light profinite sets. Then the result follows from a direct application of
[
HM24
]
Thm. 3.4.11.
∎

Remark 7.9
.

From the proof of Thm. 3.4.11 of
[
HM24
]
, one sees that the class of maps
E
is produced by starting with the class
E_{0}
of maps of light condensed anima whose every pullback to a light profinite set is light profinite, and then iteratively extending
E_{0}
by two simple extension procedures (and, when necessary, taking unions over a chain of previously constructed classes of maps). For each class of maps produced one has the uniqueness claim as in 1, allowing to talk about
!
-covers, etc. The extension procedures are as follows:

1.

Given a class of maps
E^{\prime}
, enlarge to
E^{\prime}_{!}
, the class of maps which are
!
-locally on the source in
E^{\prime}
.

2.

Given a class of maps
E^{\prime}
, enlarge it to
E^{\prime}_{\ast}
, the class of maps such that each pullback to a light profinite set lies in
E^{\prime}
.

Remark 7.10
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{R}\to\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
is a map in
\operatorname{CAlg}(\operatorname{Pr}^{L}_{st})
, where both
\mathcal{R}
and
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
are compactly assembled. Let
E
denote the collection of
\mathcal{R}
-
!
-able maps, and similarly
E^{\prime}
the collection of
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
-
!
-able maps. Then
E\subset E^{\prime}
. Moreover, we have the following compatibility property. Suppose we take the six functor formalism for
\mathcal{R}
-valued etale sheaves, viewed as a lax symmetric mononidal functor

\operatorname{Span}_{E}(\operatorname{CondAn}^{light})\to\operatorname{Mod}_{\mathcal{R}}(\operatorname{Pr}^{L})

(see
[
HM24
]
Lemma 3.2.5), and then apply
-\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
. Then the resulting six functor formalism agrees with the one for
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
-valued etale sheaves, restricted from
(\operatorname{CondAn}^{light},E^{\prime})
to
(\operatorname{CondAn}^{light},E)
.

Following the previous remark, and as in the proof of
[
HM24
]
Prop. 3.5.22, to justify this we need to see that if we have added a new
\mathcal{R}
-
!
-able map by the extension procedures 1 or 2, then it is also in the collection of maps which are
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
-
!
-able in the corresponding step for
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
. For procedure 2 this is obvious. For procedure 1, we need to show that a
!
-cover for
\mathcal{R}
is also a
!
-cover for
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
. However,
M_{!}:E\to\operatorname{Pr}^{L}
promotes naturally to
M_{!}:E\to\operatorname{Mod}_{\mathcal{R}}(\operatorname{Pr}^{L})
(
[
HM24
]
Prop. 3.5.22), and then we can apply
-\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
to conclude (recalling that
\operatorname{Mod}_{\mathcal{R}}(\operatorname{Pr}^{L})\to\operatorname{Pr}^{L}
preserves and detects colimits).

There are some important classes of morphisms one can define associated to any six functor formalism. We spell some of them out now in the context of the six functor formalism from Theorem
7.8
. Recall first that a map in an
\infty
-category is called
truncated
if some iterated diagonal of it is an isomorphism. Some of these notions will only be defined for truncated maps.

Remark 7.11
.

In fact,
!
-able morphisms in the six functor formalism from Theorem
7.8
have a strong tendency to be truncated (or at least, unions of truncated objects). Indeed, in practice the
!
-covers one uses to produce new examples of
!
-able maps (via property 3 in Theorem
7.8
) are also covers in the light condensed Grothendieck topology, hence new examples of
!
-covers one produces are at most one less degree of truncated than old ones. This is not a completely satisfactory situation: for example one would like to have that an arbitrary etale map, not necessarily truncated, between light condensed anima is
\operatorname{Sp}
-
!
-able, but this is not true for the six functor formalism given by Theorem
7.8
. Some new principle of construction (or extension) of six functor formalisms would be necessary to “reach” such maps.

Definition 7.12
.

Let
\mathcal{R}\in\operatorname{CAlg}(\operatorname{Pr}^{L}_{st})
be compactly assembled.

1.

(
[
HM24
]
Def. 4.6.1) A truncated map
f:X\to Y
of light condensed anima is called
\mathcal{R}
-proper
if it is
\mathcal{R}
-
!
-able, and either:

(a)

f
is an isomorphism, in which case we define an isomorphism
f_{!}\simeq f_{\ast}
using the pullback of
f
along itself and base-change in the pullback square
X\overset{(\operatorname{id},\operatorname{id})}{\longrightarrow}X\times_{Y}X
;

(b)

\Delta_{f}:X\to X\times_{Y}X
is proper and the natural map
f_{!}\to f_{\ast}
, coming from the isomorphism
(\Delta_{f})_{\ast}\simeq(\Delta_{f})_{!}
and base-change in the pullback
X\times_{Y}X
, is an isomorphism.

Thus by definition a proper map comes with a canonical isomorphism
f_{!}\simeq f_{\ast}
.

2.

(
[
HM24
]
Def. 4.6.1) A truncated map
f:X\to Y
of light condensed anima is called
\mathcal{R}
-etale
if it is
\mathcal{R}
-
!
-able, and either:

(a)

f
is an isomorphism, in which case we define an isomorphism
f^{\ast}\simeq f^{!}
using the pullback of
f
along itself and
X\overset{(\operatorname{id},\operatorname{id})}{\longrightarrow}X\times_{Y}X
;

(b)

\Delta_{f}
is etale and the natural map
f^{\ast}\to f^{!}
, coming from the isomorphism
(\Delta_{f})^{!}\simeq(\Delta_{f})^{\ast}
and the pullback of
f
along itself, is an isomorphism.

Thus by definition an etale map comes with a canonical isomorphism
f^{\ast}\simeq f^{!}
. Here of course
f^{!}
denotes the right adjoint to
f_{!}
.

3.

(
[
HM24
]
Def. 4.5.1) A map
f:X\to Y
of light condensed anima is called
\mathcal{R}
-smooth
if it is
\mathcal{R}
-
!
-able, and the following two conditions are satisfied:

(a)

The formation of
f^{!}
commutes with arbitrary base-change (via the natural comparison map coming from base-change for
f_{!}
), and likewise for any base change of
f
.

(b)

The natural transformation
f^{\ast}(-)\otimes f^{!}(1)\rightarrow f^{!}(-)
(adjoint to the projection formula for
f_{!}
) is an isomorphism. (In particular,
f^{!}
preserves colimits.)

(c)

If
1\in\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(Y;\mathcal{R})
denotes the unit object, then
f^{!}1\in\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(X;\mathcal{R})
is invertible.

Remark 7.13
.

Every
f:X\to Y
which is truncated and
\mathcal{R}
-etale is
\mathcal{R}
-smooth. In this case
f^{!}1\simeq 1
.

Remark 7.14
.

We have the following closure properties:

1.

The classes of
\mathcal{R}
-etale and
\mathcal{R}
-proper maps are geometric,
[
HM24
]
Lemma 4.6.3.

2.

The class of
\mathcal{R}
-smooth maps is not geometric (it is not closed under passage to diagonals), but it is closed under composition and pullback (clear from the definition).

Remark 7.15
.

There are also many situations in which one can test properness or smoothness locally on the source or target in some sense or another. Let
f:X\to Y
be a map in
\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}
. We have:

1.

Suppose
f
is truncated. If for all
S\in\operatorname{ProfSet}^{light}
with a map
S\to Y
the map
X\times_{Y}S\to S
is
\mathcal{R}
-proper (resp.
\mathcal{R}
-etale), then
f
is
\mathcal{R}
-proper (resp.
\mathcal{R}
-etale); see
[
HM24
]
Lemma 4.6.3.

2.

If for all
S\in\operatorname{ProfSet}^{light}
with a map
S\to Y
the map
X\times_{Y}S\to S
is
\mathcal{R}
-smooth, then
f
is
\mathcal{R}
-smooth; see
[
HM24
]
Lemma 4.5.7.

3.

Let
g:X^{\prime}\to X
be a truncated
\mathcal{R}
-proper map. Suppose
g_{\ast}1\in\operatorname{CAlg}(\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(X;\mathcal{R}))
is descendable (See section
6
). If
f\circ g
is
\mathcal{R}
-
!
-able, then
f
is
\mathcal{R}
-
!
-able; and if
f
is truncated and
f\circ g
is truncated and proper, then
f
is proper; see
[
HM24
]
Lemma 4.7.4 and Cor. 4.7.5

4.

Suppose
\{g_{i}:X_{i}\to X\}_{i\in I}
is a collection of
\mathcal{R}
-smooth maps to
X
such that the pullback functors
\{g_{i}^{\ast}\}_{i\in I}
are jointly conservative. If each
f\circ g_{i}
is
\mathcal{R}
-
!
-able (resp.
\mathcal{R}
-smooth), then
f
is
\mathcal{R}
-
!
-able (resp.
\mathcal{R}
-smooth); see
[
HM24
]
Lemmas 4.7.1 and 4.5.8.

5.

Suppose
f
is truncated. Let
\{g_{i}:X_{i}\to X\}_{i\in I}
be a collection of truncated
\mathcal{R}
-etale maps to
X
such that the pullacks
\{g_{i}^{\ast}\}_{i\in I}
are jointly conservative and each
f\circ g_{i}
is
\mathcal{R}
-etale. Then
f
is
\mathcal{R}
-etale; see
[
HM24
]
Lemma 4.6.3.

Of course, implicit here is the claim that the
g
in 2 and the
\{g_{i}\}
in 3 give examples of
!
-covers.

Remark 7.16
.

Suppose
\mathcal{R}\to\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
is a map in
\operatorname{CAlg}(\operatorname{Pr}^{L})
such that both
\mathcal{R}
and
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
are compactly assembled. If a map
f
is truncated and
\mathcal{R}
-proper, then it is also
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
-proper. Similarly if
f
is truncated and
\mathcal{R}
-etale, then it is
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
-etale, and if
f
is
\mathcal{R}
-smooth, then it is
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
-smooth. Using the compatibility from Remark
7.10
, this is clear by applying
-\otimes_{\mathcal{R}}\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
.

Remark 7.17
.

By construction, any
f:X\to Y
which is truncated and
\mathcal{R}
-proper comes with a natural isomorphism
f_{!}\simeq f_{\ast}
. One can check inductively that these natural isomorphisms commute with the base-change comparison maps and the projection formula comparison maps, where the ones for
f_{!}
come from the six functor formalism and the ones for
f_{\ast}
come from adjunction. In particular, for
\mathcal{R}
-proper maps the formation of
f_{\ast}
commutes with base-change and satisfies a projection formula. Thus we could build another six functor formalism with
E
the class of all
\mathcal{R}
-proper maps based on this via the explicit procedure of
[
HM24
]
Prop. 3.3.3. But it follows from the uniqueness principle
[
DK24
]
that this six functor formalism agrees with the one from Theorem
7.8
. Thus the isomorphism
f_{!}\simeq f_{\ast}
for
\mathcal{R}
-proper maps
f
automatically comes with all the relevant higher coherences.

Similarly, when
f:X\to Y
is truncated and
\mathcal{R}
-etale, the natural isomorphism
f_{!}\simeq f_{\natural}
(where
f_{\natural}
denotes the left adjoint to
f^{\ast}
), adjoint to
f^{!}\simeq f^{\ast}
, commutes with base change and projection formulas, again up to all higher coherences as encoded in six functor formalisms.

Now we give the basic examples of
\operatorname{Sp}
-etale and
\operatorname{Sp}
-proper maps. (We defer discussion of smoothness to the next section.)

Lemma 7.18
.

For every light profinite set
S
and every open subset
U\subset S
(viewed as a light condensed subset), the inclusion
U\to S
is
\operatorname{Sp}
-etale.

Proof.

First suppose
U
is also a light profinite set. Then
U\to S
is
\operatorname{Sp}
-proper by definition, and if we set
V=S\setminus U
so that

\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(S)=\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(U)\times\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(V)

via pullback, then it is easy to see in terms of this decomposition that
U\to S
is
\operatorname{Sp}
-etale.

In the general case, we can write
U=\sqcup_{n}U_{n}
as a disjoint union of light profinite sets indexed by
\mathbb{N}
. As
U_{n}\to U
is
\operatorname{Sp}
-etale on pullback to any light profinite set by the first part of this proof, we deduce that
U_{n}\to U
is
\operatorname{Sp}
-etale. So is
U_{n}\to S
, again by the first part of this proof. Then by Remark
7.15
we deduce that
U\to S
is
\operatorname{Sp}
-etale, as desired.
∎

Generalizing this, we have the following, ensuring in particular that there is no clash of terminology concerning the word “etale”.

Proposition 7.19
.

Suppose
f:X\to Y
is a truncated etale map of light condensed anima. Then
f
is
\operatorname{Sp}
-etale.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on the truncatedness of
f
. If
f
is
-2
-truncated, then it is an isomorphism, and the claim is trivial. Now we perform the inductive step. Suppose
f:X\to Y
is
d+1
-truncated, and we know the claim for
d
-truncated maps. By Remark
7.15
we can assume
Y\in\operatorname{ProfSet}^{light}
. By the comparison of etale maps with topological sheaves (Lemma
4.20
), there is a collection of open subsets
\{U_{i}\subset Y\}_{i\in I}
and maps
\{s_{i}:U_{i}\to X\}
over
Y
which are jointly surjective. The map
\sqcup_{i}U_{i}\to X
is
d
-truncated and etale, hence
\mathcal{R}
-etale by induction. As each
U_{j}\to\sqcup_{i}U_{i}
and
U_{j}\to X
is
\operatorname{Sp}
-etale by Lemma
7.18
, we deduce from Remark
7.15
that
f
is
\operatorname{Sp}
-etale, as desired.
∎

We also have the following examples of
\operatorname{Sp}
-proper maps, proved using the descendability formalism from the previous section.

Theorem 7.20
.

Suppose
X
is a light condensed anima such that either:

1.

X
is a second-countable compact Hausdorff space of finite cohomological dimension; or

2.

X
is a 1-truncated light profinite anima of finite cohomological dimension.

Then the map

f:X\to\ast

is
\operatorname{Sp}
-proper.

Proof.

In both cases, we have the following properties:

1.

\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(X;\operatorname{Sp})
is rigid and countably assembled (see Section
6
);

2.

X
has finite cohomological dimension (by assumption);

3.

there exists a light profinite set and a surjective
g:S\twoheadrightarrow X
, such that for any
T\to X
with
T\in\operatorname{ProfSet}^{light}
the fiber product
T\times_{X}S
is also a light profinite set.

Let us show that for any such
X
the map
X\to\ast
is
\operatorname{Sp}
-proper.

Indeed, by assumption 3 and Theorem
7.20

g:S\to K
is
\operatorname{Sp}
-proper. As
S\to\ast
is likewise
\operatorname{Sp}
-proper, using Remark
7.15
it suffices to show that
g_{\ast}\mathbb{S}\in\operatorname{CAlg}(\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(K;\operatorname{Sp}))
is descendable. Applying the criterion of Theorem
6.21
, it suffices to show that for all points
x:\ast\to K
, the pullback
x^{\ast}g_{\ast}\mathbb{S}
is descendable, of some index which is uniform in
x
. But by base change
x^{\ast}g_{\ast}\mathbb{S}
is
(g_{x})_{\ast}\mathbb{S}
for
g_{x}:g^{-1}(x)\to\ast
. Since
g
is surjective,
g_{x}
has a section, hence so does the unit map
\mathbb{S}\to(g_{x})_{\ast}\mathbb{S}
, and thus
(g_{x})_{\ast}\mathbb{S}
is descendable of index
\leq 1
, whence the claim.
∎

Remark 7.21
.

The same argument shows that for any surjective map
f:T\twoheadrightarrow S
of light profinite sets,
f_{\ast}\mathbb{S}
is descendable in
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(S;\operatorname{Sp})
. In particular
f
is a
!
-cover by Remark
7.15
. Of course, finite disjont unions are also
!
-covers by Lemma
7.18
. Thus the Grothendieck topology on light profinite sets has the property that every cover is a
!
-cover. (Caution that this is only true for light profinite sets, not for general light condensed anima.)

This implies the following convenient property: the condition that a map
f:X\to Y
in
\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}
be
\mathcal{R}
-
!
-able is local on
Y
in the light condensed Grothendieck topology, i.e. if
\{Y_{i}\to Y\}_{i\in I}
is a jointly surjective family of maps to
Y
and
X\times_{Y}Y_{i}\to Y_{i}
is
\mathcal{R}
-
!
-able for all
i
, then
f
is
\mathcal{R}
-
!
-able. The same holds for the classes of
\mathcal{R}
-proper,
\mathcal{R}
-smooth, and
\mathcal{R}
-etale maps.

Remark 7.22
.

The presence of finite dimensionality assumptions in Theorem
7.20
is unavoidable. In fact, suppose
X
is a light condensed anima such that
f:X\to\ast
is
\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Z})
-proper. Then
X
has finite cohomological dimension.

Indeed, suppose the contrary. Then there is a sequence
\mathcal{A}_{0},\mathcal{A}_{1},\ldots
of etale sheaves of abelian groups on
X
and integers
i_{0},i_{1},\ldots
tending to
\infty
such that
H^{i_{n}}(X;\mathcal{A}_{n})\neq 0
for all
n\geq 0
. Consider
\mathcal{A}=\oplus_{n\geq 0}\mathcal{A}_{n}[i_{n}]
. Because
f_{\ast}
preserves colimits (due to the properness assumption), we have

H^{0}(X;\mathcal{A})\simeq\oplus_{n\geq 0}H^{i_{n}}(X;\mathcal{A}_{n}).

On the other hand we also have
\mathcal{A}=\prod_{n\geq 0}\mathcal{A}_{n}[i_{n}]
because this holds after
N
-truncation for any
N
. Then since
f_{\ast}
preserves limits we also have

H^{0}(X;\mathcal{A})\simeq\prod_{n\geq 0}H^{i_{n}}(X;\mathcal{A}_{n}).

We deduce that the comparison map from the direct sum to the product is an iso. Thus
H^{i_{n}}(X;\mathcal{A}_{n})=0
for all but finitely many
n
, a contradiction.

Note that this also means that if
f:X\to Y
is any
D(\mathbb{Z})
-proper map of light condensed anima, then the pullback
X\times_{Y}S
along any map from a light profinite set
S\to Y
has finite cohomological dimension, and for
s\in S
there is a uniform bound on the cohomological dimensions of the fibers.

Remark 7.23
.

By Theorem
7.20
, if
G
is a light profinite group of finite cohomological dimension, then
BG\to\ast
is
\operatorname{Sp}
-proper. Suppose now
G
is only
virtually
of finite cohomological dimension, and let
N
be an open normal subgroup of finite cohomological dimension. Consider

BG\overset{f}{\to}B(G/N)\overset{g}{\to}\ast.

The map
g
has proper diagonal, so there is a natural map
g_{!}\to g_{\ast}
. But
g
is etale so
g_{!}
is left adjoint to
g^{\ast}
, and this
g_{!}\to g_{\ast}
is just the usual “norm” or transfer map from homotopy orbits to homotopy fixed points.

Thus if we pass to
\mathcal{R}=L_{T(n)}\operatorname{Sp}
coefficients, then by telescopic Tate vanishing (
[
Kuh04
]
, the norm map
g_{!}\to g_{\ast}
is an iso hence
g
is proper, and therefore so is
BG\to\ast
.

On the other hand, turning this around, if
G
has finite cohomological dimension and hence
g\circ f
is proper a priori, we deduce that the norm map
g_{!}\to g_{\ast}
is an isomorphism on any object of the form
f_{\ast}\mathcal{F}
; this is the “Theorem of Tate-Thomason”.

Now we consider the topological situation.

Proposition 7.24
.

Suppose
X,Y
are topological spaces each of which admits a basis of second countable compact Hausdorff spaces of finite cohomological dimension. Then any map
f:\underline{X}\to\underline{Y}
is
\operatorname{Sp}
-
!
-able.

Proof.

Because
!
-able maps are a geometric class of maps, it suffices to show that
f:X\to\ast
is
!
-able (and same for
Y
, but the hypotheses on
X
and
Y
are the same). Open covers are
\operatorname{Sp}
-etale by Lemma
7.18
, so by Remark
7.15
we can reduce to the case where
X
is second countable finite dimensional compact Hausdorff which is given by Theorem
7.20
.
∎

In particular, if
F
is a local field, then this result applies to any
F
-manifold.

Note that the uniqueness result of
[
DK24
]
shows that the six functor formalism on the class of topological spaces in the previous proposition agrees with any other reasonable one made by potentially different means. For example, on the Hausdorff full subcategory we could use the usual method of factorizations into open immersions and proper maps. Let’s just record the following which gives the essence of the comparison.

Lemma 7.25
.

Suppose
X
is a light condensed anima and
j:U\hookrightarrow X
is an open immersion with closed complement
i:Z\subset X
(meaning, on pullback to any light profinite set we see an open inclusion and its closed complement). Then for any
\mathcal{F}\in\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(X;\operatorname{Sp})
, there is a unique and functorial fiber sequence

j_{!}j^{\ast}\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{F}\to i_{\ast}i^{\ast}\mathcal{F}

such that the second map is the unit for the adjunction and the first map is the counit for the adjunction (using that
j:U\hookrightarrow X
is etale so
j^{!}=j^{\ast}
).

Proof.

The spectrum of maps
j_{!}j^{\ast}\mathcal{F}\to i_{\ast}i^{\ast}\mathcal{F}
is
0
by adjunction, because
i^{\ast}j_{!}=0
by base-change and the fact that
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(\emptyset;\operatorname{Sp})=0
. For the same reason the spectrum of natural transformations in
\mathcal{F}
of that form is
0
. Thus there is a unique and functorial nullhomotopy of our purported fiber sequence. Because isomorphisms of etale sheaves are detected on points (Remark
4.18
), to prove it is indeed a fiber sequence it suffices to check on points, where
j
becomes either
\emptyset\subset\ast
or
\ast\subset\ast
, where in both cases the result is trivial.
∎

Example 7.26
.

In particular, if
f:X\to S
is a map of second countable finite dimensional compact Hausdorff spaces and
j:U\to X
is as above, then for any sheaf
\mathcal{F}
on
X
we can calculate the lower-
!
of its restriction to
U
in terms of lower-
*
functors as the fiber

(f\circ j)_{!}f^{\ast}\mathcal{F}=\operatorname{Fib}(f_{\ast}\mathcal{F}\to(f\circ i)_{\ast}f^{\ast}\mathcal{F}).

Intuitively, compactly supported cohomology on
U
is the fiber of cohomology of the compactification restricting to cohomology of the boundary.

8
Smooth morphisms

Now we go a bit beyond the basic results proved at the end of the previous section. We are interested in exploring the six functor formalism on those light condensed anima which are associated to
F
-analytic Artin stacks, as discussed in Section
5
.

Lemma 8.1
.

Let
F
be a local field, and suppose
f:X\to Y
is a representable submersion in
\operatorname{Sh}(\operatorname{Man}_{F})
. Then
\underline{X}\to\underline{Y}
is
\operatorname{Sp}
-
!
-able.

Proof.

By Remark
7.21
, we can use descent on
Y
to reduce to the case where
Y
is an
F
-manifold, when this follows from Proposition
7.24
.
∎

We would like to be able to say that if
f:X\to Y
is a representable submersion which is
surjective
, then
\underline{X}\to\underline{Y}
is a
!
-cover, but this is perhaps too much to ask in full generality in the nonarchimedean setting. However we can show it in the archimedean setting using
smoothness
(Definition
7.12
). In principle this requires checking a number of separate conditions. But we now give a very convenient mechanism for reducing some of these conditions to others.

First, note that by descent it suffices to treat the case
Y=S\in\operatorname{ProfSet}^{light}
. Moreover, again by descent, to check that the formation of
f^{!}
commutes with base-change it suffices to show this for base-changes along maps of light profinite sets. In that setting the following condition will be useful.

Definition 8.2
.

Let
f:X\to S
be a map of light condensed anima with
S\in\operatorname{ProfSet}^{light}
, and let
\mathcal{R}\in\operatorname{CAlg}(\operatorname{Pr}^{L}_{st})
be compactly assembled. Say that
f

satisfies categorical base-change
(with
\mathcal{R}
-coefficients) if for all maps
S^{\prime}\to S
of light profinite sets with pullback
X^{\prime}=X\times_{S}S^{\prime}
, the natural map in
\operatorname{CAlg}(\operatorname{Pr}_{st}^{L})

\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(X;\mathcal{R})\otimes_{\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(S;\mathcal{R})}\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(S^{\prime};\mathcal{R})\rightarrow\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(X^{\prime};\mathcal{R})

is an isomorphism.

Note that this definition does not refer to the six functor formalism, but only to the symmetric monoidal pullback functoriality. However, we have the following lemma.

Lemma 8.3
.

Let
f:X\to S
be a map of light condensed anima with
S\in\operatorname{ProfSet}^{light}
, and let
\mathcal{R}\in\operatorname{CAlg}(\operatorname{Pr}^{L}_{st})
be compactly assembled. If
f
is
\mathcal{R}
-
!
-able, then
f
satisfies categorical base-change with
\mathcal{R}
-coefficients.

Proof.

By Remark
7.9
, it suffices to show that the class of maps of light condensed anima, each of whose base-change to a light profinite set satisfies categorical base-change, contains
E_{0}
and is closed under the two extension procedures
E^{\prime}\mapsto E^{\prime}_{!}
and
E^{\prime}\mapsto E^{\prime}_{\ast}
.

To see it contains
E_{0}
we need to show that a map of light profinite sets
X\to S
satisfies categorical base-change. Using that
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(-;\operatorname{Sp})
on a light profinite set is compactly generated by the unit, this follows from proper base-change.

Closure under
E^{\prime}\mapsto E^{\prime}_{\ast}
is tautological. For closure under
E^{\prime}\mapsto E^{\prime}_{!}
, suppose
X\to S
admits a
!
-cover
\{X_{i}\to X\}_{i\in I}
in
E^{\prime}
such that each
X_{i}\to S
lies in
E^{\prime}
. Each iterated fiber product of the
X_{i}\to X
mapping to
S
also lies in
E^{\prime}
and hence satisfies categorical base change. Then we write
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(X;\mathcal{R})
as the colimit of the Cech nerve via
!
-functors. By definition this is a colimit in
\operatorname{Pr}^{L}
, also after base-change to
S^{\prime}
. But the diagram lies in
\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(S;\mathcal{R})}(\operatorname{Pr}^{L})
hence also gives a colimit there. Using that relative tensor products preserve colimits we conclude.
∎

Using categorical base-change we get the following criterion for smoothness.

Proposition 8.4
.

Let
f:X\to S
be a map of light condensed anima with
S\in\operatorname{ProfSet}^{light}
and let
\mathcal{R}\in\operatorname{CAlg}(\operatorname{Pr}^{L}_{st})
such that
\mathcal{R}
is
semi-rigid
(Remark
6.22
,
[
Ari+22
]
App. C). Suppose that
f
is
\mathcal{R}
-
!
-able. Then the following properties are equivalent:

1.

f
is
\mathcal{R}
-smooth;

2.

f^{!}
commutes with colimits and
f^{!}1
is invertible.

Furthermore, if we just assume that
f^{!}
commutes with colimits, then the formation of
f^{!}
commutes with base-change, so we can also test invertibility of
f^{!}1
locally on
S
.

Proof.

Suppose
f^{!}
preserves colimits. Since
\mathcal{R}
and
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(S;\operatorname{Sp})
are both semi-rigid, so is their tensor product
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(S;\mathcal{R})
. Thus by
[
Ari+22
]
Prop. C.5.5 the projection formula for
f^{!}
is automatic.

Now, suppose
f^{\prime}:X^{\prime}\to S^{\prime}
is the base-change of
f
along a map of light profinite sets
S^{\prime}\to S
. Then
(f^{\prime})_{!}
is a
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(S^{\prime};\mathcal{R})
-linear map in
\operatorname{Pr}^{L}
which agrees with
f_{!}
as a
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(S;\mathcal{R})
-linear map on restriction to
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(X;\mathcal{R})
. By categorical base-change it follows that

(f^{\prime})_{!}=f_{!}\otimes_{\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(S;\mathcal{R})}\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(S^{\prime};\mathcal{R}).

By preservation of colimits and the projection formula,
f^{!}
is the right adjoint to
f_{!}
in the 2-categorical sense of
\operatorname{Mod}_{\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(S;\mathcal{R})}(\operatorname{Pr}^{L})
and hence this adjunction passes through the tensor product, establishing the claim.
∎

Remark 8.5
.

Suppose
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(X;\mathcal{R})
is compactly generated. Then
f^{!}
preserves colimits if and only if
f_{!}
sends compact objects to compact objects. It even suffices to just consider a generating set of compact objects.

We apply this, first in the archimedean case.

Theorem 8.6
.

Suppose that
f:X\to Y
is a representable submersion in
\operatorname{Sh}(\operatorname{Man}_{\mathbb{R}})
. Then
f:\underline{X}\to\underline{Y}
is
\operatorname{Sp}
-smooth.

Proof.

Working locally on
Y
(Remark
7.21
), we reduce to the case where
Y
is a manifold. We can also work locally on the source in the sense of open covers by Remark
7.15
. Thus we can further reduce to the case of a projection off an open polydisk,
S\times D\to S
. By base-change and composition this in turn reduces to showing that the open interval
f:(-1,1)\to\ast
is
\operatorname{Sp}
-smooth. By Lemma
8.4
, for this it suffices to show that
f^{!}
preserves colimits and
f^{!}\mathbb{S}
is invertible. Since
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}((-1,1);\operatorname{Sp})
identifies with usual sheaves of spectra on the topological space
(-1,1)
(Lemma
4.20
), and the open sub-intervals form a basis for the topology closed under finite intersection, to prove
f^{!}
preserves colimits it suffices to show that
f_{!}(h_{U})
is a compact object in
\operatorname{Sp}
for any open subinterval
U\subset(-1,1)
. But calculating using Example
7.26
and the compactification
[-1,1]
gives
f_{!}(h_{U})\simeq\Sigma^{-1}\mathbb{S}
, whence the conclusion. To prove that
f^{!}\mathbb{S}
is invertible, we can replace
(-1,1)
by
\mathbb{R}
; then by base-change for
f^{!}
and the fact
\mathbb{R}
has a group structure we deduce that
f^{!}\mathbb{S}
is a constant sheaf. Thus it suffices to show that
\Gamma((-1,1);f^{!}\mathbb{S})
is an invertible spectrum. But this is by definition the dual of what we calculated earlier to be
\mathbb{S}[-1]
, whence the claim.
∎

Corollary 8.7
.

1.

If
Y\twoheadrightarrow X
is any surjective representable submersion in
\operatorname{Sh}(\operatorname{Man}_{\mathbb{R}})
, then
\underline{Y}\to\underline{X}
is a
\operatorname{Sp}
-
!
-cover.

2.

Every map between
\mathbb{R}
-analytic smooth Artin stacks is
\operatorname{Sp}
-
!
-able.

3.

For every
\mathbb{R}
-analytic smooth Artin stack
X
the map
\underline{X}\to\ast
is
\operatorname{Sp}
-smooth.

Proof.

For 1, we have seen it’s
\operatorname{Sp}
-smooth. It’s also conservative on pullbacks because of surjectivity. Thus the claim follows from Remark
7.15
.

For 2, because the
!
-able maps form a geometric class, it suffices to show 3.

For 3, let
M\to X
be a surjective representable submersion. This is smooth and a cover by 1 and
M\to\ast
is also smooth by Lemma
8.1
. Thus
X\to\ast
is smooth by Remark
7.15
.
∎

Now we move to the non-archimedean case, which is more technically demanding.

To apply Proposition
8.4
(in conjunction with Remark
8.5
) we need criteria detecting when an object in
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(S;\operatorname{Sp}_{\widehat{p}})
is compact, and also when it is invertible. To complete the picture we also discuss dualizable objects. (Dualizable is in some sense a bit nicer than compact because being dualizable is a local condition, by descent.)

Lemma 8.8
.

Let
p
be a prime,
S
a light condensed anima, and
\mathcal{F}\in\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(S;\operatorname{Sp}_{\widehat{p}})
. Then the following are equivalent:

1.

\mathcal{F}
is dualizable.

2.

\mathcal{F}/p
is locally bounded below in the t-structure,
\pi_{\ast}(\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p})
locally lives in only finitely many degrees, and each
\pi_{i}(\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p})
is represented by a (
0
-truncated) finite etale map
X\to S
.

Moreover, the following are equivalent:

1.

\mathcal{F}
is invertible.

2.

\mathcal{F}
is dualizable, for any point
s:\ast\to S
we have
s^{\ast}\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}\simeq\mathbb{F}_{p}[d]
for some
d\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Finally, suppose
S
is a light profinite set. Then the following are equivalent:

1.

\mathcal{F}
is compact.

2.

\mathcal{F}
is dualizable and killed by some power of
p
.

Proof.

For 1, by descent we can assume
S
is a light profinite set. Suppose
\mathcal{F}
is dualizable. As the unit is compact mod
p
, it follows that
\mathcal{F}/p
is a compact object, hence lies in the thick subcategory generated by
\mathbb{S}/p
, hence is bounded below. Moreover
\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}\in\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(S;\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{F}_{p}))
is dualizable and therefore compact, hence it comes by pullback from a finite set where it has finite dimensional stalks whence
\mathcal{F}
satisfies 2. For the converse, since each homotopy group is represented by a finite etale map, we see it is locally constant with finite dimensional stalks, whence it lies in the thick subcategory generared by
\mathbb{F}_{p}
(we are still in the situation where
S
is a light profinite set). Applying Lemma
13.16
we deduce that
\mathcal{F}
lies in the thick subcategory generated by the unit
\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}
, whence
\mathcal{F}
is dualizable.

For the claim about invertible objects, again we can reduce to light profinite sets. Clearly 1 implies 2, and for the converse note that we can check
\mathcal{F}^{\vee}\otimes\mathcal{F}\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}
by applying
-\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}
because both sides are bounded below (as we saw above, a dualizable
\mathcal{F}
lies in the thick subcategory generated by
\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}
hence so does its dual).

Finally, for the claim about compact objects, compact implies dualizable and killed by a power of
p
because the generating compact object
\mathbb{S}/p
is dualizable and killed by a power of
p
. For the converse, we saw above that any
\mathcal{F}
which is dualizable lies in the thick subcategory generated by
\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}
. If
\mathcal{F}
is killed by a power of
p
, then we deduce that
\mathcal{F}
lies in the thick subcategory generated by some
\mathbb{S}/p^{n}
which is compact.
∎

We will now consider the setting of
F=\mathbb{Q}_{p}
, with
\operatorname{Sp}_{\widehat{p}}
-coefficients. Our results are not quite so strong as in the Archimedean case: we will not show that all
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-analytic smooth Artin stacks are
!
-able, but only a (fairly large) subclass.

We will rely on Proposition
5.15
, showing that a
p
-adic Lie group
G\to S
in families locally on
S
admits a compact open subgroup object
H\subset G
such that each fiber is a uniform pro-
p
group. We can use this in conjunction with the following:

Lemma 8.9
.

Let
S
be a compact Hausdorff (hence light profinite)
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-manifold. Let
H\to S
be a compact Hausdroff
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-Lie group over
S
such that each fiber is a uniform pro-
p
-group. Denote by
BH\to S
the relative classifying stack. Then:

1.

BH
has finite cohomological dimension.

2.

\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(BH;\operatorname{Sp})
is rigid, with a family of compact generators provided by the
(j_{n})_{!}\mathbb{S}
for
j_{n}:BH^{p^{n}}\to BH
.

3.

\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(BH;\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{F}_{p}))
is compactly generated by the unit
\mathbb{F}_{p}
.

Proof.

For 1, by Remark
6.23
it suffices to show that each fiber of
BH\to S
has uniformly bounded finite cohomological dimension. We recalled this with mod
p
coefficients in
5
. With
\mathbb{Z}[1/p]
-coefficients the cohomological dimension of each fiber is
0
because they are pro-
p
groups.

For 2, the rigidity claim follows from 1 and Example
6.19
, and we get compact generation by the
j_{!}\mathbb{S}
where
j
runs over all finite etale maps to
BH
. To see that the
(j_{n})_{!}\mathbb{S}
are enough to generate, note by Proposition
5.16
every finite etale map to
BH
is dominated by
BH^{p^{n}}\times_{S}T
for some
T\subset S
compact open and
n\geq 0
. But the
j_{!}
of this is a retract of the one for
j_{n}
, proving the claim.

For 3, from 2 we get that the
(j_{n})_{!}\mathbb{F}_{p}
give compact generators. To show that
\mathbb{F}_{p}
on its own generates, it suffices to show that each
(j_{n})_{!}\mathbb{F}_{p}
lies in the thick subcategory generated by
\mathbb{F}_{p}
. For that, because
H/H^{p^{n}}\to S
is a finite etale group object with
p
-group fibers (
5
), by base-change and working locally on
S
it suffices to show that if
Q
is a finite
p
-group then for the map
f:\ast\to\ast/Q
we have that
f_{!}\mathbb{F}_{p}
lies in the thick subcategory generated by
\mathbb{F}_{p}
. This holds because the group ring of a finite
p
-group with
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-coefficients, as a representation, admits a finite filtration with associated gradeds the trivial representation
\mathbb{F}_{p}
.
∎

Here is our main result on
!
-ability in the nonarchimedean setting. We do not yet have to pass to
p
-complete coefficients; that will come when we discuss smoothness.

Theorem 8.10
.

Let
X
be a
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-analytic smooth Artin stack. If there exists a surjective representable submersion
M\to X
with
M\in\operatorname{Man}_{F}
which is a
\operatorname{Sp}
-
!
-cover, then every surjective representable submersion
Y\to X
is a
\operatorname{Sp}
-
!
-cover.

Call
X
“
!
-good” if it satisfies this condition. Then
\ast
is
!
-good, and we have the following closure properties:

1.

Suppose
Y\to X
is a map such that there exists a surjective representable submersion
M\to X
from a manifold such that the pullback
Y\times_{X}M
is representable by a manifold. (For example
Y\to X
could be a representable submersion.) Then
X
is
!
-good
\Rightarrow

Y
is
!
-good.

2.

If
X
is
!
-good and
G\to X
is a group object in representable submersions over
X
, then the relative classifying stack
BG
is
!
-good.

3.

If
X
admits an etale cover by
!
-good stacks, then
X
is
!
-good.

Finally, for any
!
-good
X
, the map
X\to\ast
is
\operatorname{Sp}
-
!
-able. (Hence any map between such
X
’s is likewise
!
-able.)

Proof.

Suppose given a surjective representable submersion
M\to X
with
M\in\operatorname{Man}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
which is a
!
-cover, and let
Y\to X
be an arbitrary surjective representable submersion. We already know
Y\to X
is
!
-able by Lemma
8.1
, so we can test whether
Y\to X
is a
!
-cover on pullback to
M
. Thus it suffices to show that any surjective submersion
M\to N
of manifolds is a
!
-cover. But it’s etale locally split.

Now consider 1. Assuming
X
is
!
-good, we have that
M\to X
is a
!
-cover, hence so is its pullback
M\times_{X}Y\to Y
(note also that this pullback commutes with the functor to light condensed anima by Proposition
5.3
). Thus
Y
is
!
-good.

Now consider 2. This is the only one where we have to work a bit. Let
M\to X
be a surjective representable submersion, so a
!
-cover by goodness of
X
. The composition
M\to X\to BG
is a surjective representable submersion, and we claim it’s a
!
-cover. Choose another surjective representable submersion
N\to X
. (We could choose the same one, but we separate the notation for clarity.) By hypothesis
N\to X
is a
!
-cover. Since
BG
maps to
X
we can pull back our situation along
N\to X
and therefore reduce to the case where
X=N
is a manifold. Then it suffices to show that
N\to BG
is a
!
-cover. Working etale locally, we can assume
N
is compact Hausdorff. Then by Proposition
5.15
there is a compact open sub-group object
H\subset G
such that each fiber
H_{x}
is a uniform pro-
p
-group and by Proposition
5.16

BH\to BG
is (surjective and) etale. Thus we reduce to
H=G
. Then
f:N\to BH
is proper because
H
is light profinite (
5.16
) and so by Remark
7.15
it suffices to show that
f_{\ast}\mathbb{S}
is descendable. Then by Lemma
8.9
we can apply Theorem
6.21
and work pointwise, where we have a section, to conclude.

Finally, suppose
\{U_{i}\to X\}_{i\in I}
is an etale cover such that each
U_{i}
is
!
-good. Choose a surjective representable submersion
M_{i}\to U_{i}
for each
i
, so by hypothesis this is a
!
-cover. Then
\sqcup_{i}M_{i}\to X
is a surjective representable submersion and it suffices to show it’s a
!
-cover. But we can check this on each
U_{i}
where it’s refined by
M_{i}\to U_{i}
as required.

It remains to show that if
X
is
!
-good then
X\to\ast
is
!
-able (as always, with
\operatorname{Sp}_{\widehat{p}}
-coefficients). Let
M\to X
be a
!
-cover from a manifold. We know
M\to\ast
is
!
-able by Lemma
8.1
, and the claim follows by Remark
7.15

∎

Example 8.11
.

Any quotient stack is
!
-good. Indeed,
M/G\to\ast/G
is a representable submerion, so this follows from 1 and 2. More generally we can take local quotient stacks, or local quotient stacks over local quotient stacks, etc. This generality will certainly cover all the Artin stacks we will need to consider.

Smoothness is a lot less common in the nonarchimedean setting: already of course
\mathbb{Q}_{p}\to\ast
is not smooth. But Lazard proved both that Poincaré duality holds (with
p
-adic coefficients) for the continuous cohomology of sufficiently small
p
-adic Lie groups, and that every
p
-adic Lie group has a sufficiently small (in the same sense) open subgroup. Later Serre clarified that “sufficiently small” exactly means compact and
p
-torsionfree. This suggests that classifying stacks are smooth, and proper under a hypothesis like Serre’s. In fact we have the following.

Theorem 8.12
.

Suppose that
X
is a
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-analytic smooth Artin stack which is
!
-good. Let
G\to X
be a group object in representable submersions over
X
, and let
BG\to X
denote the relative classifying stack. Then:

1.

BG\to X
is
\operatorname{Sp}_{\widehat{p}}
-smooth.

2.

If
G\to X
is proper and each fiber
G_{x}
is
p
-torsionfree, then
BG\to X
is
\operatorname{Sp}_{\widehat{p}}
-proper.

Proof.

By Example
8.11
we have that
BG
is also
!
-good. In particular
BG\to X
is
!
-able. Let us first show Claim 2. By Remark
7.21
we can check properness on pullback to any light profinite set
S
(after implicitly passing to the light condensed anima). By refining via a surjection we can even assume
S
is a
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-manifold and the map
S\to X
comes from a map in
\operatorname{Sh}(\operatorname{Man}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}})
. Now we replace
X
by
S
. Thus
G\to S
is a group object in proper submersions over the compact Hausdorff
p
-adic manifold (hence light profinite set)
S
, and we are assuming also that each fiber is
p
-torsionfree.

By Proposition
5.15
we can find a compact open subgroup
H\subset G
such that each fiber
H_{s}
is a uniform pro-
p
-group. By Lemma
8.9

\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(\underline{BH};\operatorname{Sp})
is compactly generated by dualizable objects. However, the map
f:BH\to BG
is etale (Proposition
5.16
), hence
f_{!}
sends compact objects to compact objects, and
f
is even finite etale, hence
f_{!}
sends dualizables to dualizables (check on pullback to a light profinite set). Moreover, since
f^{\ast}
is conservative,
f_{!}
sends a set of generators to a set of generators. Thus we deduce that
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(\underline{BG};\operatorname{Sp})
is likewise compactly generated by dualizable objects. But Serre showed (
[
Ser65
]
) that every compact
p
-torsionfree
p
-adic Lie group has finite mod
p
cohomological dimension, bounded by the dimension of the manifold. It follows from Remark
6.23
that
\underline{BG}
has finite mod
p
cohomological dimension, and then we can apply Theorem
6.21
(or rather Remark
6.22
) to see that for the (proper, as
G
is light profinite) map
e:S\to\underline{BG}
, the object
e_{\ast}\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}
is descendable. Thus
\underline{BG}\to S
is proper by Remark
7.15
, as desired.

For claim 1 we proceed similarly. We can check smoothness
!
-locally (Remark
7.21
) and hence it suffices to consider the case where
X=S
is a
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
-manifold, which we can even assume compact Hausdorff (hence light profinite). Then by Proposition
5.15
there exists a compact open subgroup object
H\subset G
such that each fiber
H_{s}
is a uniform pro-
p
-group. Because
BH\to BG
is etale and surjective (Proposition
5.16
), we can reduce to the case
H=G
. Then the
(j_{n})_{!}\mathbb{S}/p
from Example
6.14
give compact generators, so applying Proposition
8.4
, it suffices to show that each of these goes to a compact object under
f_{!}
, and that
f^{!}\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}
is invertible.

We put aside the invertibility for now and come back to it later. Thus consider the problem of proving
f_{!}
sends these compacts to compacts. As
H^{p^{n}}
is uniform whenever
H
is, the problem is actually the same for all
n
: we just need to show that if
H
is family of uniform pro-
p
-groups over
S
, then
f_{!}\mathbb{S}/p
is compact in
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(S;\operatorname{Sp}_{\widehat{p}})
. By 2,
f
is proper, so we can replace
f_{!}
by
f_{\ast}
. Note that
f_{\ast}\mathbb{S}/p
is bounded below by the finite cohomological dimension (
8.9
), so applying Lemma
8.8
we reduce to showing the following: the homotopy group sheaves of
f_{\ast}\mathbb{F}_{p}
are
0
outside finitely many degrees and in all degrees are locally trivial with finite fibers.

We start by analyzing
H^{1}f_{\ast}\mathbb{F}_{p}=\pi_{-1}f_{\ast}\mathbb{F}_{p}
. The global sections are the homomorphisms of
S
-group objects from
H
to the constant object
\mathbb{F}_{p}
, and similarly for sections over any
S^{\prime}\subset S
. As
H^{p}\subset H
is a normal subgroup object, this is the same as the homomorphisms
H/H^{p}\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
. Recall (Proposition
5.16
) that
H/H^{p}
is finite etale, and in fact a finite locally constant
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-vector space sheaf. This implies that
H^{1}f_{\ast}\mathbb{F}_{p}
is just the
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-linear dual of
H/H^{p}
, and the claim holds in degree
1
. Now we recall that
f_{\ast}\mathbb{F}_{p}
is a commutative algebra object. Note that for any local section
x\in H^{1}f_{\ast}\mathbb{F}_{p}
, the square
x^{2}
vanishes in
H^{2}f_{\ast}\mathbb{F}_{p}
. Indeed, as
f_{\ast}
commutes with base-change we can check this fiberwise where it reduces to the classical fact about uniform pro-
p
-groups (
5
). It follows we can make a comparison map

\Lambda^{i}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}H^{1}f_{\ast}\mathbb{F}_{p}\to H^{i}f_{\ast}\mathbb{F}_{p}

of sheaves of
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-vector spaces on
S
extending the identity map in degree
1
. Again checking on stalks and using the classical fact we deduce that this is an isomorphism. As the left hand side is clearly finite locally constant (given we already know this in degree 1) and vanishes for all but finitely many
i
, we have thus proved the desired claim that
f_{\ast}\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}
is dualizable, and so the formation of
f^{!}
commutes with base-change and satisfies the projection formula by Lemma
8.4
.

What remains to be shown is that
f^{!}\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}
is an invertible object. For this we first claim that (mod
p
) it is bounded below in the t-structure. By base-change and testing on points we can reduce to the case
S=\ast
, and we only need see that
e^{\ast}f^{!}\mathbb{S}/p
is bounded below. But by Proposition
4.34
and a Postnikov tower argument we have that
e^{\ast}f^{!}\mathbb{S}/p
is the colimit of the sections of
f^{!}\mathbb{S}/p
over the
BH^{p^{n}}
. These are the duals to
(f_{n})_{\ast}\mathbb{S}/p
which are bounded below and have a uniform bound on the
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-homology by the above calculations, implying the claim.

Thus by Lemma
8.8
it suffices to show that
f^{!}\mathbb{F}_{p}
is invertible in
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(BH;\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{F}_{p}))
(back in the setting of general
S
). For this, recall from Lemma
8.9
that
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(BH;\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{F}_{p}))
is compactly generated by the unit. It follows that the comparison map in
\operatorname{CAlg}(\operatorname{Pr}^{L})

\operatorname{Mod}_{f_{\ast}\mathbb{F}_{p}}(\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(S;\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{F}_{p})))\to\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(BH;\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{F}_{p}))

is an isomorphism. Now
f^{!}
is just the double right adjoint to the pullback functor
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(S;\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{F}_{p}))\to\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(BH;\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{F}_{p}))
. Translating this over via the isomorphism, we find that it suffices to show that the double right adjoint to the base-change functor

-\otimes f_{\ast}\mathbb{F}_{p}:\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(S;\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{F}_{p}))\to\operatorname{Mod}_{f\ast\mathbb{F}_{p}}(\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(S;\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{F}_{p})))

sends the unit
\mathbb{F}_{p}
to an invertible object in the target. This double right adjoint sends

M\mapsto\underline{\operatorname{Map}}(f_{\ast}\mathbb{F}_{p};M)

where we take internal Hom in
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(S;\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{F}_{p}))
, and give this the
f_{\ast}\mathbb{F}_{p}
-module structure coming from the source
f_{\ast}\mathbb{F}_{p}
.

Thus it suffices to show that the dual
\underline{\operatorname{Map}}(f_{\ast}\mathbb{F}_{p};\mathbb{F}_{p})
of
f_{\ast}\mathbb{F}_{p}
is invertible as a module over
f_{\ast}\mathbb{F}_{p}
. But we calculated the homotopy of
f_{\ast}\mathbb{F}_{p}
as the exterior algebra on a locally free sheaf of finite rank
d
in degree
-1
. Thus the claim follows from the familiar self-duality (up to shift) of exterior algebras.
∎

We have identified various situations where a certain map
f
is smooth. The next important thing is to identify the dualizing object
f^{!}1
in more explicit or tractable terms. This is in fact the main point of this article. But to accomplish it we need a digression.

9
On nonstandard “paths”

Our main theorem says that there is an identification between two a priori different sheaves on
*/G
, where
G
is a
p
-adic Lie group. To accomplish this, we will construct, using the deformation to the tangent bundle, a certain sheaf on
(*/G)\times(\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times})
whose specialization to
1\in\mathbb{Q}_{p}
gives the first sheaf and whose specialization to
0\in\mathbb{Q}_{p}
gives the second sheaf. In this way, our problem will be translated into the question of how to identify the stalks at two different points of a certain sheaf on the stack
\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}
(or rather, we need an in-families analog of this, over
\ast/G
, but we start with the simplest instance).

To explain our solution to this problem, let’s look at an analogous but much more familiar situation. Suppose we have a connected real manifold
M
(or more generally a path-connected topological space
M
), two points
m_{0},m_{1}\in M
, and a sheaf (say, of anima)
\mathcal{F}
on
M
. We want to try to identify the stalks
\mathcal{F}_{0}=m_{0}^{\ast}\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{F}_{1}=m_{1}^{\ast}\mathcal{F}
. One classical way to proceed is to choose a path
\gamma:[0,1]\rightarrow M
with
\gamma(0)=m_{0}
and
\gamma(1)=m_{1}
. Replacing
\mathcal{F}
with its pullback along
\gamma
we can reduce to considering the problem for
M=[0,1]
. The key property of
[0,1]
that makes this an advantageous reduction is that the pullback functor
\pi^{\ast}:\operatorname{An}=\operatorname{Sh}(\ast)\rightarrow\operatorname{Sh}([0,1])
is fully faithful; this is an expression of the contractibility of
[0,1]
, see
[
Lur17
]
A.2. Moreover, a sheaf
\mathcal{F}
lies in the essential image of
\pi^{\ast}
if and only if
\pi^{\ast}\pi_{\ast}\mathcal{F}\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\mathcal{F}
, and it follows in particular that the essential image of
\pi^{\ast}
is closed under all colimits and finite limits (in fact all limits, due to the existence of a left adjoint to
\pi^{\ast}
, a reflection of the fact that
[0,1]
is not just contractible but also
locally
contractible, again see
[
Lur17
]
A.2).

What this all means is that that the condition of “being a constant sheaf” on
[0,1]
really is just a condition, not extra structure, and it is moreover a very robust condition. On the full subcategory of constant sheaves, there is a canonical isomorphism
m_{0}^{\ast}\simeq m_{1}^{\ast}
, because both describe inverses to the isomorphism
\pi^{\ast}
. Thus, after a choice of path connecting
m_{0}
and
m_{1}
in
M
, we get a robust condition on
\mathcal{F}
(that of having constant pullback to the path) which ensures a functorial identification of the stalks of
\mathcal{F}
at these two different points.

We would like the same thing for the stack
\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}
instead of
M
, with respect to the points coming from
0,1\in\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. Unfortunately, there is no map
[0,1]\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}
(say, of light condensed anima) sending
0
to
0
and
1
to
1
: the totally disconnected nature of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
obstructs this rather severely.

However, in our setting we are not actually working with sheaves of anima; we are only working with
p
-complete sheaves of spectra. This raises the possibility of repeating the above story but with a different compact Hausdorff space
\widetilde{I}
instead of
[0,1]
, one for which
\pi^{\ast}:\operatorname{Sh}(\ast;\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\operatorname{Sh}(\widetilde{I};\mathcal{C})
is not necessarily fully faithful for
\mathcal{C}=\operatorname{An}
, but maybe only for
\mathcal{C}=\operatorname{Sp}_{\hat{p}}
, the
\infty
-category of
p
-complete spectra.

Remarkably, this idea works: there is such an
\widetilde{I}
, equipped with two points
i_{0},i_{1}
, mapping to
\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}
in the appropriate way. This
\widetilde{I}
is a one-dimensional compact Hausdorff space which, while not path connected, is contractible from the perspective of torsion coefficients. It is a variant of the famous “topologist’s sine curve”, ending in the solenoid
\varprojlim_{N}\mathbb{R}/N\mathbb{Z}
instead of the usual closed interval. We will describe
\widetilde{I}
and the map
\widetilde{I}\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}
explicitly towards the end of the section; for now we start with some abstract generalities about such exotic “intervals”, using the notion of etale sheaves with coefficients from the previous section (
7.3
).

Definition 9.1
.

Let
f:X\to S
be a map light condensed anima and
\mathcal{C}\in\operatorname{Pr}^{L}
compactly assembled. Say that
f
is a
\mathcal{C}
-blob
if for every base-change
f^{\prime}
of
f
, the functor
(f^{\prime})^{\ast}
(on etale sheaves with
\mathcal{C}
-coefficients) is fully faithful.

Under some hypotheses, we get reasonable fiberwise criteria.

Lemma 9.2
.

Suppose
\mathcal{C}\in\operatorname{Pr}^{L}
is compactly assembled, and let
f:X\to S
be a map of light condensed anima. Suppose that:

1.

either the right adjoint to pullback on etale
\mathcal{C}
-sheaves exists for all base-changes of
f
and commutes with (further) base-change, or the left adjoint to pullback on
\mathcal{C}
-sheaves exists for all base-changes of
f
and commutes with base-change;

2.

for all
s:\ast\to S
, the pullback
f_{s}:X_{s}\to\ast
satisfies that
(f_{s})^{\ast}
is fully faithful.

Then
f
is an
\mathcal{C}
-blob. Moreover, we have that a sheaf
\mathcal{F}\in\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(X;\mathcal{C})
lies in the essential image of
f^{\ast}
if and only if for every
s:\ast\to S
, the pullback of
\mathcal{F}
to
X_{s}
is a constant sheaf, i.e. is pulled back from
\ast
.

In this situation we will call
f
a
right
\mathcal{C}
-blob
or a
left
\mathcal{C}
-blob
depending on which version of condition 1 holds.

Proof.

We need to see that for every base-change
f^{\prime}
of
f
, the pullback
(f^{\prime})^{\ast}
is fully faithful. In the right blob case, we use the criterion which says that
(f^{\prime})^{\ast}
is fully faithful if and only if the unit map

\operatorname{id}\rightarrow(f^{\prime})_{\ast}(f^{\prime})^{\ast}

is an isomorphism. By Lemma
7.3
, we can test this on pullback to points. But by assumption the formation of this unit map commutes with base-change. Similarly in the left blob case we can use the similar criterion involving the left adjoint to
(f^{\prime})^{\ast}
.
∎

Remark 9.3
.

If
\mathcal{C}\in\operatorname{CAlg}(\operatorname{Pr}_{st}^{L})
is compactly assembled, then the right adjoint condition in 1 holds if
f
is
\mathcal{C}
-proper, and the left adjoint condition holds if
f
is
\mathcal{C}
-smooth.

The following simple observation is then our main result in this abstract context.

Theorem 9.4
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a compactly assembled presentable
\infty
-category, let
f:X\to S
be either a left
\mathcal{C}
-blob or a right
\mathcal{C}
-blob, let
\mathcal{F}\in\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(X;\mathcal{C})
, and let
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}:S\to X
be two sections of
f
.

If
\mathcal{F}
restricted to the fiber
X_{s}
is a constant sheaf for all
s:\ast\to S
, then there is a natural isomorphism
(\sigma_{1})^{\ast}\mathcal{F}\simeq(\sigma_{2})^{\ast}\mathcal{F}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
9.2
,
\mathcal{F}\simeq f^{\ast}\mathcal{G}
for some unique and functorial
\mathcal{G}\in\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(S;\mathcal{C})
. It follows that for any section
\sigma
of
f
,

\sigma^{\ast}\mathcal{F}\simeq\mathcal{G},

giving the conclusion.
∎

The following criterion will help recognize blobs and constant sheaves for our purposes.

Lemma 9.5
.

Let
p
be a prime number and
K
a second-countable compact Hausdorff space of finite (mod
p
) cohomological dimension. Suppose that
R\Gamma(K;\mathbb{F}_{p})=\mathbb{F}_{p}
.
Then
K
is a right
\operatorname{Sp}_{\widehat{p}}
-blob, i.e. the map
f:K\to\ast
is a right
\operatorname{Sp}_{\widehat{p}}
-blob.

Moreover, if we further suppose that
\Gamma(K;BG)=BG
for all finite groups
G
, then for
\mathcal{F}\in\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(K;\operatorname{Sp}_{\widehat{p}})
the following conditions are equivalent:

1.

\mathcal{F}
is constant;

2.

\mathcal{F}
is a colimit of dualizable objects.

Proof.

By Theorem
7.20
and Remark
9.3
,
K
will be a right
\operatorname{Sp}_{\widehat{p}}
-blob provided that
f^{\ast}:\operatorname{Sp}_{\widehat{p}}\to\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(K;\operatorname{Sp}_{\widehat{p}})
is fully faithful. Thus it suffices to show that if
A
is a
p
-torsion spectrum, then

A\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}R\Gamma(K;A).

(See also
4.20
for the comparison of etale sheaves and usual sheaves.) Note that the mod
p
cohomological dimension hypothesis passes to
p
-torsion abelian group sheaves by extensions and filtered colimits. Thus we can argue using the Postnikov tower to reduce to the case where
A
lives in degree
0
. Then another devissage reduces to
A=\mathbb{F}_{p}
which was the hypothesis.

Since every object in
\operatorname{Sp}_{\widehat{p}}
is a filtered colimit of dualizable objects (the compact generator
\mathbb{S}/p
is dualizable), by pullback we see that 1 implies 2. For the converse, we first claim that the collection of constant sheaves is closed under colimits. This follows because
\mathcal{F}
is constant if and only if the counit is an isomorphism:

f^{\ast}f_{\ast}\mathcal{F}\simeq\mathcal{F}.

Indeed, both
f^{\ast}
and
f_{\ast}
preserve colimits (the latter by the cohomological dimension estimate and a Postnikov tower argument). Thus it suffices to show that any dualizable
\mathcal{F}
is constant. Since dualizable objects are bounded below (
8.8
) we can use the counit criterion again to see that it suffices to show
\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}
is constant. For this, since pullbacks are t-exact it suffices to show that each
\pi_{i}(\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p})
is constant. But by Lemma
8.8
each of these represented by a finite etale map
X\to K
, hence is locally constant with finite dimensional stalks. From
H^{0}(K;\mathbb{F}_{p})=\mathbb{F}_{p}
we see that
K
is connected so the dimension of the stalks must be constant. Thus
\pi_{i}(\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p})
is classified by a map
K\to B\operatorname{GL}_{d}(\mathbb{F}_{p})
and hence is constant by assumption.
∎

Now we will describe our exotic “interval”. We start with the product space

(\mathbb{Z}\cup\{+\infty\})\times\mathbb{R},

equipped with the
\mathbb{Z}
-action where
k\in\mathbb{Z}
acts by

(n,t)\mapsto(n+k,t-k),

where of course we say
+\infty+k=+\infty
, as required by continuity. This is a free and properly discontinuous action, and we denote by

X:=\left((\mathbb{Z}\cup\{+\infty\})\times\mathbb{R}\right)/\mathbb{Z}

the quotient topological space. The open-closed decomposition

\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{R}\hookrightarrow(\mathbb{Z}\cup\{+\infty\})\times\mathbb{R}\hookleftarrow\{+\infty\}\times\mathbb{R}

is preserved by the
\mathbb{Z}
-action, and therefore descends to an open-closed decomposition of
X
. The closed part
\{+\infty\}\times\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
identifies with the circle
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
; call the inclusion
i:\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow X
. On the other hand, the open part
(\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{R})/\mathbb{Z}
identifies with
\mathbb{R}
via the map

j:\mathbb{R}\overset{t\mapsto(0,t)}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow(\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{R})/\mathbb{Z},

with inverse induced by
(n,t)\mapsto t-n
.

Remark 9.6
.

In terms of the set-theoretic decomposition
X=\mathbb{R}\sqcup\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
coming from this open-closed decomposition, we can describe the topology of
X
as follows:

1.

For
t\in\mathbb{R}
, a neighborhood base of
t\in X
is given by the sets
U_{\epsilon,t}=(t-\epsilon,t+\epsilon)\subset\mathbb{R}
for varying
\epsilon>0
.

2.

For
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
, let
\epsilon>0
and
T\in\mathbb{R}
. Define the set
U_{T,\epsilon,\alpha}\subset X
to consist of those points in
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
of
\epsilon
-distance to
\alpha
, together with those points in
\mathbb{R}
which are within
\epsilon
-distance of a coset representative
\widetilde{\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}
of
\alpha
satisfying
\widetilde{\alpha}>T
. Then these
U_{T,\epsilon,\alpha}
form a neighborhood basis of
\alpha
.

From this description we easily see that
X
is locally compact Hausdorff, and that a basis of open neighborhoods of
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
in
X
is given by the
(T,+\infty)\sqcup\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}\subset\mathbb{R}\sqcup\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}=X
for varying
T\in\mathbb{R}
.

Remark 9.7
.

We can give a more pictorial description of
X
as follows. consider the embedding of
\mathbb{R}
into
\mathbb{R}^{3}
via

t\mapsto(t,\operatorname{cos}(t),\operatorname{sin}(t)).

This gives a helix. Then
X
is the closure this helix inside the partial compactification
(\mathbb{R}\cup\{+\infty\})\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. In essence we add a boundary circle at
t=+\infty
. This identification is implemented by the map

(\mathbb{Z}\cup\{+\infty\})\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow(\mathbb{R}\cup\{+\infty\})\times\mathbb{R}^{2}

sending
(n,t)\mapsto(t+n,\operatorname{cos}(t),\operatorname{sin}(t))
for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
(+\infty,t)\mapsto(+\infty,\operatorname{cos}(t),\operatorname{sin}(t))
.

Of course, we could apply some increasing homeomorphism
\mathbb{R}\simeq(-\infty,0)
to the
t
-axis to make the boundary circle lie above the point
t=0
instead of
t=+\infty
and in this way get a closed embedding of
X
into
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, resembling the topologist’s sine curve, but with a limiting circle instead of a limiting line segment.

Now, let
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
. Consider the map
