(\mathbb{Z}\cup\{+\infty\})\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow(\mathbb{Z}\cup\{+\infty\})\times\mathbb{R}

given by multiplication by
N
on each factor, so
(n,t)\mapsto(N\cdot n,N\cdot t)
and
(+\infty,t)\mapsto(+\infty,N\cdot t)
. This passes through the quotients, giving an induced map

[N]:X\rightarrow X.

This map
[N]
respects the open-closed decomposition. On the open part it is the map
\cdot N:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
: in particular, a homeomorphism. On the closed part, it is the map
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
induced by
\cdot N
. This is the standard
N
-fold covering map of the circle.

For
N,N^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
we have
[N]\circ[N^{\prime}]=[N\cdot N^{\prime}]
, whence a functor

(\mathbb{N},|)^{op}\rightarrow\operatorname{Top}

from the opposite of the poset of natural numbers under divisibility to topological spaces, sending every
N
to
X
and where for
N|M
the induced map
X\rightarrow X
is given by
[M/N]
. Then we take the filtered limit of this diagram, getting

\widetilde{X}:=\varprojlim_{N}X.

This gets an open-closed decomposition inherited from
X

\mathbb{R}\overset{\widetilde{j}}{\hookrightarrow}\widetilde{X}\overset{\widetilde{i}}{\hookleftarrow}\varprojlim_{N}\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}

into the real line and a solenoid.

Now let’s get rid of the non-compactness of these spaces, which comes from the open part. Denote by
I\subset X

the closed subset which is gotten by removing
(-\infty,0)
from the open locus
j:\mathbb{R}\hookrightarrow X
. We have
[N]^{-1}(I)=I
for
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, and in the inverse limit we get a closed subset
\widetilde{I}\subset\widetilde{X}
, again just obtained from the open locus
\mathbb{R}
by removing
(-\infty,0)
.

Lemma 9.8
.

Let
K
denote either
I
or
\widetilde{I}
. Then
K
is second countable compact Hausdorff and has covering dimension 1.

Proof.

We have already seen that
X
, hence
\widetilde{X}
, is Hausdorff. Second countability is clear from Remark
9.7
. To show that
\widetilde{I}
is compact, because
\widetilde{I}
is an inverse limit of copies of
I
it suffices to see that
I
is. However the composition

(\mathbb{N}\cup\{+\infty\})\times[0,1]\subset(\mathbb{Z}\cup\{+\infty\})\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow X

has image
I
, so this follows from compactness of
\mathbb{N}\cup\{+\infty\}
and
[0,1]
. For the covering dimension assertion, clearly our space does not have covering dimension
0
, so it suffices to bound the covering dimension by 1. Again because
\widetilde{I}
is a filtered inverse limit of copies of
I
, to see that
\widetilde{I}
has covering dimension
\leq 1
it suffices to see the same for
I
. For that we use the neighborhood bases from Remark
9.7
(say with
\epsilon<1/2
for clarity). The boundary of the neighborhood of a point
t\in[0,\infty)
is either 2 points if
t\neq 0
or 1 point if
t=0
, and the boundary of the neighborhood of a point
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
is a disjoint union of two copies of
\mathbb{N}\cup\{+\infty\}
. In all cases the boundary is 0-dimensional, so our space has inductive dimension
1
, hence covering dimension
\leq 1
as desired.
∎

Lemma 9.9
.

Let
K
denote either
I
or
\widetilde{I}
, let
i:Z\subset X
denote the closed subset from the open-closed decomposition, and let
j:U\subset X
denote the open complement. (So for
K=I
we have
U=[0,+\infty)
and
Z=\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
, while for
K=\widetilde{I}
we have
U=[0,+\infty)
and
Z=\varprojlim_{N}\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
.)

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a sheaf of anima on
K
such that
j^{\ast}\mathcal{F}
is a constant sheaf on
U
. Then

\Gamma(K,\mathcal{F})\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\Gamma(Z;i^{\ast}\mathcal{F}).

Proof.

Since
K
is paracompact Hausdorff, by
[
Lur09
]
7.1.5.6 we have

\Gamma(Z;i^{\ast}\mathcal{F})=\varinjlim_{V}\Gamma(V;\mathcal{F})

where
V
runs over open neighborhoods of
Z
in
K
. Thus we get

\Gamma(X;\mathcal{F})\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\Gamma(Z;i^{\ast}\mathcal{F})\times_{\varinjlim_{V}\Gamma(V\cap U;\mathcal{F})}\Gamma(U;\mathcal{F}).

It follows that a sufficient condition for
\Gamma(K;\mathcal{F})\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\Gamma(Z;i^{\ast}\mathcal{F})
is that
Z
should have a neighborhood basis of open subsets
V
such that
\Gamma(U;\mathcal{F})\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\Gamma(U\cap V;\mathcal{F})
. In our setting, such a neighborhood basis is given, in as in Remark
9.7
, by the
(T,\infty)\sqcup Z
for
T>0
. As both
[0,\infty)
and
(T,\infty)
are contractible, they have the same sections for a constant sheaf, whence the claim.
∎

Remark 9.10
.

In my original approach to the main result in this section (Theorem
9.13
), I was using the topos
\mathcal{T}=\operatorname{Sh}(\mathbb{Z}\cup\{+\infty\})^{\mathbb{Z}}
and its inverse limit
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
instead of these topological spaces
X
and
\widetilde{X}
. The relationship between the two is that
\mathcal{T}
is the category of constructible sheaves on
X
(or
I
) with respect to the stratification with 2 strata given by the open-closed decomposition.

In the context of
\mathcal{T}
, Lemma
9.9
was pointed out to me by Lucas Mann. Using it allowed to greatly simplify my original construction of the comparison isomorphism between the stalks at
0
and
1
from Theorem
9.13
. The idea to switch from the topos
\mathcal{T}
to the corresponding topological space
X
was suggested to me by Shachar Carmeli. It makes the presentation more accessible. I thank them both for their very helpful remarks.

The following attempts to express the extent to which
\widetilde{I}
is “contractible” from the perspective of sheaf theory.

Lemma 9.11
.

Let
A\in\operatorname{An}
. Suppose that for each
a\in A
and each
i\geq 1
, the homotopy group
\pi_{i}(A,a)
is torsion (i.e. every element has finite order). Then
\Gamma(\widetilde{I};A)=A
.

Proof.

By Lemma
9.9
, we reduce to the analogous claim for the solenoid
S=\varprojlim_{N}\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
. The functor
A\mapsto\Gamma(S;A)
preserves finite limits, as with any topological space. Because
S
is paracompact Hausdorff of finite covering dimension, it also preserves limits of Postnikov towers (
[
Lur09
]
7.2). Thus, by the usual induction with the Postnikov tower, it suffices to show:

1.

For any set
A
we have
\Gamma(S;A)=A
.

2.

For any torsion group
G
, every
G
-torsor on
S
is trivial.

3.

For any torsion abelian group
A
we have
H^{i}(S;A)=0
for
i>1
(the case
i=1
being handled by 2).

Point 1 is the same as the connectedness of the solenoid, which follows from the connectedness of the circle. For point 2, by compactness a
G
-torsor on
S
descends to the
N^{th}
stage
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
for some
N
. There, by contractibility of
\mathbb{R}
, it is induced by a homomorphism
\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow G
. If
G
is torsion then this homomorphism becomes trivial on composing with
\cdot M:\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
for some
M\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, whence triviality of our descended
G
-torsor on pullback to the
(N\cdot M)^{th}
stage, whence triviality of our original
G
-torsor on the solenoid. Finally, 3 is true for any sheaf of abelian groups by the fact that
S
has covering dimension 1.
∎

Remark 9.12
.

In particular, the previous results imply that the hypotheses of Lemma
9.5
are satisfied. Thus
\widetilde{I}
is a
\operatorname{Sp}_{\widehat{p}}
-blob and an object
\mathcal{F}\in\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(\widetilde{I};\operatorname{Sp}_{\widehat{p}})
is constant if and only if it is a colimit of dualizable objects.

Now we describe the map of light condensed anima
\gamma:\widetilde{I}\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}
which we use to “connect” the points
0
and
1
. More generally, we will give such a map for any local field
F
and any choice of
q\in F
with
0<|q|<1
. (For
F=\mathbb{Q}_{p}
we can of course choose
q=p
).

For this, we take the composition

\widetilde{I}\to I\to\mathbb{Z}\cup\{+\infty\}/\mathbb{Z}\to F/F^{\times},

where:

1.

The first map is the natural projection to the
1^{st}
term in the inverse limit defining
\widetilde{I}
;

2.

The second map is projection off
\mathbb{R}
in terms of the definition
I=((\mathbb{Z}\cup\{+\infty\})\times\mathbb{R})/\mathbb{Z}
;

3.

The third map is induced by
n\mapsto q^{n}
.

Note that the points
0,1\in F
lift along
\gamma
to points
i_{0},i_{1}\in\widetilde{I}
. For example we can take
i_{0}
to be the identity element of the solenoid closed subspace of
\widetilde{I}
and we can take
i_{1}
to be
0
inside the
[0,+\infty)
open subspace of
\widetilde{I}
.

We get the following, which is our main result in this section. Write
0,1:\ast\to F
for the specified points of
F
(or their composition to
F/F^{\times}
).

Theorem 9.13
.

Let
F
be a local field and
p
a prime. Suppose given
X\in\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}
and
\mathcal{F}\in\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(X\times(F/F^{\times});\operatorname{Sp}_{\widehat{p}})
satisfying the following condition: for all
x:\ast\rightarrow X
, the sheaf
x^{\ast}\mathcal{F}\in\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(F/F^{\times};\operatorname{Sp}_{\widehat{p}})
is a colimit of dualizable objects. (We will only apply this when
\mathcal{F}
itself is invertible.)

Then there is a natural isomorphism
0^{\ast}\mathcal{F}\simeq 1^{\ast}\mathcal{F}\in\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(X;\operatorname{Sp}_{\widehat{p}})
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{G}=\gamma^{\ast}\mathcal{F}\in\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(X\times\widetilde{I};\operatorname{Sp}_{\widehat{p}})
. By Remark
9.12
,
\widetilde{I}
is a right
\operatorname{Sp}_{\widehat{p}}
-blob and
\mathcal{G}
lies in the essential image of the pullback functor
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(X;\operatorname{Sp}_{\widehat{p}})\to\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(X\times\widetilde{I};\operatorname{Sp}_{\widehat{p}})
. Thus by Theorem
9.4
we have
i_{0}^{\ast}\mathcal{G}\simeq i_{1}^{\ast}\mathcal{G}
, which gives the claim.
∎

This was valid for all complete valued fields
F
, but of course if
F
is archimedean (i.e.
F=\mathbb{R}
or
F\simeq\mathbb{C}
), then we can do better, because already
F
itself is a left
\mathcal{C}
-blob for all
\mathcal{C}
(see
[
Lur17
]
A.2); we don’t even need the
F^{\times}
-equivariance. Thus the same arguments as above give the following analog of the previous theorem.

Theorem 9.14
.

Let
F
be an
archimedean
complete normed field, let
X\in\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}
, and let
\mathcal{F}\in\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(X\times F;\operatorname{Sp})
. Suppose that for all
x:\ast\rightarrow X
, the sheaf
x^{\ast}\mathcal{F}\in\operatorname{Sh}(F;\operatorname{Sp})
is is locally constant. Then there is a natural isomorphism
0^{\ast}\mathcal{F}\simeq 1^{\ast}\mathcal{F}\in\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(X;\operatorname{Sp})
.

10
Duality and linearization for real and p-adic Lie groups

Recall from Theorem
8.6
that for a submersion
f:X\to Y
in
\operatorname{Sh}(\operatorname{Man}_{\mathbb{R}})
, the map
f:\underline{X}\to\underline{Y}
of light condensed anima is
\operatorname{Sp}
-smooth. In particular we have a dualizing object
f^{!}\mathbb{S}\in\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(\underline{X};\operatorname{Sp})
. Now we want to identify this in terms of the relative tangent bundle
T_{X/Y}
, as in Atiyah duality. First let’s explain what construction associated to the tangent bundle we’re aiming for. It is defined for an arbitrary vector bundle. We start with a description in terms of the six functor formalism.

Definition 10.1
.

Let
X\in\operatorname{Sh}(\operatorname{Man}_{\mathbb{R}})
and let
f:V\to X
be a vector bundle on
X
, with zero section
e:X\to V
. Define

\mathbb{S}^{V}=e^{\ast}f^{!}\mathbb{S}\in\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(\underline{X};\operatorname{Sp}).

By smoothness of
f
, this is an invertible object. It follows formally that it is locally constant in the sense of
5.8
(the proof of smoothness of
f
also shows this directly). Thus it lifts to
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(|X|;\operatorname{Sp})=\operatorname{An}_{/|X|}\otimes\operatorname{Sp}
by
5.8
: in other words it comes from a local system of spectra on the anima
|X|
underlying
X
. Let us now prove this in a different, more informative way, by explicitly describing a lift of
\mathbb{S}^{V}
to
\operatorname{An}_{/|X|}
.

Lemma 10.2
.

Let
f_{\natural}
denote the left adjoint to
f^{\ast}
(it exists and commutes with base change by smoothness). Then there is a natural isomorphism (compatible with base change)

\mathbb{S}^{V}\simeq f_{\natural}e_{\ast}\mathbb{S}.

Proof.

We have
f_{\natural}(-)=f_{!}(-\otimes f^{!}\mathbb{S})
because
f^{!}(-)\simeq f^{\ast}(-)\otimes f^{!}\mathbb{S}
. Thus

f_{\natural}e_{\ast}\mathbb{S}=f_{!}(f^{!}\mathbb{S}\otimes e_{\ast}\mathbb{S})=f_{!}(e_{\ast}(e^{\ast}f^{!}\mathbb{S}))

by the projection formula for the closed inclusion
e
. However
e_{\ast}=e_{!}
again because it’s a closed inclusion. The claim follows.
∎

To move forward, we consider the open complement
j:V\setminus 0\to V
to
e
.

Lemma 10.3
.

There is a natural isomorphism

\mathbb{S}^{V}\simeq\operatorname{cofib}((p\circ j)_{\natural}\mathbb{S}\to p_{\natural}\mathbb{S}).

Proof.

We have a cofiber sequence

j_{\natural}\mathbb{S}\to\mathbb{S}\to e_{\ast}\mathbb{S}

by Lemma
7.26
. The result follows by applying
p_{\natural}
.
∎

Now that we’ve translated everything in terms of the left adjoint to pullback, we can move to the homotopy theory context using the following.

Lemma 10.4
.

Let
f:Y\to X
be a map in
\operatorname{Sh}(\operatorname{Man}_{\mathbb{R}})
. Suppose
f
is a
fiber bundle
in the sense that, locally on
X
, it is isomorphic to a projection map
U\times F\to U
for some
U\in\operatorname{Man}_{\mathbb{R}}
and
F\in\operatorname{Sh}(\operatorname{Man}_{\mathbb{R}})
. Then:

1.

the left adjoint
f_{\natural}
to
f^{\ast}:\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(X;\operatorname{An})\to\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(Y;\operatorname{An})
exists and commutes with all base-change;

2.

this left adjoint
f_{\natural}
sends locally constant sheaves to locally constant sheaves (
5.8
), and hence a fortiori restricts to the left adjoint to the pullback
\operatorname{An}_{/|X|}\to\operatorname{An}_{/|Y|}
via the comparison
5.8
.

Proof.

For 1, note that such claims can be checked locally by descent, so it suffices to handle the case of
f:F\to\ast
for some
F\in\operatorname{Sh}(\operatorname{Man}_{F})
. Resolving
F
by open polydisks we further reduce to open polydisks, and then to the unit interval. In that case the claim is elementary, see
[
Lur17
]
A.2.

Again for 2, we reduce to the case of a projection which, by commutation with base-change, reduces to
f:F\to\ast
, where the claim is trivial.
∎

In total we deduce the following:

Proposition 10.5
.

Let
X\in\operatorname{Sh}(\operatorname{Man}_{F})
and let
V\to X
be a vector bundle. Write
V\setminus 0
for the complement of the zero section. View
|V\setminus 0|
and
|V|
as objects in
\operatorname{An}_{/|X|}
, and write
\Sigma^{\infty}
for the suspension spectrum functor from pointed objects in
\operatorname{An}_{/|X|}
to its stabilization. Then

\mathbb{S}^{V}\simeq\Sigma^{\infty}(|V|/|V\setminus 0|)

Proof.

This follows by combining the two previous lemmas, noting that
V\to X
and
V\setminus 0\to X
are both fiber bundles.
∎

Now that we’ve discussed
\mathbb{S}^{V}
, we can give the proof of the Atiyah duality statement. But first we isolate a very simple lemma which we will use repeatedly.

Lemma 10.6
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a smooth map (with respect to some six functor formalism). Then we have

f^{!}1\simeq\delta^{\ast}\pi^{!}1,

where
\delta:X\to X\times_{Y}X
is the diagonal and
\pi:X\times_{Y}X\to X
is the first projection.

Proof.

By base change for upper shriek in the pullback
X\times_{Y}X
, we have
(\pi_{2})^{\ast}f^{!}1\simeq\pi^{!}1
where
\pi_{2}
is the second projection. Applying
\delta^{\ast}
we deduce the claim.
∎

Thus dualizing objects can be simply recovered from what should just be a specialization of them, namely their pullback along a section, as long as we allow ourselves to work in families and change the base. Of course this is a general principle:
X
is the “moduli space of points in
X
”, and the universal section proving this is the diagonal section of the first projection. But combined with the deformation to the tangent space, it gives a very useful technique for calculating dualizing objects, as in the next proof.

Theorem 10.7
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a representable submersion in
\operatorname{Sh}(\operatorname{Man}_{\mathbb{R}})
. Write
T_{f}
for the relative tangent bundle. Then there is a natural isomorphism

f^{!}\mathbb{S}\simeq\mathbb{S}^{T_{f}}

in
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(\underline{X};\operatorname{Sp})
.

Proof.

By the lemma,
f^{!}\mathbb{S}\simeq\delta^{\ast}\pi^{!}1
. Now we consider the deformation to the tangent bundle from
3.19
, which we can extend to representable submersions by descent. This gives a representable submersion

\Pi:D(X/Y)\rightarrow X\times\mathbb{R}

equipped with a section
\Sigma
, such that over
1\in\mathbb{R}
we recover
\pi
with its section
\delta
, while over
0\in\mathbb{R}
we recover
T_{f}\to X
together with its zero section. Because
\Pi
is a representable submersion, by Theorem
8.6
we have that
\Pi^{!}\mathbb{S}
is invertible (and indeed locally constant), and hence so is
\Sigma^{\ast}\Pi^{!}\mathbb{S}
. Therefore by Theorem
9.13
we get a canonical isomorphism

1^{\ast}\Sigma^{\ast}\Pi^{!}\mathbb{S}\simeq 0^{\ast}\Sigma^{\ast}\Pi^{!}\mathbb{S}.

By base-change (which for upper
!
functors is a part of smoothness), this gives the desired isomorphism

\delta^{\ast}\pi^{\ast}\mathbb{S}\simeq\mathbb{S}^{T_{f}}.

∎

Now let’s discuss classifying stacks of Lie groups. Recall from Corollary
8.7
that these are also smooth. The same argument as above gives the following.

Lemma 10.8
.

Let
X\in\operatorname{Sh}(\operatorname{Man}_{\mathbb{R}})
, and let
G\to X
be a group object in representable submersions over
X
. Write
f:BG\to X
for the relative classifying stack, and
e:X\to BG
for the canonical quotient map. Let also
\mathfrak{g}\to X
denote the Lie algebra of
G
, viewed as a vector bundle on
X
.

Then there is a natural isomorphism

e^{\ast}f^{!}\mathbb{S}\simeq\mathbb{S}^{-\mathfrak{g}},

where we write
\mathbb{S}^{-\mathfrak{g}}
for the inverse of
\mathbb{S}^{\mathfrak{g}}
.

Proof.

We deform
G
to its Lie algebra (
3.21
), giving the group object in representable submersions

DG\to X\times\mathbb{R}.

Pass to relative classifying stacks. Applying Theorem
9.13
this gives

e^{\ast}f^{!}\mathbb{S}\simeq(e^{\prime})^{\ast}(f^{\prime})^{!}\mathbb{S}

where we write
f^{\prime}:B\mathfrak{g}\to X
and
e^{\prime}:X\to B\mathfrak{g}
for the maps analogous to
f
and
e
but applied to the additive Lie group
\mathfrak{g}
. Thus it suffices to show for an arbitrary vector bundle
V
over
X
that upper shriek followed by upper star in

X\to BV\to X

gives
\mathbb{S}^{-V}
. But upper shriek followed by upper shriek obviously gives
\mathbb{S}
, so by projection formula for upper shriek it suffices to see that upper shriek of
\mathbb{S}
along
X\to BV
gives
\mathbb{S}^{V}
. This follows by Lemma
10.6
.
∎

Now by combining with another general principle we deduce the following.

Theorem 10.9
.

Let
G
be a real Lie group. Then for the projection
f:BG\to\ast
(which we recall is smooth by Corollary
8.7
), we have

f^{!}\mathbb{S}\simeq\mathbb{S}^{-\operatorname{ad}_{G}}

in
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(BG;\operatorname{Sp})
, where
\operatorname{ad}_{G}
denotes the adjoint representation of
G
, viewed as a vector bundle on
\ast/G
.

Proof.

By Lemma
10.6
,
f^{!}\mathbb{S}
identifies with upper shriek followed by upper star in

BG\to BG\times BG\to BG

where the first map is the diagonal
\delta
and the second map is the first projection
\pi
. On the other hand this diagram is isomorphic to
f
and
e
of the previous lemma in the following situation: take
G
with its adjoint action and view it as a group
G^{ad}
over
BG
. We conclude by applying the previous lemma.
∎

Remark 10.10
.

We used an identification of
BG\times BG
with the relative classifying stack (over
BG
) associated to the adjoint action of
G
on itself. This may seem opaque. We offer the following perspective. For any groupoid
X
, there is a canonical functor

\operatorname{Ad}_{X}:X\to\operatorname{Gp}

to the category of groups. It sends a point
x\in X
to the group
\operatorname{Aut}(x)
, and a map
\gamma:x\to y
to the isomorphism
\operatorname{Aut}(x)\to\operatorname{Aut}(y)
given by conjugation by
\gamma
. The composition with the functor from groups to groupoids given by taking the classifying space has a different description: it sends
x\in X
to the component of
X
in which
x
lives. The identification is gotten by the obvious inlusion functor
B\operatorname{Aut}(x)\rightarrow X
based at
x
.

In particular, if
X
is connected this composed functor to groupoids is constant with value
X
. On the other hand if
X
is also pointed, hence identifies with
BG
for a group
G
, then
\operatorname{Ad}_{X}
by construction encodes the adjoint action of
G
on itself.

Taking Grothendieck constructions, this provides the desired canonical identification. It is functorial in
G
and therefore passes to topoi.

Example 10.11
.

Suppose that
G
is a real Lie group which is
compact
. Then the diagonal of
p:BG\to\ast
is
\operatorname{Sp}
-proper (since it is a locally isomorphic to projection off
G
). Thus by Definition
7.12
we get a natural transformation

p_{!}\to p_{\ast}.

On the other hand, passing to left adjoints in
f^{!}\simeq f^{\ast}\otimes f^{!}\mathbb{S}
and using Theorem
10.9
shows that

p_{!}(-)\simeq p_{\natural}(-\otimes\mathbb{S}^{\operatorname{ad}_{G}}).

Thus we obtain a natural transformation

p_{\natural}(-\otimes\mathbb{S}^{\operatorname{ad}_{G}})\to p_{\ast}(-)

of functors
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(BG;\operatorname{Sp})\to\operatorname{Sp}
, relating the left and right adjoints to the pullback. But recall from
5.8
that
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(BG;\operatorname{Sp})=\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(B|G|;\operatorname{Sp})
identifies with the
\infty
-category of spectra with
|G|
-action. In these terms
p_{\natural}
stands for the
|G|
-orbit functor and
p_{\ast}
for the
|G|
-fixed point functor. Thus we recover the twisted transfer maps of
[
ACD89
]
.

Note that the comparison of the two a priori different maps can be made by seeing they both satisfy the conditions of
[
NS18
]
Thm. I.4.1. For the map constructed above, we can argue for this as follows: because
e:\ast\to\ast/G
is proper we have that
p_{!}\to p_{\ast}
is an isomorphism on any
e_{\ast}X
, but by duality for
e
(a proper representable submersion) this is the same as the
e_{\natural}X[d]
, hence one sees that
p_{!}\to p_{\ast}
is an iso on compact objects and hence
p_{!}
is the left Kan extension of the restriction of
p_{\ast}
to the compact objects.

Now we discuss the
p
-adic case. It’s pretty similar, but one difference is that we can’t define
\mathbb{S}^{V}
exactly as in the real case, because
\mathbb{Q}_{p}\to\ast
is not smooth. This problem goes away when we pass to classifying stacks, though.

Definition 10.12
.

Let
X\in\operatorname{Sh}(\operatorname{Man}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}})
, and let
V\to X
be a vector bundle on
X
. Define

\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}^{V}=e^{\ast}f^{!}\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}},

where
X\overset{e}{\rightarrow}BV\overset{f}{\rightarrow}X
are the quotient and projection maps associated to the relative classifying stack
BV
.

Note that
BV\to X
is indeed (
!
-able and) smooth: the universal case is
X=B\operatorname{GL}_{d}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
which is
!
-good, so this follows from Theorem
8.12
and closure under pullbacks of
!
-able smooth maps. Thus, as in the real case,
\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}^{V}
is invertible. Moreover its
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-homology lives only in degree
d=\operatorname{dim}(V)
, again just as in the real case (see the proof of Theorem
8.12
).

Proceeding exactly as in the real case we get the following.

Lemma 10.13
.

Let
X
be a
!
-good
p
-adic analytic smooth Artin stack (
8.10
), and let
G\to X
be a group object in representable submersions over
X
. Write
f:BG\to X
for the relative classifying stack, and
e:X\to BG
for the canonical quotient map. Let also
\mathfrak{g}\to X
denote the Lie algebra of
G
, viewed as a vector bundle on
X
.

Then there is a natural isomorphism

e^{\ast}f^{!}\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}\simeq\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}^{\mathfrak{g}}.

Proof.

We once again apply the deformation to the tangent bundle, specialized along the identity section, see
3.21
. This gives a relative Lie group

DG\to X\times(F/F^{\times})

which over
1
recovers
G
and over
0
recovers
\mathfrak{g}
with its additive Lie group structure. Pass to the relative classifying stack of
DG
, call it
BDG
. Note that every stack which appears is still
!
-good,
8.11
. Then by Theorem
9.13
and base-change we deduce the claim.
∎

Now we can deduce the main result of this article.

Theorem 10.14
.

Let
G
be a
p
-adic Lie group. Then for
f:BG\to\ast
, we have

f^{!}\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}\simeq\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}^{\operatorname{ad}_{G}}

where
\operatorname{ad}_{G}
denotes the adjoint representation of
G
, viewed as a vector bundle on
BG
.

Proof.

By base-change,
f^{!}\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}
identifies with the shriek pullback followed by star pullback in

BG\to BG\times BG\to BG

which in turn identifies with the
e
and
f
from Lemma
10.13
applied to the group over
BG
corresponding to the adjoint action of
G
. Thus Lemma
10.13
gives the conclusion.
∎

Example 10.15
.

Suppose furthermore that
G
is compact and
p
-torsionfree. Then
f_{!}\simeq f_{\ast}
by Theorem
8.12
. Thus we deduce that

f_{\natural}(-)\simeq f_{\ast}(-\otimes\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}^{\operatorname{ad}_{G}}).

This means that homology and cohomology on
BG
with
p
-complete spectrum coefficients differ by this explicit twist.

Given this result, one would like to get some handle on the construction
V\mapsto\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}^{V}
from Definition
10.12
. If we change coefficients from
\operatorname{Sp}_{\widehat{p}}
to
\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Z})_{\widehat{p}}
, a very explicit description is possible. This also shows that our duality does recover Lazard’s duality with the same explicit twist.

Proposition 10.16
.

Let
V
be a vector bundle over an
X\in\operatorname{Sh}(\operatorname{Man}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}})
, say of constant dimension
d
. Write
\Lambda^{d}V
for the top exterior power of
V
. This is a one-dimensional vector bundle, hence is classified by a map

X\to B\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}

in
\operatorname{Sh}(\operatorname{Man}_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}})
. Composing with the homomorphism

\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}\to\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}

which is the identity on
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
and sends
p\mapsto 1
, we deduce that
\Lambda^{d}V
admits a canonical
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-structure; denote this by
(\Lambda^{d}V)_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
. Writing this as the limit of its mod
p^{n}
versions, which are finite etale, we can view
(\Lambda^{d}V)_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}
as an object in
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(X;\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Z})_{\widehat{p}})
. Then we have

(\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}^{V}\otimes\mathbb{Z})_{\widehat{p}}\simeq(\Lambda^{d}V)_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}[d].

Proof.

The universal case is
X=B\operatorname{GL}_{d}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
. We know that
(\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}^{V}\otimes\mathbb{Z})_{\widehat{p}}
is invertible and lives in degree
d
, so its
(-d)
-shift is classified by a map of light condensed anima

B\operatorname{GL}_{d}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\to B\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times},

or in other words a homomorphism
\operatorname{GL}_{d}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\to\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
. We need to see that this identifies with the determinant map followed by the homomorphism
\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}\to\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
considered in the statement. Because the abelianization of
\operatorname{GL}_{d}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
is realized by the determinant, we can restrict along
\operatorname{GL}_{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\to\operatorname{GL}_{d}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
and thereby reduce to the case
d=1
.

Thus we’re left with showing the following: consider the trivial one-dimensional vector bundle
\mathbb{Q}_{p}\to\ast
over the point, and use this to form

(\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}^{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}\otimes\mathbb{Z})_{\widehat{p}}[-1].

By functoriality we have an action of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}
on this free
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-module of rank
1
, induced by the action by scalar multiplication on the group object
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
. We need to show that
p
acts trivially and
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
acts by scalar multiplication.

To see that
p
acts trivially, note
p\in\mathbb{Z}[1/p]^{\times}
, so by the reciprocity explained in the next section (Section
11
), we can reduce to showing the analogous result for the the action of
p\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}
on
\mathbb{S}^{\mathbb{R}}
. But then it follows because
p
lies in the same connected component of
\mathbb{R}^{\times}
as
1
.

To see that
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
acts by scalar multiplication, we can pass from
B\mathbb{Q}_{p}
to
B\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, using that
B\mathbb{Z}_{p}\to B\mathbb{Q}_{p}
is etale. For clarity of notation let’s replace
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
by an arbitrary free
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-module of rank one
L
. We’re trying to show that the twist for
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-coefficients of
BL
identifies with
L[1]
.

This twist is
\mathbb{D}_{L}:=e^{\ast}f^{!}\mathbb{Z}_{p}
in the diagram

\ast\to BL\to\ast.

But since
L
is abelian,
BL
gets a group structure, so
f^{!}\mathbb{Z}_{p}
identifies with the constant sheaf on our twist, say
f^{!}\mathbb{Z}_{p}=f^{\ast}\mathbb{D}_{L}
. Adjunction gives a natural map

f_{!}f^{!}\mathbb{Z}_{p}\to\mathbb{Z}_{p}

which is split surjective (by considering
e
). On the other hand by properness of
f
and the above we can rewrite this split surjective map as

(f_{\ast}\mathbb{Z}_{p})\otimes\mathbb{D}_{L}\to\mathbb{Z}_{p}.

Looking in degree
0
we deduce that
\operatorname{Hom}(L,\mathbb{Z}_{p})[-1]\otimes\mathbb{D}_{L}\simeq\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, so that
\mathbb{D}_{L}\simeq L[1]
as desired.

∎

Remark 10.17
.

This is analogous to the following well-known Archimedean fact: if
V
is a vector bundle of dimension
d
over
X\in\operatorname{Sh}(\operatorname{Man}_{\mathbb{R}})
, then
\mathbb{S}^{V}\otimes\mathbb{Z}
can be described as follows: classify the top exterior power
\Lambda^{d}V
by a map

X\to B\mathbb{R}^{\times},

then project to
B(\pm 1)=B\mathbb{Z}^{\times}
to deduce a local system of free
\mathbb{Z}
-modules of rank
1
on
X
. Shifting up by
d
, this identifies with
\mathbb{S}^{V}\otimes\mathbb{Z}
. In the case of the tangent bundle of a manifold, this is of course just the usual description of the “orientation” local system.

Remark 10.18
.

We will get more information about this operation
V\mapsto\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}^{V}
in the next section.

11
J-homomorphisms from six functor formalisms

In the previous section, we recorded the “twists” relevant for Poincaré duality using the following construction: given a smooth map
f:X\to Y
equipped with a section
s:Y\to X
, form

s^{\ast}f^{!}1,

which is an invertible object in the category which is value of our six functor formalism on
Y
. We applied this in particular when
f:X\to Y
is a vector bundle and
s
its zero section, or when
f:X\to Y
is the relative classifying stack of a vector bundle and
s
is the natural quotient map.

In this section we would like to encode relationships between these twists for different
X\to Y
, in the general abstract context of a six functor formalism in the sense of
[
HM24
]
. We will keep
Y
fixed, so passing to the slice category we may as well assume
Y=\ast
. Three basic examples of such relationships we want to encode are the following:

1.

If
V\to\ast
is a finite dimensional real vector space, then the twist for
V
(with its zero section) identifies with the inverse of the twist for
BV
.

2.

If
V\to W\to W/V
is a short exact sequence of vector bundles, then the twist for
W
is the tensor product of the twists for
V
and
W/V
.

3.

If
f:X\to Y
is etale, then the twist for
f
is canonically trivial.

There are also more subtle relationships between the real and
p
-adic situations, which we will explore, but in the end they are of similar flavor to the above: they are all in some way “K-theoretic” in nature.

However, to express these relationships in a simple way it pays to change the setting a bit. Once we are in a “linear” context like vector bundles, it happens surprisingly often that the smooth map
f:X\to\ast
in question satisfies the property that
f^{\ast}
is fully faithful. For example this holds for real vector bundles (as is well-known), but also for relative classifying stacks of
p
-adic vector bundles (as we will check later). Then if we pass to the classifying stack one more time, we get an even simpler situation: the pullback
f^{\ast}
is not just fully faithful, but also an equivalence. By smoothness it then follows that
f^{!}
is also an equivalence, and therefore so is its adjoint
f_{!}
. Then the twist is, up to passing to the inverse (which is a matter of sign convention), directly encoded by the six functor formalism: it is the image of the span

\ast\overset{f}{\leftarrow}X\overset{f}{\rightarrow}\ast

under the lax symmetric monoidal functor which “is” our six functor formalism.

This leads to the following definition.

Definition 11.1
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},E,M)
be a six functor formalism in the sense of
[
HM24
]
(see Section
7
). Say that a map
f:X\to Y
in
\mathcal{C}
is
vectorial
if it lies in
E
, and for every base-change
f^{\prime}
of
f
, the functors
(f^{\prime})^{\ast}
and
(f^{\prime})_{!}
are equivalences.

Say that an object
X\in\mathcal{C}
is
vectorial
if the map
f:X\to\ast
is vectorial.

Lemma 11.2
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},E,M)
be a six functor formalism. Then:

1.

The class of vectorial maps in
\mathcal{C}
contains all isomorphisms and is closed under composition and base-change.

2.

Given composable maps
f
and
g
with composition
g\circ f
, if
f
is vectorial and
g\circ f
is vectorial, then
g
is vectorial.

3.

If
f:X\to S
and
g:Y\to S
are vectorial, then for any map
h:X\to Y
with
g\circ h=f
, the map
h
lies in
E
and
h^{\ast}
and
h_{!}
are both equivalences (but
h
need not be vectorial).

Proof.

Part 1 is clear from the definition. Parts 2 and 3 follow from the 2-of-3 property for isomorphisms. To see that
h
in 3 need not be vectorial, consider
\ast\to B\mathbb{R}\to\ast
in light condensed anima. The map
\ast\to B\mathbb{R}
is not vectorial because it has
\mathbb{R}\to\ast
as a base-change.
∎

Remark 11.3
.

Given a six functor formalism
(\mathcal{C},E,M)
such that
\mathcal{C}
has finite limits and any object
S\in\mathcal{C}
, we can restrict to the slice
\mathcal{C}_{/S}
to get a new six functor formalism. A map
f:X\to S
in
\mathcal{C}
is vectorial as a map in
\mathcal{C}
if and only if it is vectorial as an object in
\mathcal{C}_{/S}
. Thus we can often reduce discussion of vectorial maps to vectorial objects.

Remark 11.4
.

Every vectorial map
f:X\to Y
is smooth. Indeed since
f_{!}
is an equivalence (also after any base-change), the required properties of the right adjoint
f^{!}
follow from those of
f_{!}
.

Definition 11.5
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},E,M)
be a six functor formalism. For a vectorial object
X
, we define the twist
J(X)\in M(\ast)
by

J(X)=f_{!}f^{\ast}1

where
f:X\to\ast
.

In other words,
J(X)
is the “compactly supported cohomology of
X
”. Note that
J(X)
is an invertible object, because
f_{!}
and
f^{\ast}
are
M(\ast)
-linear isomorphisms and thus so is their composition
f_{!}f^{\ast}:M(\ast)\to M(\ast)
.

The relation of this
J(X)
with the construction of twists in the previous section is as follows.

Lemma 11.6
.

Suppose
X
is vectorial, and that we are given a section
s:\ast\to X
of
f:X\to\ast
. Then there is a natural isomorphism

J(X)\simeq(s^{\ast}f^{!}1)^{-1}

of invertible objects in
M(\ast)
.

Moreover, if we further assume
\ast\to X
is smooth and we set
X^{\prime}:=\ast\times_{X}\ast
with its natural projection
f^{\prime}:X^{\prime}\to\ast
and diagonal section
s^{\prime}:\ast\to X^{\prime}
, we have

J(X)\simeq(s^{\prime})^{\ast}(f^{\prime})^{!}1.

Proof.

Both
s^{\ast}
and the left adjoint
f_{\natural}
of
f^{\ast}
are inverse to the equivalence
f^{\ast}
, hence
s^{\ast}\simeq f_{\natural}
. Thus

s^{\ast}f^{!}1\simeq f_{\natural}f^{!}1.

But passing to right adjoints we see that
f_{\natural}f^{!}
is inverse to
f_{!}f^{\ast}
, whence the first claim. For the second claim, we certainly have
(s^{\prime})^{!}(f^{\prime})^{!}1=1
, but then we can apply the projection formula for
(s^{\prime})^{!}
and base-change of
\ast\to X
along itself to conclude.
∎

From the definition it is mercifully straightforward to encode all the multiplicativity properties of
X\mapsto J(X)
. For this we make the following definition, following
[
Qui72
]
.

Definition 11.7
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},E,M)
be a six functor formalism.

1.

Define
\operatorname{Vect}_{M}\subset\mathcal{C}
to be the full subcategory of
\mathcal{C}
spanned by the vectorial objects.

2.

Define
Q(\operatorname{Vect}_{M})
to be the span category
\operatorname{Span}_{V}(\operatorname{Vect}_{M})
where
V
is the class of vectorial maps between vectorial objects. The closure properties required to form the span category follow from Lemma
11.2
, and
Q(\operatorname{Vect}_{M})
is symmetric monoidal via cartesian product in
\operatorname{Vect}_{M}
.

3.

Denote by
|Q(\operatorname{Vect}_{M})|
the (possibly large)
E_{\infty}
-anima gotten by geometric realization;

4.

Define
\operatorname{K}(\operatorname{Vect}_{M}):=\Omega|Q(\operatorname{Vect}_{M})|
to be the loop space, again viewed as a (group-like)
E_{\infty}
-monoid in (possibly large) anima.

The following gives the multiplicativity of the
J
-construction “with all higher coherences”.

Theorem 11.8
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},E,M)
be a six functor formalism. Then there is a natural map of group-like
E_{\infty}
-anima

J:\operatorname{K}(\operatorname{Vect}_{M})\to\operatorname{Pic}(M(\ast))

refining the
J
construction of Definition
11.5
.

Proof.

Start with the lax symmetric monoidal functor
M:\operatorname{Span}_{E}(\mathcal{C})\to\operatorname{Mod}_{M(\ast)}\operatorname{Pr}^{L}
which “is” our six functor formalism. Restrict to
Q(\operatorname{Vect}_{M})
. Then by vectoriality,
M
becomes strongly symmetric monoidal, and it lands inside the full subcategory of the target spanned by the invertible objects (indeed, by objects isomorphic to the unit
M(\ast)
). In fact by Lemma
11.2
it lands inside the further subcategory of this where all morphisms are invertible, which is by definition
\operatorname{Pic}(\operatorname{Mod}_{M(\ast)}\operatorname{Pr}^{L})
. Thus by adjunction we get an induced map

|Q(\operatorname{Vect}_{M})|\to\operatorname{Pic}(\operatorname{Mod}_{M(\ast)}\operatorname{Pr}^{L}).

Taking loop spaces, the left hand side becomes
K(\operatorname{Vect}_{M})
and the right hand side becomes
\operatorname{Pic}(M(\ast))
, so we get
J
.
∎

Let’s unravel what this is encoding a little bit. There is a natural
E_{\infty}
-map

[-]:\operatorname{Vect}_{M}^{\simeq}\to\operatorname{K}(\operatorname{Vect}_{M})

sending a vectorial object
X
to the loop corresponding to the span

\ast\leftarrow X\rightarrow\ast.

Thus, from
J
we get an
E_{\infty}
map

\operatorname{Vect}_{M}^{\simeq}\to\operatorname{Pic}(M(\ast))

encoding that
J(X\times Y)\simeq J(X)\otimes J(Y)
in a symmetric monoidal fashion.

To see what else Theorem
11.8
is giving, let’s describe
\pi_{0}\operatorname{K}(\operatorname{Vect}_{M})
(basically following
[
Qui72
]
).

Lemma 11.9
.

The abelian group
\pi_{0}\operatorname{K}(\operatorname{Vect}_{M})
is freely generated by the above
[X]
for
X\in\operatorname{Vect}_{M}
modulo the following relations:

1.

[\ast]=0
;

2.

[X]=[Y]
if
X\simeq Y
;

3.

[X\times_{S}Y]+[S]=[X]+[Y]

whenever we have a pullback of the form
X\to S\leftarrow Y
where all objects are vectorial and the map
X\to S
is vectorial.

Proof.

It’s clear that
Q(\operatorname{Vect}_{M})
is connected, and we know that
\pi_{0}\operatorname{K}(\operatorname{Vect}_{M})=\pi_{1}Q(\operatorname{Vect}_{M})
is abelian (by the
E_{\infty}
-structure). Thus it suffices to show that for an abelian group
A
, the set of homotopy classes of functors

F:Q(\operatorname{Vect}_{M})\to BA

is in bijection with the set of elements
\{a_{X}\}_{X\in\operatorname{Vect}_{M}}
of
A
indexed by
X\in\operatorname{Vect}_{M}
subject to the relations 1,2 and 3, via sending a functor
F
to the image of
[X]
under
F
(note there are no base-point issues as
A
is abelian).

The wide subcategory of
Q(\operatorname{Vect}_{M})
corresponding to the spans of the form

X\leftarrow C\overset{\sim}{\to}{Y}

is equivalent to
\operatorname{Vect}_{M}^{op}
, hence has an initial object, hence is contractible. Thus any
F
as above is homotopic to one which is the constant functor with value
\ast
on this subcategory, i.e.
F
takes every object to the basepoint and takes every morphism given by a span as above to the identity. Any homotopy between two such functors can likewise be taken constant on this subcategory; but then it has to be the identity homotopy.

Thus, in this setting everything simplifies: our functor is completely determined by its value on maps of the form
X\overset{=}{\leftarrow}X\to Y
, and this is an element of
A
(because every object is sent to the base-point). Checking what’s necessary for this to be a functor which is trivial on the wide subcategory discussed above, we see that giving
F
as above is equivalent to giving an element
a_{f}
of
A
for every vectorial map
f:X\to Y
between vectorial objects of
\mathcal{C}
, such that the following relations hold:

1.

If
f^{\prime}
is a pullback of
f
then
a_{f^{\prime}}=a_{f}
;

2.

If
f
is an isomorphism then
a_{f}=0
.

3.

If
f
and
g
are composable then
a_{g\circ f}=a_{g}+a_{f}
.

Now, we can go back and forth between these
\{a_{f}\}
and our desired
\{a_{X}\}
via the following: given
\{a_{f}\}
, define
a_{X}=a_{X\to\ast}
; and given
\{a_{X}\}
, define
a_{X\to Y}=a_{X}-a_{Y}
.
∎

Example 11.10
.

As a special case, we see that if
f:X\to Y
is a vectorial map between vectorial objects, and if
\ast\to Y
is any map, then

J(X)\simeq J(F)\otimes J(Y)

where
F=\operatorname{Fib}(f)
, a kind of “twisted multiplicativity”.

Next we want to encode that
J
admits canonical trivializations when restricted to certain special kinds of vectorial objects, namely the proper ones and the etale ones. To explain this at the naive level, suppose
X
is vectorial and
X
is proper (i.e. the map
f:X\to\ast
is proper). Then
f_{!}\simeq f_{\ast}
is right adjoint to
f^{\ast}
, but as
f^{\ast}
is an equivalence it follows that
f_{!}
is the inverse of
f^{\ast}
, so that
f_{!}f^{\ast}\simeq\operatorname{id}
and hence we get a trivialization

J(X)\simeq 1.

Similarly, if
X\to\ast
is etale, then
f_{!}\simeq f_{\natural}
is left adjoint to
f^{\ast}
and the same argument gives the same conclusion
J(X)\simeq 1
.

To give the higher coherences for this, consider the symmetric monoidal subcategories

Q(\operatorname{Vect}^{\pi}_{M}),Q(\operatorname{Vect}^{\epsilon}_{M})\subset Q(\operatorname{Vect}_{M})

where the objects are the vectorial
X
which are moreover proper (resp. etale), and the morphisms are the spans
X\leftarrow C\to Y
giving morphisms in
Q(\operatorname{Vect}_{M})
where the object
C
(and hence each leg) is also required to be proper (resp. etale).

Then exactly as in Definition
11.7
we define group-like
E_{\infty}
-anima

K(\operatorname{Vect}^{\pi}_{M}),K(\operatorname{Vect}^{\epsilon}_{M})

equipped with a natural map to
K(\operatorname{Vect}_{M})
, by geometrically realizing the
Q
-categories and then taking loop spaces.

Theorem 11.11
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},E,M)
be a six functor formalism. The
E_{\infty}
-map

J:K(\operatorname{Vect}_{M})\to\operatorname{Pic}(M(\ast))

of
11.8
admits a canonical trivialization on restriction to
K(\operatorname{Vect}^{\pi}_{M})
(resp.
K(\operatorname{Vect}^{\epsilon}_{M})
).

Proof.

We explain the proper case; the etale case is handled in the same way. We can restrict our six functor formalism to the full subcategory of
\mathcal{C}
spanned by the proper
X
, which is closed under finite limits. Then the
Q(\operatorname{Vect}_{M})
-category for the restricted formalism contains the
Q(\operatorname{Vect}_{M}^{\pi})
for the original one. Thus we see that it suffices to show that if every map in
\mathcal{C}
is proper, then
J\sim 0
. (Unfortunately we can’t assume every object is vectorial, because vectorial objects are not closed under pullback.)

In principle one can prove this just by examining the construction of the six functor formalism in the situation where every map is proper. But that would require “opening the hood” and we don’t want to do that. Instead we will use a dirty trick: the Eilenberg swindle.

Consider
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=\operatorname{Pro}(\mathcal{C})
. Extending
M^{\ast}:\mathcal{C}^{op}\to\operatorname{CAlg}(\operatorname{Pr}^{L})
by filtered colimits, we get a new lax symmetric monoidal functor
(M^{\prime})^{\ast}:(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})^{op}\to\operatorname{CAlg}(\operatorname{Pr}^{L})
. It follows formally by taking limits along right adjoints that for this extended functor, we still have the property that “every map is proper” in the weak sense that for every map
f:X\to Y
in
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
the functor
f^{\ast}:M^{\prime}(Y)\to M^{\prime}(X)
has a right adjoint satisfying the projection formula and commuting with pullback. Thus we can apply
[
HM24
]
Prop 3.3.3 to extend
(M^{\prime})^{\ast}
to a six functor formalism on
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
. When we restrict back to
\mathcal{C}
, it must agree with our original six functor formalism by the uniqueness result
[
DK24
]
.

Moreover, the inclusion
\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
preserves finite limits and sends vectorial maps to vectorial maps. It follows that our
J
for
M
factors through the
J
for
M^{\prime}
, and hence it suffices to show
K(\operatorname{Vect}_{M^{\prime}})=0
. But now consider the functor
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
defined by

X\mapsto\prod_{\mathbb{N}}X.

This preserves finite limits; moreover it sends vectorial maps to vectorial maps. Thus it induces a map

S:\operatorname{K}(\operatorname{Vect}_{M^{\prime}})\to\operatorname{K}(\operatorname{Vect}_{M^{\prime}}).

On the other hand by Hilbert hotel
S\simeq S\times\operatorname{id}
as limit-preserving functors
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
. Passing to K-theory we deduce that

S\sim S+\operatorname{id}

whence, by subtracting in the group-like
E_{\infty}
-space of endomorphisms of
K(\operatorname{Vect}_{M^{\prime}})
, we have a homotopy
0\sim\operatorname{id}
and thus
\operatorname{K}(\operatorname{Vect}_{M^{\prime}})=0
as desired.
∎

Remark 11.12
.

Although produced in the same way, these two nullhomotopies are not compatible. More precisely, on
K(\operatorname{Vect}^{\pi}_{M}\cap\operatorname{Vect}^{\epsilon}_{M})
we get two nullhomotopies of
J
. This gives rise to a map

K(\operatorname{Vect}^{\pi}_{M}\cap\operatorname{Vect}^{\epsilon}_{M})\to\Omega\operatorname{Pic}(M(\ast))=\operatorname{Aut}_{M(\ast)}(1).

This map is not generally trivial. Instead it gives a measure of the “cardinality” of the object
X\in\operatorname{Vect}^{\pi}_{M}\cap\operatorname{Vect}^{\epsilon}_{M}
which is “both compact and discrete, hence finite”. Compare
[
CSY21
]
.

This describes our J-homomorphism construction and its fundamental properties at the most primitive level. But in practice, vectorial objects tend to have abelian group structures allowing to infinitely deloop, and this can be used to simplify the theory a bit. To proceed along these lines, we will start to assume that the
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
on which our six functor formalism lives is an
\infty
-topos (so that we have a correspondence between connected pointed objects and group objects given by looping and delooping), and that we have some reasonable interactions between descent in
\mathcal{C}
and the six functor formalism as in the following definition.

Definition 11.13
.

Say that a six functor formalism
(\mathcal{C},E,M)

has good descent
if the following conditions are satisfied:

1.

\mathcal{C}
is an
\infty
-topos;

2.

M^{\ast}:\mathcal{C}^{op}\to\operatorname{Pr}^{L}
preserves limits;

3.

the condition that a map
f:X\to Y
in
\mathcal{C}
lie in
E
is local on
Y
.

Example 11.14
.

This condition holds for the six functor formalism on light condensed anima considered in Section
7
(for any coefficient category
\mathcal{R}
as in that section). Indeed, 1 and 2 hold by construction, and for 3 see Remark
7.21
.

Moreover, the condition of having good descent evidently passes to slice topoi.

Remark 11.15
.

If
(\mathcal{C},E,M)
is a six functor formalism with good descent, the the property of a map
f:X\to Y
in
\mathcal{C}
being smooth (resp. proper, resp. etale, resp. vectorial) is local on
Y
. This follows from the results in
[
HM24
]
quoted in Remark
7.15
.

The following lemma gives some ways in which passing to classifying spaces simplifies the situation.

Lemma 11.16
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},E,M)
be a six functor formalism with good descent.

1.

Suppose
G
is a group object in
\mathcal{C}
which is vectorial (forgetting the group structure). Then
BG
is also vectorial. More generally, suppose
G\to\ast
is smooth, and pullback along it is fully faithful after any base-change. Then
BG\to\ast
is vectorial.

2.

Suppose
G
and
G^{\prime}
are vectorial group objects and
f:G\to G^{\prime}
is any homomorphism. Then
BG\to BG^{\prime}
is vectorial.

3.

Suppose
G
is a group object which is etale and vectorial. Then
BG
is etale and vectorial.

4.

Suppose
G
is a group object such that
f:G\to\ast
is proper, and pullback along
f
is fully faithful, also after any base-change (for example
G
could be proper and vectorial). Then
BG
is proper and vectorial.

Proof.

For 1, let’s prove the more general claim. By descent pullback along
\ast\to BG
is fully faithful, also after any base-change. Since obviously pullback along
\ast\to\ast
is an equivalence also after any base-change, we deduce that pullback along
BG\to\ast
is an equivalence, also after any base-change. The same argument applies to
!
-pullback, but we first need to show that
BG\to\ast
is
!
-able. But
\ast\to BG
is vectorial and hence smooth, while pullback along it is conservative, hence
BG\to\ast
is
!
-able (and smooth) by
[
HM24
]
Lemma 4.7.4 and Cor. 4.7.5.

For 2, again by descent
\ast\to BG
and
\ast\to BG^{\prime}
are vectorial, therefore so is
BG\to BG^{\prime}
by Lemma
11.2
.

For 3,
\ast\to BG
is an etale cover and
\ast\to\ast
is etale, so this follows from
[
HM24
]
Lemma 4.6.3.

For 4, the map
e:\ast\to BG
is proper and vectorial by descent, and
e_{\ast}1
is descendable because
e_{\ast}1\simeq 1
by vectoriality. Since
\ast\to\ast
is proper, by
[
HM24
]
4.7.4 and 4.7.5 it follows that
f:BG\to\ast
is proper, and in particular
!
-able. The same argument as in 1 shows that
\ast
- or
!
-pullback along
BG\to\ast
is an equivalence also after any base-change. (Caution that even though in this case
f^{!}\simeq f^{\ast}
, the map
f
is not generally etale, because its diagonal is generally not etale.) ∎

Definition 11.17
.

Suppose given a six functor formalism
(\mathcal{C},E,M)
which has good descent. Say that a spectrum object
X\in\mathcal{C}\otimes\operatorname{Sp}
is
vectorial
if the following conditions are satisfied:

1.

X
is bounded below in the t-structure.

2.

For
n>>0
, the object
\Omega^{\infty}\Sigma^{n}X\in\mathcal{C}
is vectorial.

Denote the full subcategory of vectorial objects in
\mathcal{C}\otimes\operatorname{Sp}
by
\operatorname{vect}_{M}
.

Remark 11.18
.

Suppose
X
is connective. If
\Omega^{\infty}\Sigma^{n}X
is vectorial for some
n\geq 0
, then it is vectorial for all
n^{\prime}\geq n
. This follows from Lemma
11.16
.

Lemma 11.19
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},E,M)
be a six functor formalism with good descent. Then
\operatorname{vect}_{M}\subset\mathcal{C}\otimes\operatorname{Sp}
is a stable subcateogry, i.e. it is closed under fibers and shifts.

Proof.

Closure under shifts is immediate from the definition. For closure under fibers, let
f:X\to Y
be a map between vectorial spectrum objects in
\mathcal{C}
. Shifting we can assume that
X,Y\in(\mathcal{C}\otimes\operatorname{Sp})_{\geq 1}
and
\Omega^{\infty}\Sigma^{-1}X
and
\Omega^{\infty}\Sigma^{-1}Y
are vectorial. Then by Lemma
11.16
the map
\Omega^{\infty}X\to\Omega^{\infty}Y
is vectorial. Therefore by Lemma
11.2
its fiber is also vectorial. But this fiber is
\Omega^{\infty}F
where
F=\operatorname{Fib}(f)
and we conclude.
∎

For this stable category
\operatorname{vect}_{M}
, we can define its Q-construction to simply be the span category with all maps allowed in the spans, then take the geometric realization, then take the loop space to define
K(\operatorname{vect}_{M})
. But now this is actually a standard construction of Barwick known to be equivalent to all other possible definitions of the (connective) algebraic K-theory of a stable
\infty
-category (
[
Bar15
]
).

Our next purpose is to show that
J
can also be defined on this stable level. For this we build a bridge between the stable and unstable situations as follows. Consider the symmetric monoidal subcategory

Q(\operatorname{vect}_{M,\geq 1})\subset Q(\operatorname{vect}_{M})

where the objects are the
X\in\operatorname{vect}_{M}
such that
\pi_{i}X=0
for
i<1
and
\Omega^{\infty}X
is vectorial, and the the maps are the spans
X\leftarrow C\to Y
such that
\pi_{i}C=0
for
i<1
and the map
C\to Y
is surjective on
\pi_{1}
and vectorial on
\Omega^{\infty}
.

Then besides this symmetric monoidal inclusion there is also the symmetric monoidal functor

\Omega^{\infty}:Q(\operatorname{vect}_{M,\geq 1})\to Q(\operatorname{Vect}_{M})

induced by
X\mapsto\Omega^{\infty}(X)
on objects and spans.

Theorem 11.20
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},E,M)
be a six functor formalism with good descent. Then there is a unique map of (group-like)
E_{\infty}
-anima

J^{new}:\operatorname{K}(\operatorname{vect}_{M})\to\operatorname{Pic}(M(\ast))

equipped with an isomorphism
J^{new}\mid_{\operatorname{K}(\operatorname{vect}_{M,\geq 1})}\simeq J\circ\Omega^{\infty}
, where
J
is the earlier construction
11.8
.

From now on we will drop the “new” superscript and just call both of these maps
J
.

Proof.

Clearly, it suffices to show that

K(\operatorname{vect}_{M,\geq 1})\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}K(\operatorname{vect}_{M}).

For this, note that we have

(\mathcal{C}\otimes\operatorname{Sp})_{>>-\infty}=\varinjlim((\mathcal{C}\otimes\operatorname{Sp})_{\geq 1})

where the colimit on the right is the sequential colimit where each transition map is given by the shift functor
X\mapsto X[1]
. Passing to span categories and then restricting to vectorial subcategories, we get

Q(\operatorname{vect}_{M})=\varinjlim Q(\operatorname{vect}_{M,\geq 1}).

Thus it suffices to show that the functor
X\mapsto X[1]
induces an isomorphism

K(\operatorname{vect}_{M,\geq 1})\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}K(\operatorname{vect}_{M\geq 1}).

In fact we will show it induces
-\operatorname{id}
. Note that these
K(\operatorname{vect}_{M,\geq 1})
fit into the framework of Quillen K-theory of exact
\infty
-categories developed in
[
Bar15
]
: the underlying additive category is that of spectrum objects
X
with
\pi_{i}(X)=0
for
i<1
such that
\Omega^{\infty}X
is vectorial; the class of “egressive” maps is that of maps surjective on
\pi_{1}
whose underlying map of objects in
\mathcal{C}
is vectorial, and the class of “ingressive” maps is all maps. In particular we have access to the additivity theorem. However, we have a functorial fiber sequence

X\mapsto(X\to 0\to X[1])

where
0\to X[1]
is egressive so from the additivity theorem we deduce the claim.
∎

Remark 11.21
.

This theorem explains how to calculate
J(X)
for
X\in\operatorname{vect}_{M}
. Namely, find an
n\in 2\mathbb{Z}
such that
X[n]
lies in
\operatorname{vect}_{M,\geq 1}
. Then

J(X)=J(X[n])=J(\Omega^{\infty}(X[n])).

We can completely analogously translate the canonical trivializations of
J
from
11.11
to the stable setting.

Theorem 11.22
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},E,M)
be a six functor formalism with good descent. Let
\operatorname{vect}_{M}^{\pi}
denote the full subcategory of objects
X\in\operatorname{vect}_{M}
such that
\Omega^{\infty}X[n]\in\mathcal{C}
is proper (and vectorial) for
n>>0
, and similarly for
\operatorname{vect}_{M}^{\epsilon}
with etale instead of proper.

Then
\operatorname{vect}_{M}^{\pi}
and
\operatorname{vect}_{M}^{\epsilon}
are stable subcategories of
\operatorname{vect}_{M}
, and

J:K(\operatorname{vect}_{M})\to\operatorname{Pic}(M(\ast))

admits a canonical trivialization on restriction to each of
K(\operatorname{vect}_{M}^{\pi}),K(\operatorname{vect}_{M}^{\epsilon})
.

Proof.

The fact that these are stable subcategories follows exactly as in the proof of
11.19
, and the canonical trivializations are deduced from their unstable versions (
11.11
) by exactly the same method as in the proof of
11.20
.
∎

Finally, we return to light condensed anima with their six functor formalism of etale sheaves with coefficients in
\operatorname{Sp}_{\widehat{p}}
for a fixed prime
p
, as in Section
7
. We will produce many examples of vectorial light condensed spectra. They will actually be spectrum objects concentrated in degree
0
, that is, light condensed abelian groups. By definition, such an
A
is vectorial (resp. proper and vectorial, resp. etale and vectorial) as a light condensed spectrum if and only if some iterated classifying space
B^{n}A
is vectorial (resp. proper and vectorial, resp. etale and vectorial) as a light condensed anima. In all examples we consider,
n=2
will suffice, and often
n=1
will suffice.

Theorem 11.23
.

Let
p
be a prime. In the six functor formalism for etale sheaves on light condensed anima with
p
-complete spectrum coefficients, we have:

1.

For any (discrete)
\mathbb{Z}[1/p]
-module
M
, the corresponding light condensed abelian group
M
is etale and vectorial.

2.

For any compact Hausdorff
\mathbb{Z}[1/p]
-module
M
which is second-countable and finite dimensional, the corresponding light condensed abelian group
M
is proper and vectorial.

3.

For any local field
F
of characteristic
\neq p
and any finite dimensional
F
-vector space
V
, the corresponding light condensed abelian group
V
is vectorial.

Proof.

For 1, we claim that
f:BM\to\ast
is etale and pullback along it is fully faithful. This fully faithfulness automatically passes to all base-changes by Lemma
9.2
, so by Lemma
11.16
that will show that
M
is etale and vectorial. Etaleness is Lemma
7.19
. For full fidelity, we need to show that

f_{\natural}f^{\ast}X=X

for all
X\in\operatorname{Sp}_{\widehat{p}}
. Working mod
p
and using the Postnikov tower, it suffices to treat
X=\mathbb{F}_{p}
. So we need to see that the group homology of
M
with
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-coefficients is
\mathbb{F}_{p}[0]
. But by resolutions and Kunneth we can reduce to
M=\mathbb{Z}[1/p]
where it’s a simple calculation.

For 2, because of the short exact sequence

0\to M^{0}\to M\to\pi_{0}M\to 0

and Lemma
11.19
we can reduce to the connected and totally disconnected cases. In the totally disconnected case,
M
is Pontryagin dual to a countable torsion discrete
\mathbb{Z}[1/p]
-module, hence
M\simeq\varprojlim M_{n}
is a sequential limit of abelian groups of prime-to-
p
order. By Lemma
11.16
it suffices to show that
BM\to\ast
is proper and pullback along it is fully faithful (this will then automatically be so after any base change by Lemma
9.2
). The properness follows from Theorem
7.20
because prime-to-
p
groups have mod
p
cohomological dimension
0
. For full fidelity, arguing as usual with the Postnikov tower we reduce to showing that the
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-cohomology is just
\mathbb{F}_{p}
in degree
0
, but again this follows from the same remark about cohomological dimension. For connected
M
, we claim that
M\to\ast
is proper and fully faithful on pullback. Properness is Theorem
7.20
. For full fidelity, note by Pontryagin duality that
M
is dual to a torsionfree countably generated
\mathbb{Z}[1/p]
-module, hence identifies with a sequential inverse limit of finite products of copies of the solenoid
C=\varprojlim\mathbb{R}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z}
. By Corollary
4.34
we reduce to
C
itself, in which case the claim follows from a simple (mod
p
) cohomology calculation (exactly as in
9.11
).

For 3, first consider nonarchimedean local fields
F
of residue characteristic
\neq p
. In the short exact sequence

\mathcal{O}_{F}\to F\to F/\mathcal{O}_{F}

the first term is vectorial by 2 and the last term is vectorial by 1, so
F
and hence
V
(as a direct sum of copies of
F
) is also vectorial by Lemma
11.19
.

Next consider archimedean local fields, which reduces to showing that
\mathbb{R}
is vectorial. But
\mathbb{R}\to\ast
is smooth and pullback along it is fully faithful (also after any base change) so this follows from Lemma
11.16
.

Finally we consider nonarchimedean local fields of residue characteristic
p
. This reduces to showing that
F=\mathbb{Q}_{p}
is vectorial. However we have a short exact sequence

0\to\mathbb{Z}[1/p]\to\mathbb{Q}_{p}\oplus\mathbb{R}\to C\to 0

where
C=\varprojlim_{n}\mathbb{R}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z}
is the
p
-solenoid. By 1 the first term is vectorial and by 2 the last term is vectorial. Thus
\mathbb{Q}_{p}\oplus\mathbb{R}
is vectorial. As
\mathbb{R}
is vectorial, so is
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
as desired.
∎

By the general theory (Theorem
11.20
) we have a map

J:\operatorname{K}(\operatorname{vect}_{\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(-;\operatorname{Sp}_{\widehat{p}})})\to\operatorname{Pic}(\operatorname{Sp}_{\widehat{p}})=\operatorname{Pic}(\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}})

(for the last equality, see Lemma
13.16
). For any local field
F
of characteristic
\neq p
, by Lemma
11.23
we can restrict to finite free
F
-modules and therefore get

J_{F}:\operatorname{K}(F)\to\operatorname{Pic}(\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}).

This is our replacement for the piecemeal constructions of J-homomorphisms in
[
Cla12
]
.

Remark 11.24
.

Of course, as the above proofs show,
\mathbb{R}
is already vectorial with
\operatorname{Sp}
-coefficients, so we get a more refined
J_{\mathbb{R}}:\operatorname{K}(\mathbb{R})\to\operatorname{Pic}(\mathbb{S})
. We can also work in families as in Section
10
to directly see that this
J_{\mathbb{R}}
factors through the connective topological K-theory spectrum
ko
(although such a factoring is also automatic and unique by
[
Sus84
]
and finiteness of homotopy groups of spheres).

Similarly, for
F
nonarchimedean but of residue characteristic
\ell\neq p
we actually get
J_{F}:\operatorname{K}(F)\to\operatorname{Pic}(\mathbb{S}[1/\ell])
which again is more refined.

Remark 11.25
.

One should be careful with sign conventions. By Lemma
11.6
we have that for a finite dimensional
\mathbb{R}
-vector space
V
, the invertible spectrum
J_{\mathbb{R}}(V)
identifies with the
inverse
of the dualizing spectrum
0^{\ast}f^{!}\mathbb{S}
associated to

\ast\overset{0}{\to}V\overset{f}{\to}\ast,

and by Lemma
10.5
this dualizing spectrum
0^{\ast}f^{!}\mathbb{S}
identifies with the suspension spectrum of
V/(V\setminus 0)
, which is one of the usual ways of getting the real J-homomorphsim. These identifications were made using the six functor formalism which is lax symmetric monoidal by definition, and it follows that as maps of spectra, we get an identification of
J_{\mathbb{R}}
with the
negative
of the usual J-homomorphism
K(\mathbb{R})\to\operatorname{Pic}(\mathbb{S})
.

On the other hand
J_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
sends
V
to the dualizing object
\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}^{V}
for
BV
considered in Section
10
, which lives in positive degrees. The fact these behaviors are “opposite” is inevitable by reciprocity,
11.26
. But which one is positive and which one is negative is a matter of sign convention which (like in the analogous situation of local Artin maps) has no mathematical significance.

The main result on these J-homomorphisms is their reciprocity law (from
[
Cla12
]
), which we reprove here. We state it in a bit more elementary finitary way:

Theorem 11.26
.

Let
p
be a prime,
F
a global field of characteristic
\neq p
, and
S
a finite set of places of
F
containing all the infinite places and all the places above
p
. Then there is a canonical null-homotopy of the composition

K(\mathcal{O}_{F,S})\to\oplus_{\nu\in S}K(F_{\nu})\to\operatorname{Pic}(\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}),

where the first map is induced by the natural homomorphism
\mathcal{O}_{F,S}\to F_{\nu}
on the
\nu
-component and the second map is given by
J_{F_{\nu}}
on the
\nu
-component.

Proof.

Consider the short exact sequence of light condensed
\mathcal{O}_{F,S}
-modules

0\to\mathcal{O}_{F,S}\to\prod_{\nu\in S}F_{\nu}\to C_{F,S}\to 0.

The third term is another solenoidal compact Hausdorff space. By Lemma
11.23
all objects appearing are vectorial, the first term is etale, and the last term is proper.

For any finitely generated projective
\mathbb{Z}[1/S]
-module
M
we can tensor this short exact sequence with
M
to get another short exact sequence with the same properties. In K-theory we have that the middle term is the sum of the two outer terms, but these outer terms go to
0
under
J
by Theorem
11.22
. Thus the middle term goes to
0
under
J
. But the middle term breaks up as the sum over
\nu
of the
\nu
-adic J-homomorphisms, whence the claim.∎

Remark 11.27
.

We already used one simple corollary of this in the proof of Proposition
10.16
: namely, the image of
p\in\mathbb{Q}_{p}^{\times}=\pi_{1}K(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
under
J_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
is trivial because this is so for the image of
p\in\mathbb{R}^{\times}
under
J_{\mathbb{R}}
.

Remark 11.28
.

The nicest way to state this kind of result for an aribitrary (possibly infinite) set of primes
S
was given in
[
Bra23
]
, in terms of restricted direct products of exact categories.

It’s perhaps not so worthwhile to prove that the new definition of
J_{F}
agrees with the old one from
[
Cla12
]
: one should just use the new definition instead (though in principle arguments like those in Appendix C of
[
Cla12
]
are sufficient to establish the comparison). In the paper
[
Cla17
]
, which showed that Theorem
11.26
(combined with cohomological dimension estimates for number fields and local fields) recovers the Artin reciprocity law, we only needed minimal information on the
J_{F}
: besides the reciprocity law
11.26
and certain functorial properties which clearly hold for any definition, we just needed to know what
J_{\mathbb{Q}_{p}}
was in degrees
0
and
1
. For the new definition, this is already covered by Proposition
10.16
.

Remark 11.29
.

We can at least ask to characterize the
J_{F}
after
K(1)
-localization, and this is a tractable problem which again only requires knowing
J_{F}
in degrees
0
and
1
.

The result can be expressed in terms of the following calculation: when
F
is a field of characteristic
\neq p
and of virtual cohomological dimension
\leq 2
, the co-descent spectral sequence from
[
Cla17
]
2.4 gives a natural short exact sequence

0\to H^{0}(G_{F};\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{\vee}\to[K(F),L_{K(1)}\operatorname{Pic}(\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}})]\to H^{2}(G_{F};\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}(1))^{\vee}\to 0

where
(-)^{\vee}
denotes Pontryagin duality (turning discrete
p
-torsion abelian groups into profinite abelian
p
-groups).

Then in these terms, for any local field
F
of characteristic
\neq p
, our J-homomorphism

J_{F}\in[K(F),L_{K(1)}\operatorname{Pic}(\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}})]

projects to the fundamental class

\operatorname{inv}_{F}\in H^{2}(G_{F};\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}(1))^{\vee}

for local Tate duality (and therefore Theorem
11.26
recovers and refines the Hasse reciprocity law). Indeed, pairing with a class
u\in F^{\times}=K_{1}(F)
this reduces to the result in
[
Cla17
]
that the local Artin map is recovered one term up in the spectral sequence where we have

[\Sigma K(F),L_{K(1)}\operatorname{Pic}(\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}})]=H^{1}(G_{F};\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{\vee}.

Moreover, among all elements of
[K(F),L_{K(1)}\operatorname{Pic}(\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}})]
which project to
\operatorname{inv}_{F}
, we can pin down
J_{F}
uniquely by its effect on
\pi_{0}
.

12
Morava E-theory in the light condensed framework

Let us start with some recollections on chromatic homotopy theory, from the perspective of the “
v_{n}
-self maps” of Hopkins-Smith (
[
HS98
]
). Let
p
be a prime number. The
\infty
-category
\operatorname{Sp}_{(p)}
of
p
-local spectra is in some ways similar to the derived
\infty
-category
\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Z}_{(p)})
. In the latter, one has a distinguished self-map of any object, given by multiplication by
p
. For
M\in\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Z}_{(p)})
, the object

M[p^{-1}]=\varinjlim(M\overset{\cdot p}{\to}M\overset{\cdot p}{\to}\ldots)

promotes to an object in
\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Q})
. Thus it is controlled by linear algebra over a field. The “difference” between
M
and
M[p^{-1}]
, or more precisely the cofiber of the natural map
M\to M[p^{-1}]
, is the colimit of
M/p^{n}=\operatorname{cofib}(M\overset{p^{n}}{\to}M)
. This in turn promotes to an object of
D(\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}\mathbb{Z})
, and hence is controlled by a nilpotent thickening of a field.

In this way, the canonical self-map
\cdot p
allows to decompose
D(\mathbb{Z}_{(p)})
into pieces, each of which is in some way controlled by linear algebra over nilpotent thickenings of fields. The story with
\operatorname{Sp}_{(p)}
is similar, except we need to iterate this process, and the meaning of being “controlled by a nilpotent thickening of a field” is a bit more abstract. We still have a self-map
\cdot p
, and for
M\in\operatorname{Sp}_{(p)}
we still have that
M[p^{-1}]
promotes to an object in
\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Q})
. (This is a reflection of Serre’s theorem that the stable homotopy groups of spheres in positive degrees are finite.) On the other hand,
M/p^{n}
does not behave like it lives over a field anymore. Instead, we get a new functorial self-map of
M/p^{n}
called
v_{1}
. It’s not literally a map
M/p^{n}\to M/p^{n}
, but rather a map from some shift of
M/p^{n}
to
M/p^{n}
, but the formalism of modding out and inverting works in that context as well.

There is also a subtlety that
v_{1}
is really only well-defined as a homotopy class, and even then only up to replacing
v_{1}
to
v_{1}^{N}
for some large
N
. But if all we want to do with
v_{1}
is complete along it or invert it, this ambiguity doesn’t matter.

Then it turns out that for all
M\in\operatorname{Sp}_{(p)}
, the spectrum
M/p[v_{1}^{-1}]
in some sense “lives over a field”, or at least over something that formally behaves like a nil-thickening of a field. This can be codified in terms of the Balmer spectrum, but let’s not get into the details of that.

Thus we have gone one step further than in the algebraic case of
\operatorname{D}(\mathbb{Z}_{(p)})
, but we are still not done, because once again
M/(p,v_{1})
does not generally “live over a field”. Instead we get a new functorial degree-shifting self-map
v_{2}
, and
M/(p,v_{1})[v_{2}^{-1}]
behaves like it lives over a nilpotent thickening of a field. And so on and so forth.

If this process applied to a spectrum
M
stops after
n
steps, meaning that
M/(p,v_{1},\ldots,v_{n})=0
, then
M
is called
L_{n}^{f}
-local
. In some sense this means that
M
lives over a Zariski open subset of
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{S}_{(p)})
, namely the one described informally as the complement of the common vanishing locus of
(p,v_{1},\ldots,v_{n})
. But it’s a bit subtle because the
v_{i}
are not globally defined. In any case, there is a corresponding localization functor
L_{n}^{f}
on spectra, which fits into a tower under the identity functor

\ldots L_{n}^{f}\to L_{n-1}^{f}\to\ldots\to L_{0}^{f}\to L_{-1}^{f}=0,

called the (finitary, or telescopic) chromatic tower.

In fact we have
L_{n}^{f}M=M\otimes L_{n}^{f}\mathbb{S}
for all
M\in\operatorname{Sp}
, just like with Zariski localizations. Thus the most basic
L_{n}^{f}
-local spectrum is the
L_{n}^{f}
-local sphere. It is very difficult to calculate, due to the intricate nature of the
v_{i}
and the stable homotopy groups of spheres.

Fortunately, however, there is a simpler
L_{n}^{f}
-local
E_{\infty}
-ring spectrum which avoids these complications, but still controls a large sector of
L_{n}^{f}
-local stable homotopy theory. (More precisely, it controls the
L_{n}
-local spectra, the more calculable portion of
L_{n}^{f}
-local spectra.) This is the
Morava
E
-theory
, denoted
E
(we suppress the implicit dependence on
p
and
n
). Its homotopy groups are
0
in odd degrees, and are 2-periodic, via an invertible class
\sigma\in\pi_{2}E
. Finally, its
\pi_{0}
is isomorphic, non-canonically, to a power series ring over a Witt vector ring:

\pi_{0}E\simeq W(\mathbb{F}_{p^{n}})[[u_{1},\ldots,u_{n-1}]].

The classes
p,u_{1},\ldots,u_{n-1}\in\pi_{0}E
are some kind of incarnation of
p,v_{1},\ldots,v_{n-1}
(at least for the purposes of completion or inversion), and the class
\sigma\in\pi_{2}E
is some kind of incarnation of
v_{n}
. But now we see all these classes globally and, in some sense, algebraically.

With this as motivation, we now turn to a more thorough discussion of this Morava E-theory spectrum
E
. We start with some algebraic preliminaries, as Morava E-theory (and indeed the whole chromatic story) is closely tied to the algebraic geometry of formal groups, though we avoided mentioning this connection in the above motivation.

More precisely, we are interested in one-dimensional formal groups over
\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}
-algebras
R
. For this our main reference is
[
Goe08
]
; we give small recap of some material there, but incorporating the perspective of Cartier’s theory
[
Car67
]
, for which
[
Mum69
]
is a good reference.

Recall that if
\widehat{G}
is a one-dimensional formal group over
R
, then we get:

1.

An
R
-module
\omega=\operatorname{coLie}(\widehat{G})
which is locally free of rank one, namely the cotangent bundle to
\widehat{G}
at the identity section;

2.

An inductively defined sequence of elements
p=v_{0},v_{1},\ldots
with

v_{i}\in\omega^{\otimes(p^{i}-1)}\otimes_{R}R/(p,v_{1},\ldots,v_{i-1}),

called the
Hasse invariants
.

Namely, we recall that for a homomorphism
f:\widehat{G}\to\widehat{H}
of one-dimensional formal groups over an
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-algebra, the induced map
f^{\ast}:\omega_{\widehat{H}}\to\omega_{\widehat{G}}
is
0
if and only if
f
factors (necessarily uniquely) through the relative Frobenius
\widehat{G}\to\widehat{G}^{(p)}
. In particular, the map
[p]:\widehat{G}\to\widehat{G}
admits a factoring through the Frobenius, whence a map
V_{1}:\widehat{G}^{(p)}\to\widehat{G}
. The section
v_{1}\in\omega^{\otimes(p-1)}
corresponds to the effect of
V_{1}
on the cotangent space at the identity, using
\omega_{\widehat{G}^{(p)}}=\omega_{G}^{\otimes p}
. Then if
v_{1}=0
, we can further factor through to
V_{2}:\widehat{G}^{(p^{2})}\to\widehat{G}
, whose effect on the contangent space at the identity is
v_{2}
, etc.

A one-dimensional formal group over a
\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}
-algebra
R
is said to have
exact height
n
if
v_{0},v_{1},\ldots,v_{n-1}
are all
0
and
v_{n}
is invertible. Exact height
0
just means that
R
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra. For
n\geq 1
, by definition
\widehat{G}
over
R
has exact height
n
if and only if
R
is an
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-algebra, and there exists a (necessarily unique) isomorphism
G^{(p^{n})}\simeq G
identifying the relative Frobenius
G\to G^{(p^{n})}
with the multiplication by
p
map
G\to G
.

One can classify the one-dimensional formal groups of exact height
n
in terms of the Cartier theory of
p
-typical curves. Namely, by Cartier theory, formal groups over a
\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}
-algebra
R
correspond to certain
V
-reduced
V
-complete
p
-typical Cartier modules
M
over
R
: to
\widehat{G}
we assign the Cartier module of
p
-typical curves in
\widehat{G}
. (Recall that Cartier modules are abelian groups equipped with left operators
V
,
F
, and
[r]
for
r\in R
satisfying certain properties.) The one-dimensional formal groups correspond to those
M
such that
M/VM
, which carries a natural
R
-module structure via the
[r]
, is locally free of rank one (it corresponds to
\omega_{\widehat{G}}^{-1}
). In such a case we have that, Zariski locally on
\operatorname{Spec}(R)
, there is an
e\in M
which forms a
V
-basis, meaning the image of
e
in
M/VM
is a basis for that
R
-module. Given such a
V
-basis
e\in M
, elements
m\in M
correspond uniquely to sequences
(r_{d})_{d\geq 0}
of elements of
R
via an expansion of the form
m=\sum_{d\geq 0} V^{d}[r_{d}]e
, and the structure of
M
as a Cartier module (and hence of
\widehat{G}
as a formal group) is uniquely specified by a single relation of the form

Fe=\sum_{d\geq 0}V^{d}[u_{d+1}]e,

where
u_{1},u_{2},\ldots\in R
can be arbitrary.

On the formal group side, the generator
e\in M
corresponds to a
p
-typical coordinate
t
. As for the
u_{d}\in R
giving the defining relation, when
R
is an
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-algebra we have
p=VF
in the Cartier ring, so multiplying the defining relation by
V
on the left we deduce the following expansion of the
p
-series of
\widehat{G}
:

[p]_{\widehat{G}}(t)=\sum_{d\geq 1}^{\widehat{G}}u_{d}t^{p^{d}}.

Here the sum on the right is taken with respect to the group structure on
\widehat{G}
. In particular we see that if we set
u_{0}:=p
, then these
u_{0},u_{1},u_{2},\ldots
, which are globally defined as elements of
R
given the
p
-typical coordinate
t
, lift the Hasse invariants
v_{0},v_{1},\ldots
via the trivialization of
\omega^{p^{n}-1}
coming from the coordinate. Thus a formal group presented by a Cartier module as above has exact height
n
if and only if
u_{0},u_{1},\ldots,u_{n-1}=0
and
u_{n}
is a unit.

With this in mind, we now write down the simplest possible Cartier module yielding a formal group of exact height
n\geq 1
.

Definition 12.1
.

Let
n\geq 1
. Define the
Honda formal group

\widehat{H}_{n}
over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
to be the formal group whose associated Cartier module is presented by a single generator
e
with the single relation
Fe=V^{n-1}e
. Thus, by the above discussion,
\widehat{H}_{n}
has a
p
-typical coordinate
t
with
p
-series given by

[p](t)=t^{p^{n}},

and this uniquely specifies
\widehat{H}_{n}
.

As is well-known, we can use
\widehat{H}_{n}
to understand all formal groups of exact height
n
. We give the explanation from the perspective of Cartier modules.

Definition 12.2
.

Let
\mathbb{F}_{p^{n}}
be a finite field of order
p^{n}
. Define a noncommutative ring
\mathcal{O}_{n}
equipped with a distinguished element
\pi\in\mathcal{O}_{n}
and a distinguished homomorphism
W(\mathbb{F}_{p^{n}})\to\mathcal{O}_{n}
by the following prescriptions:

1.

\mathcal{O}_{n}
is free of rank
n
as a right
W(\mathbb{F}_{p^{n}})
-module on
1,\pi,\ldots,\pi^{n-1}
;

2.

\pi\alpha=\varphi(\alpha)\pi
, where
\varphi
is the Frobenius on
W(\mathbb{F}_{p^{n}})
;

3.

\pi^{n}=p
.

Note that this is consistent because
\varphi^{n}=\operatorname{id}
on
\mathbb{F}_{p^{n}}
. Also, by property 2 we can equally well replace “right” with “left” in property 1.

This is a sort of noncommutative Witt vector ring: note that the left and right ideals generated by
\pi
agree, left or right multiplication by
\pi
is injective, and

\mathcal{O}_{n}=\varprojlim_{i}\mathcal{O}_{n}/\pi^{i},

with
\mathcal{O}_{n}/\pi=\mathbb{F}_{p^{n}}
. Thus every element of
\mathcal{O}_{n}
admits a unique expansion in the form
\sum_{d\geq 0}\pi^{d}[c_{d}]
with
c_{d}\in\mathbb{F}_{p^{n}}
, where
[c_{d}]
stands for the Teichmueller lift in
W(\mathbb{F}_{p^{n}})
.

We will be interested in the group of units
\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\times}
. This is the subset of elements of
\mathcal{O}_{n}
whose image in
\mathcal{O}_{n}/\pi=\mathbb{F}_{p^{n}}
is nonzero, and it can be viewed as a profinite group via

\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\times}=\varprojlim_{i}(\mathcal{O}_{n}/\pi^{i})^{\times}.

Likewise,
\mathcal{O}_{n}
is a profinite ring.

Theorem 12.3
.

Let
n\geq 1
. Then:

1.

Every one-dimensional formal group
\widehat{G}
of exact height
n
over an
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-algebra
R
is pro-etale locally isomorphic to
\widehat{H}_{n}
.

2.

The pro-etale sheaf of rings
