\operatorname{End}(\widehat{H}_{n})
of endomorphisms of
\widehat{H}_{n}
over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
, when base-changed to
\mathbb{F}_{p^{n}}
, is represented by the pro-constant ring scheme on the profinite ring
\mathcal{O}_{n}
.

3.

The identification in 2 is equivariant with respect to
\operatorname{Gal}(\mathbb{F}_{p^{n}}/\mathbb{F}_{p})
, which acts on
\mathcal{O}_{n}
since this ring was functorially constructed in terms of
\mathbb{F}_{p^{n}}
.

Proof.

First consider 1. Working Zariski locally on
\operatorname{Spec}(R)
we can assume that the Cartier module of
\widehat{G}
is presented by a single generator
e
with the single relation

Fe=\sum_{d\geq 0}V^{d+n-1}[c_{d}]e

where
c_{d}\in R
for all
d\geq 0
and
c_{0}\in R^{\times}
. We need to find, pro-etale locally, a new
V
-basis vector
e^{\prime}
such that
Fe^{\prime}=V^{n-1}e^{\prime}
. Write

e^{\prime}=\sum_{k\geq 0}V^{k}[b_{k}]e.

The condition that
e^{\prime}
be a
V
-basis is
b_{0}\in R^{\times}
. Furthermore, using relations in the Cartier ring and the above expression for
Fe
we compute that

Fe^{\prime}=\sum_{d,k\geq 0}V^{d+k+n-1}[\varphi^{d+n}(b_{k})\cdot c_{d}]e

whereas

V^{n-1}e^{\prime}=\sum_{k\geq 0}V^{k+n-1}[b_{k}]e.

Equating coefficients, we find that
e^{\prime}
does what we want if and only if

b_{0}=\varphi^{n}(b_{0})\cdot c_{0},

b_{1}=\varphi^{n+1}(b_{0})\cdot c_{1}+\varphi^{n}(b_{1})\cdot c_{0},

b_{2}=\varphi^{n+2}(b_{0})\cdot c_{2}+\varphi^{n+1}(b_{1})\cdot c_{1}+\varphi^{n}(b_{2})\cdot c_{0},

etc. Divide through by
c_{0}
. Then when we try to inductively solve for the
b_{k}
, we find that each equation amounts to a monic polynomial that
b_{k}
should be a root of, and that this monic polynomial has derivative the constant polynomial on the unit
-c_{0}^{-1}
, hence describes a finite etale extension. To enforce that
b_{0}
be a unit, we can divide the first equation by
b_{0}
and see that we’re still left with a finite etale equation
b_{0}^{p^{n}-1}=c_{0}^{-1}
for
b_{0}
, but one which forces
b_{0}
to be a unit. Thus at the top of this tower of finite etale extensions we get an isomorphism
\widehat{G}\simeq\widehat{H_{n}}
, as required.

More precisely, what the above argument showed was that the functor on
R
-algebras
\operatorname{Hom}(\widehat{H_{n}},\widehat{G})
parametrizing the set of homomorphisms
\widehat{H_{n}}\to\widehat{G}
is canonically the inverse limit

\operatorname{Hom}(\widehat{H_{n}},\widehat{G})\simeq\varprojlim_{i}\operatorname{Hom}_{i}(\widehat{H_{n}},\widehat{G})

where
\operatorname{Hom}_{i}(\widehat{H_{n}},\widehat{G})
parametrizes homomorphisms of the associated Cartier modules mod
V^{i}
(so in particular
\operatorname{Hom}_{0}(\widehat{H_{n}},\widehat{G})=\operatorname{Spec}(R)
), and

\operatorname{Hom}_{i+1}(\widehat{H_{n}},\widehat{G})\to\operatorname{Hom}_{i}(\widehat{H_{n}},\widehat{G})

is finite etale and surjective for all
i\geq 0
, described by the sequence of separable monic polynomials discussed above; moreover the subfunctor
\operatorname{Isom}(\widehat{H_{n}},\widehat{G})\subset\operatorname{End}(\widehat{H_{n}},\widehat{G})
parametrizing isomorphisms comes by pullback from
\operatorname{Isom}_{1}(\widehat{H_{n}},\widehat{G})
, which is the finite etale extension of
\operatorname{Spec}(R)
described by
c_{0}X^{p^{n}-1}=1
inside
\operatorname{Hom}_{1}(\widehat{G},\widehat{H_{n}})=\operatorname{Spec}(R[X]/(c_{0}X^{p^{n}}-X))
.

Now, moving on to 2, we specialize the above to
\widehat{G}=\widehat{H}_{n}
. Then
c_{0}=1
and
c_{d}=0
for
d>0
, so the above equations just amount to
b_{k}=\varphi^{n}(b_{k})
for all
k\geq 0
. The polynomial
x^{p^{n}}-x
splits completely over
\mathbb{F}_{p^{n}}
, so all the finite etale extensions appearing above are constant over
\mathbb{F}_{p^{n}}
, proving the pro-constancy of
\operatorname{End}(\widehat{H}_{n})
over
\mathbb{F}_{p^{n}}
. To finish the proof of 2, it suffices to identify each ring
\operatorname{End}(\widehat{H}_{n})_{i}(\mathbb{F}_{p^{n}})
with
\mathcal{O}_{n}/\pi^{i}
, compatibly in
i
, and to prove 3 we have to make this identification functorial in the choice of
\mathbb{F}_{p^{n}}
.

By the above analysis, if
M
denotes the Cartier module of
\widehat{H}_{n}
, then the set of endomorphisms of
M/V^{i}M
over
\mathbb{F}_{p^{n}}
is in bijection with the set of sequences

b_{0},b_{1},\ldots,b_{i-1}

of elements of
\mathbb{F}_{p^{n}}
, where to such a sequence we assign the endomorphism
f
characterized uniquely by

f(e)=([b_{0}]+V[b_{1}]+\ldots+V^{i-1}[b_{i-1}])e.

This is also in bijection with elements of the Cartier ring (mod
V^{i}
) of the form

[b_{0}]+V[b_{1}]+\ldots+V^{i-1}[b_{i-1}].

Using the injective homomorphism of
W(\mathbb{F}_{p^{n}})
into the Cartier ring, assigning to a Witt vector with Witt coordinates
(a_{0},a_{1},\ldots)
the element
\sum_{d\geq 0}V^{d}[a_{d}]F^{d}
of the Cartier ring, we easily calculate that the ring structure on endomorphisms, via this parametrization, indeed corresponds to the ring structure on
\mathcal{O}_{n}/\pi^{i}
, with
\pi
corresponding to
V
(which, as an endomorphism of
\widehat{H}_{n}
, corresponds to the relative Frobenius
t\mapsto t^{p}
). The key relation
V^{n}=p
amounts to the definition of
\widehat{H}_{n}
.
∎

The upshot of this is that the structure of the moduli stack of one-dimensional formal groups of exact height
n
can be understood in terms of the following (light) profinite group, which is in fact naturally a compact
p
-adic Lie group.

Definition 12.4
.

Let
n\geq 1
. Define the
(extended) Morava stabilizer group
to be the profinite group given as the semidirect product

\mathbb{G}_{n}:=\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\times}\rtimes\operatorname{Gal}(\mathbb{F}_{p^{n}}/\mathbb{F}_{p})

with respect to the natural action of
\operatorname{Gal}(\mathbb{F}_{p^{n}}/\mathbb{F}_{p})
on
\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\times}
.

We can consider
\mathbb{G}_{n}
as a pro-constant group scheme over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
, and as such it acts on
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{F}_{p^{n}})
via the natural projection
\mathbb{G}_{n}\to\operatorname{Gal}(\mathbb{F}_{p^{n}}/\mathbb{F}_{p})
. Then Theorem
12.3
has the following well-known consequence:

Theorem 12.5
.

The pro-etale quotient
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{F}_{p^{n}})/\mathbb{G}_{n}
is the moduli stack of one-dimensional formal groups of exact height
n
over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
:

\mathcal{M}_{fg,n}=\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{F}_{p^{n}})/\mathbb{G}_{n}.

Proof.

By general formalism of semidirect products, the quotient
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{F}_{p^{n}})/\mathbb{G}_{n}
identifies with the quotient
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{F}_{p})/\mathcal{G}
, where
\mathcal{G}
is the nontrivial form of
\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\times}
gotten by twisting via the action of
\operatorname{Gal}(\mathbb{F}_{p^{n}}/\mathbb{F}_{p})
on
\mathcal{O}_{n}^{\times}
. But
\mathcal{G}=\operatorname{Aut}(\widehat{H}_{n})
by Theorem
12.3
parts 2 and 3, and the quotient stack
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{F}_{p})/\operatorname{Aut}(\widehat{H}_{n})
identifies with
\mathcal{M}_{fg,n}
by Theorem
12.3
part 1.
∎

Now, let
\operatorname{Perf}_{/\mathcal{M}_{fg,n}}
denote the slice category of affine perfect
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-schemes equipped with a map to
\mathcal{M}_{fg,n}
. Equivalently, this is the opposite of the category of pairs
(R,\widehat{G})
consisting of a perfect
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-algebra
R
and a formal group of exact height
n
over
R
, where morphisms
(R,\widehat{G})\to(R^{\prime},\widehat{G}^{\prime})
are pairs
(f,\alpha)
where
f:R\to R^{\prime}
and
\alpha:\widehat{G}\times_{\operatorname{Spec}(R)}\operatorname{Spec}(R^{\prime})\simeq\widehat{G}^{\prime}
is an isomorphism of formal groups over
R^{\prime}
. We will equip this slice category with the pro-etale Grothendieck topology (inherited from
\operatorname{Perf}
).

A theorem of Lurie, refining previous work of Morava, Hopkins-Miller, and Goerss-Hopkins, produces a canonical sheaf of
E_{\infty}
-rings on
\operatorname{Perf}_{/\mathcal{M}_{fg,n}}
, given by the
Morava
E
-theories
.

Definition 12.6
.

A
Morava E-theory of height
n\geq 1
is an
E_{\infty}
-ring spectrum
E
such that:

1.

\pi_{i}E=0
for
i
odd.

2.

\pi_{i}E\otimes_{\pi_{0}E}\pi_{2}E\overset{\sim}{\to}\pi_{i+2}E
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
. Thus
\pi_{2}E
is an invertible
\pi_{0}E
-module, and
\pi_{2i}E=(\pi_{2}E)^{\otimes i}
for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
.

3.

The Quillen formal group

\operatorname{Spf}E^{0}(K(\mathbb{Z};2))=\varinjlim_{n}\operatorname{Spec}(E^{0}(\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{n}))

over
\pi_{0}E
has the following properties:

(a)

The associated Hasse invariants
(p,v_{1},\ldots,v_{n-1})
form a regular sequence. (Recall that
v_{i}
is only defined mod
(p,v_{1},\ldots,v_{i-1})
, and is a section of a line bundle and not an honest element, but still the regular sequence condition makes sense, as do the following conditions.)

(b)

\pi_{0}E
is
(p,v_{1},\ldots,v_{n-1})
-complete.

(c)

v_{n}
is invertible on
\pi_{0}E/(p,v_{1},\ldots,v_{n-1})
.

(d)

\pi_{0}E/(p,v_{1},\ldots,v_{n-1})
is a perfect
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-algebra.

Remark 12.7
.

Recall in the introduction to this section that we discussed a similar sequence of elements
p=v_{0},v_{1},\ldots
which are even defined over the sphere spectrum, unique up to replacing
v_{i}
with some power
v_{i}^{N}
(and up to homotopy). The notation is consistent, in that if we base-change these
v_{0},v_{1},\ldots
from the sphere spectrum to a Morava E-theory (or more generally to any complex orientable ring spectrum), then we get the above
v_{0},v_{1},\ldots
coming from the Quillen formal group, again up to replacing each
v_{i}
by some power and up to homotopy. The degrees are consistent because the cotangent space of the Quillen formal group identifies with
\pi_{2}E
.

In particular, while the operation on
E
-modules (for a Morava
E
-theory of height
n
) of sequentially modding out the Hasse invariants
(-)/(p,v_{1},\ldots,v_{n-1})
is not necessarily defined over
\mathbb{S}
, this problem goes away after raising to powers, and in particular the the operation of
(p,v_{0},\ldots,v_{n-1})
-completion is defined over
\mathbb{S}
.

Given a Morava
E
-theory, we get an object of
\operatorname{Perf}_{/\mathcal{M}_{fg,n}}
, namely we take the
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-algebra
R=\pi_{0}E/p,\ldots,v_{n-1}
equipped with the base-change of the Quillen formal group. The following theorem of Lurie gives the converse, see
[
Lur20
]
Section 5.

Theorem 12.8
.

There is a fully faithful functor

(R,\widehat{G})\mapsto E(R,\widehat{G})

from
(\operatorname{Perf}_{/\mathcal{M}_{fg,n}})^{op}
to
E_{\infty}
-rings, whose essential image consists exactly of the Morava E-theories as defined above. The inverse functor sends a Morava E-theory to
R=\pi_{0}E/p,\ldots,v_{n-1}
equipped with the base-change of the Quillen formal group. Moreover, this functor
E
has the following properties:

1.

It is a hypercomplete sheaf for the pro-etale topology on
\operatorname{Perf}_{/\mathcal{M}_{fg,n}}
.

2.

Suppose given two objects
X,Y\in\operatorname{Perf}_{/\mathcal{M}_{fg,n}}
. Then the natural map

E(X)\otimes E(Y)\to E(X\times_{\mathcal{M}_{fg,n}}Y)

of
E_{\infty}
-rings realizes the target as the
(p,v_{1},\ldots,v_{n-1})
-completion of the source. (Note that
X\times_{\mathcal{M}_{fg,n}}Y
is indeed affine and perfect when
X
and
Y
are, e.g. by Theorem
12.5
.)

Proof.

The main statement is
[
Lur20
]
Thm. 5.1.5 and Claim 2 is
[
Lur20
]
Remark 5.1.6: they both follow from a universal property of Morava E-theory. For Claim 1, since Morava E-theories are
(p,v_{1},\ldots,v_{n-1})
-complete, we can test this after modding out by
(p,v_{1},\ldots,v_{n-1})
. Then since

\pi_{\ast}(E(R,\widehat{G})/(p,v_{1},\ldots,v_{n-1}))=\oplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\omega_{\widehat{G}}^{\otimes_{R}i}[2i],

each homotopy group is a quasicoherent sheaf hence satisfies derived descent, so passing to the limit using the Postnikov tower we deduce the hyperdescent claim.
∎

Now, consider the functor

\operatorname{ProfSet}^{light}\to\operatorname{Perf}

which sends a light profinite set
S=\varprojlim_{n}S_{n}
to the affine perfect
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-scheme

\operatorname{Spec}(C(S,\mathbb{F}_{p}))=\varprojlim_{n}\sqcup_{S_{n}}\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{F}_{p}),

the pro-constant
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-scheme on
S
. This functor preserves finite disjoint unions, sends pullbacks to pullbacks, and sends surjective maps to pro-etale covers. Thus it induces a pullback map of
\infty
-topoi from light condensed anima to pro-etale hypersheaves of anima on
\operatorname{Perf}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}
. Now, recall the Morava stabilizer group
\mathbb{G}_{n}
from Definition
12.4
, a light profinite group. Then we get an induced a pullback functor

\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}_{/(\ast/\mathbb{G}_{n})}\to\operatorname{Sh}^{hyp}_{proet}(\operatorname{Perf})_{/(\ast/\mathbb{G}_{n})}

where on the right we view
\mathbb{G}_{n}
as a pro-constant perfect
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-group scheme.

Next, recall that
\mathcal{M}_{fg,n}
identifies with the quotient
\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{F}_{p^{n}})/\mathbb{G}_{n}
, whence a natural map
\mathcal{M}_{fg,n}\to\ast/\mathbb{G}_{n}
. Pulling back along this, we further deduce a pullback functor

\alpha^{\ast}:\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}_{(\ast/\mathbb{G}_{n})}\to\operatorname{Sh}^{hyp}_{proet}(\operatorname{Perf})_{/\mathcal{M}_{fg,n}}

(meaning, the “left adjoint” part of a geometric morphism of
\infty
-topoi). Now, Lurie’s theorem
12.8
gives a sheaf of
E_{\infty}
-rings on
\operatorname{Sh}^{hyp}_{proet}(\operatorname{Perf})_{/\mathcal{M}_{fg,n}}
. Taking
\alpha_{\ast}
we therefore deduce a sheaf of
E_{\infty}
-rings on
\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}_{(\ast/\mathbb{G}_{n})}
, or in other words a light condensed
E_{\infty}
-ring with an action of the light condensed group
\mathbb{G}_{n}
.

Definition 12.9
.

Define
the Morava E-theory of height
n
to be the light condsensed
E_{\infty}
-ring spectrum with
\mathbb{G}_{n}
-action, denoted
\mathbb{E}_{n}
, gotten by applying
\alpha_{\ast}
to Lurie’s sheaf of
E_{\infty}
-rings on
\operatorname{Perf}_{/\mathcal{M}_{fg,n}}
via the geometric morphism
\alpha
defined above.

By construction, as a hypersheaf of
E_{\infty}
-rings on the site of light profinite sets
S
equipped with a map
S\to\ast/\mathbb{G}_{n}
(or equivalently, with
\mathbb{G}_{n}
-torsor),
\mathbb{E}_{n}
is described as follows:

\mathbb{E}_{n}(S\to\ast/\mathbb{G}_{n})=E(S\times_{\ast/\mathbb{G}_{n}}\mathcal{M}_{fg,n}\to\mathcal{M}_{fg,n}).

In particular, the underlying
E_{\infty}
-ring is given by

E_{n}:=\mathbb{E}_{n}(\ast\to\ast/\mathbb{G}_{n})=E(\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{F}_{p^{n}})\to\mathcal{M}_{fg,n})=E(\mathbb{F}_{p^{n}},(\widehat{H}_{n})_{\mathbb{F}_{p^{n}}}).

In words, it is the Morava E-theory based on the perfect
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-algebra
\mathbb{F}_{p^{n}}
, equipped with the Honda formal group.

Note that
\mathbb{E}_{n}
provides extra structure on
E_{n}
in two directions: first, it promotes it to a light condensed
E_{\infty}
-ring; explicitly, for
S\in\operatorname{ProfSet}^{light}
we have

\mathbb{E}_{n}(S\overset{triv}{\longrightarrow}\ast/\mathbb{G}_{n})=E(C(S,\mathbb{F}_{p^{n}}),(\widehat{H}_{n})_{C(S;\mathbb{F}_{p^{n}})}).

But second, it also provides a
\mathbb{G}_{n}
-action on this light condensed
E_{\infty}
-ring. This uses that we can more generally consider arbitrary
\mathbb{G}_{n}
-torsors over
S
, not just trivial ones.

If we ignore the
\mathbb{G}_{n}
-action, the light condensed structure on
\mathbb{E}_{n}
is in fact determined by the underlying
E_{\infty}
-ring
E_{n}
. This is because the “topology” on
\mathbb{E}_{n}
is somehow algebraically determined as the “
(p,v_{1},\ldots,v_{n-1})
-adic topology”. For the precise statement, recall the unique geometric morphism

\delta^{\ast}:\operatorname{An}\to\operatorname{CondAn}^{light},

with right adjoint
\delta_{\ast}(X)=X(\ast)
. In particular, given an
E_{\infty}
-ring
R
, we get a light condensed
E_{\infty}
-ring
\delta^{\ast}R
, morally by “equipping
R
with the discrete topology”.

Lemma 12.10
.

The natural map
\delta^{\ast}E_{n}\to\mathbb{E}_{n}
of light condensed
E_{\infty}
-rings realizes the target as the
(p,v_{1},\ldots,v_{n-1})
-completion of the source.

Proof.

By construction the target is
(p,v_{1},\ldots,v_{n-1})
-complete. Thus we can check mod
(p,v_{1},\ldots,v_{n-1})
. Because

\pi_{\ast}(E(R,\widehat{G})/(p,v_{1},\ldots,v_{n-1}))=\oplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\omega_{\widehat{G}}^{\otimes_{R}i}[2i]

and
\delta^{\ast}
commutes with homotopy groups, we therefore reduce to the claim that for
S\in\operatorname{ProfSet}^{light}
and
A\in\operatorname{Ab}
, we have
(\delta^{\ast}A)(S)=C(S;A)
, which is a special case of Theorem
4.15
.
∎

Remark 12.11
.

In terms of the formalism of etale sheaves from Section
7
, we can reinterpret this lemma as saying that the object

\mathbb{E}_{n}\in\operatorname{CAlg}(\operatorname{Sh}_{v}(\ast/\mathbb{G}_{n};\operatorname{Sp}^{\wedge}_{(p,v_{1},\ldots,v_{n-1})}))

lies in the full subcategory
\operatorname{CAlg}(\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(\ast/\mathbb{G}_{n};\operatorname{Sp}^{\wedge}_{(p,v_{1},\ldots,v_{n-1}})))
. (Note that
\operatorname{Sp}^{\wedge}_{(p,v_{1},\ldots,v_{n-1})}
is compactly generated, with generator
\mathbb{S}/(p,v_{1},\ldots,v_{n-1})
, so that Remark
7.4
does apply.)

While the light condensed structure on
\mathbb{E}_{n}
is in this manner completely determined by the underlying ordinary Morava E-theory
E_{n}
, the “continuous”
\mathbb{G}_{n}
-action on
\mathbb{E}_{n}
is truly extra structure on
E_{n}
, and is not so simple to describe in classical terms. Only the action of the underlying discrete group
\mathbb{G}_{n}(\ast)
is directly visible without passing to a more refined context.

The first solution (and a completely satisfactory one at that) to this issue was provided by Devinatz-Hopkins (
[
DH04
]
). They produced a hypercomplete sheaf
\mathcal{E}_{n}^{DH}
of
(p,v_{1},\ldots,v_{n-1})
-complete
E_{\infty}
-rings on the site of finite continuous
\mathbb{G}_{n}
-sets such that Morava E-theory identifies with the
(p,v_{1},\ldots,v_{n-1})
-completion of the stalk
e^{\ast}\mathcal{E}_{n}^{DH}
, where
e
is the canonical basepoint of the site of finite
\mathbb{G}_{n}
-sets (corresponding the forgetful functor to sets). By the comparison
4.32
between hypercomplete sheaves on the site of finite continuous
\mathbb{G}_{n}
-sets and etale sheaves on
\ast/\mathbb{G}_{n}
, and Remark
12.11
showing that
\mathbb{E}_{n}
is etale as a
(p,v_{1},\ldots,v_{n-1})
-complete sheaf, it makes sense to ask whether
\mathcal{E}_{n}^{DH}
and
\mathbb{E}_{n}
agree. They do indeed, and we will prove this presently.

Recall that by construction,
\mathbb{E}
, as a
v
-sheaf on
\ast/\mathbb{G}_{n}
, is determined by its values on light profinite sets
S
mapping to
\ast/\mathbb{G}_{n}
, namely in terms of Lurie’s generalized Morava E-theories from
12.8
, we have

\mathbb{E}(S\to\ast/\mathbb{G}_{n})=E(S\times_{\ast/\mathbb{G}_{n}}\mathcal{M}_{fg,n}\to\mathcal{M}_{fg,n}).

To compare with Devinatz-Hopkins’ construction, we should understand more generally its values on objects of the form
T/\mathbb{G}_{n}\to\ast/\mathbb{G}_{n}
, where
T
is a light profinite set with
\mathbb{G}_{n}
-action. The previous case corresponds to the situation where the action is free, with
S=T/\mathbb{G}_{n}
, and the current case of interest is when
T
is finite. The key is the following Kunneth/Galois property.

Proposition 12.12
.

Suppose
T,T^{\prime}
are light profinite sets with
\mathbb{G}_{n}
-action. Then the natural map of
E_{\infty}
-rings

\mathbb{E}(T/\mathbb{G}_{n})\otimes\mathbb{E}(T^{\prime}/\mathbb{G}_{n})\to\mathbb{E}((T\times T^{\prime})/\mathbb{G}_{n})

exhibits the target as the
(p,v_{1},\ldots,v_{n-1})
-completion of the source.

Proof.

In the case where
T/\mathbb{G}_{n}
and
T^{\prime}/\mathbb{G}_{n}
are light profinite sets
S
and
S^{\prime}
respectively, this is a translation of Theorem
12.8
part 2. Now consider a general
T\in\operatorname{ProfSet}^{light}
with
\mathbb{G}_{n}
-action, but keep
T^{\prime}
as before. Varying over
S\to T/\mathbb{G}_{n}
with
S\in\operatorname{ProfSet}^{light}
we deduce that

\mathbb{E}\mid_{T/\mathbb{G}_{n}}\otimes\mathbb{E}(T^{\prime}/\mathbb{G}_{n})\to P_{\ast}P^{\ast}(\mathbb{E}\mid_{T/\mathbb{G}_{n}})

is a
(p,v_{1},\ldots,v_{n-1})
-completion of
v
-sheaves on
T/\mathbb{G}_{n}
, where
P:(T\times T^{\prime})/\mathbb{G}_{n}\to T/\mathbb{G}_{n}
is the map induced by projection. Now we take global sections on both sides. Since
\mathbb{G}_{n}
is of virtual finite (mod
p
) cohomological dimension (being a compact
p
-adic Lie group) and
\mathbb{E}
is
T(n)
-local, the tensor product commutes with global sections mod
(p,v_{1},\ldots,v_{n-1})
by Remark
7.23
. Thus we deduce the claim for general
T
. Then applying a similar argument in the other coordinate we get the claim for general
T^{\prime}
as well, finishing the proof.∎

Remark 12.13
.

One can push this Galois property to different formulations. As we already noted in the above proof, if we vary over
S\to\ast/\mathbb{G}_{n}
but keep
T^{\prime}/\mathbb{G}_{n}
fixed as the basepoint
\ast\to\ast/\mathbb{G}_{n}
, we get

L_{K(n)}(\mathbb{E}_{n}\otimes E_{n})\simeq e_{\ast}e^{\ast}\mathbb{E}_{n}

as
\mathbb{E}_{n}
-algebras in
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(\ast/\mathbb{G}_{n};L_{K(n)}\operatorname{Sp})
. By the projection formula for
e
we can also rewrite this as

L_{K(n)}(\mathbb{E}_{n}\otimes E_{n})\simeq L_{K(n)}(\mathbb{E}_{n}\otimes e_{\ast}\mathbb{S})

Corollary 12.14
.

We have
\mathbb{E}(\ast/\mathbb{G}_{n})=L_{K(n)}\mathbb{S}
, the
K(n)
-local sphere spectrum.

Proof.

As E-theories are
T(n)
-local and complex orientable, they are
K(n)
-local, and thus so is
\mathbb{E}(\ast/\mathbb{G}_{n})
(as a limit of E-theories). Since furthermore
K(n)=E_{n}/(p,v_{1},\ldots,v_{n-1})
, it suffices to see that the unit map

E_{n}\to E_{n}\otimes\mathbb{E}(\ast/\mathbb{G}_{n})

is an isomorphism mod
(p,v_{1},\ldots,v_{n-1})
. But the special case of Proposition
12.12
where
T=\mathbb{G}_{n}
and
T^{\prime}=\ast
shows that a retract of this map is a mod
(p,v_{1},\ldots,v_{n-1})
-isomorphism.
∎

More generally, the same argument gives the following comparison with Devinatz-Hopkins’ construction
\mathcal{E}_{n}^{DH}
.

Theorem 12.15
.

Recall from
4.32
the pullback map
f^{\ast}
from the
\infty
-topos of sheaves of anima on finite sets with continuous
\mathbb{G}_{n}
-action to the
\infty
-topos of v-sheaves on
\ast/\mathbb{G}_{n}
, induced by sending
T
with
\mathbb{G}_{n}
-action to
T/\mathbb{G}_{n}
.

We have
f_{\ast}\mathbb{E}_{n}=\mathcal{E}_{n}^{DH}
.

Proof.

Let
T
be a finite set with continuous
\mathbb{G}_{n}
-action. By Proposition
12.12
, we have that the natural map

\mathbb{E}(T/\mathbb{G}_{n})\otimes\mathbb{E}\to P_{\ast}P^{\ast}(\mathbb{E})

is a
(p,v_{1},\ldots,v_{n-1})
-completion of v-sheaves of
\mathbb{E}
-algebras on
\ast/\mathbb{G}_{n}
, where
P:T/\mathbb{G}_{n}\to\ast/\mathbb{G}_{n}
. Taking homotopy groups, we deduce that the algebra in Morava modules associated to the
K(n)
-local
E_{\infty}
-ring spectrum
\mathbb{E}(T/\mathbb{G}_{n})
identifies with the same one used to construct
\mathcal{E}_{n}^{DH}(T)
in
[
DH04
]
. As by
[
Mat16
]
Thm. 10.9
\mathcal{E}_{n}^{DH}
is uniquely determined by its associated sheaf of algebras in Morava modules, this implies the claim.
∎

Remark 12.16
.

Recall from
12.11
that
\mathbb{E}_{n}
is etale as a
K(n)
-local v-sheaf on
\ast/\mathbb{G}_{n}
. Thus by
4.32
, we can restate this Theorem in the reverse direction: we have

f^{\ast}\mathcal{E}_{n}^{DH}=\mathbb{E}_{n},

where the pullback is in the sense of
K(n)
-local sheaves.

In
[
Mor23
]
, Mor used the left-hand side as the definition of the
\mathbb{G}_{n}
-equivariant condensed
E_{\infty}
-ring structure on
E_{n}
. Thus the approach in
[
Mor23
]
and the approach taken here are equivalent.

Recall the interpretation that
E_{n}
is a (
K(n)
-local)
\mathbb{G}_{n}
-Galois extension of
L_{K(n)}\mathbb{S}
from
[
Rog08
]
. An important and well-known application of this is the resulting “Galois descent”, the idea being that a
K(n)
-local spectrum
X
should be recovered from
X\otimes\mathbb{E}_{n}
with all its structure. One formulation of this is the following (compare
[
Mat16
]
Prop. 10.10,
[
Mor23
]
Thm. A.II).

Theorem 12.17
.

The base-change functor

L_{K(n)}\operatorname{Sp}=\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(\ast;L_{K(n)}\operatorname{Sp})\to\operatorname{Mod}_{\mathbb{E}_{n}}(\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(\ast/\mathbb{G}_{n};L_{K(n)}\operatorname{Sp}))

is an equivalence, with inverse given by taking global sections.

Proof.

Let
f:\ast/\mathbb{G}_{n}\to\ast
. We need to show that if
X\in L_{K(n)}\operatorname{Sp}
, then

X\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}f_{\ast}(f^{\ast}X\otimes\mathbb{E}_{n}),

and that if
M\in\operatorname{Mod}_{\mathbb{E}_{n}}(\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(\ast/\mathbb{G}_{n};L_{K(n)}\operatorname{Sp}))
, then

\mathbb{E}_{n}\otimes f^{\ast}f_{\ast}M\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}M,

where all operations are in the sense of
K(n)
-local etale sheaves.

For the first claim, when
X=L_{K(n)}\mathbb{S}
this is Corollary
12.14
. The general case then follows from the projection formula for
f
, which by Remark
7.23
holds because
\mathbb{G}_{n}
has virtual finite (mod
p
) cohomological dimension.

For the second claim, note again that
f_{\ast}
preserves colimits and tensoring with any
X\in L_{K(n)}\operatorname{Sp}
. Thus it suffices to check
\mathbb{E}_{n}\otimes f^{\ast}f_{\ast}M\overset{\sim}{\to}M
for a collection of
M
which generates
\operatorname{Mod}_{\mathbb{E}_{n}}(\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(\ast/\mathbb{G}_{n};L_{K(n)}Sp))
under colimits and tensoring with
L_{K(n)}\operatorname{Sp}
. By the colimit diagram given by the standard resolution of an
\mathbb{E}_{n}
-module induced by the adjunction forgetting the
\mathbb{E}_{n}
-module structure, it therefore suffices to consider
M
of the form
\mathbb{E}_{n}\otimes N
, where
N\in\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(\ast/\mathbb{G}_{n};L_{K(n)}\operatorname{Sp})
. But by the descendability argument in
7.20
the latter category is generated under colimits by the
g_{\ast}X
for
X\in L_{K(n)}\operatorname{Sp}
, where
g:\ast\to\ast/\mathbb{G}_{n}
. Thus we reduce to checking one single case, namely

M=\mathbb{E}_{n}\otimes g_{\ast}L_{K(n)}\mathbb{S}.

But this
M
identifies with
g_{\ast}E_{n}
by the projection formula for
g
, and for
M=g_{\ast}E_{n}
the desired claim follows from Proposition
12.12
.
∎

Remark 12.18
.

Theorem
12.17
implies the well-known “formula” for the
K(n)
-localization of a spectrum
X
, namely
L_{K(n)}X
identifies with the “continuous homotopy fixed point spectrum” of
\mathbb{G}_{n}
acting on the
(p,v_{1},\ldots,v_{n-1})
-completion of
X\otimes E_{n}
. The continuous homotopy fixed point spectrum is, in the present language, modeled by the
f_{\ast}
functor appearing in the above proof.

One can ask what this means from a calculational perspective. Let’s start with the most basic example
X=\mathbb{S}
for orientation. Taking the Postnikov tower of
\mathbb{E}_{n}\in\operatorname{Sh}_{v}(\ast/\mathbb{G}_{n};\operatorname{Sp})
and then applying
f_{\ast}
, we deduce a conditionally convergent spectral sequence of light condensed abelian groups

H^{p}(\mathbb{G}_{n};\pi_{q}\mathbb{E}_{n})\Rightarrow\pi_{q-p}f_{\ast}(\mathbb{E}_{n})

where by definition
H^{p}(\mathbb{G}_{n};\pi_{q}\mathbb{E}_{n}):=\pi_{0}f_{\ast}((\pi_{q}\mathbb{E}_{n})[p])
. The pushforward
f_{\ast}
on
v
-sheaves restricts to the pushforward on etale sheaves as one sees by resolving
\ast/\mathbb{G}_{n}
by light profinite sets and using
4.15
, so by
12.14
we see that
f_{\ast}(\mathbb{E}_{n})=L_{K(n)}\mathbb{S}
(where you take the
K(n)
-localization in light condensed spectra, but on underlying spectra it’s the usual thing, compare Remark
13.6
). Thus this spectral sequence converges to the homotopy groups of the
K(n)
-local sphere (appropriately “topologized”, i.e. equipped with light condensed structure).

As for the
E_{2}
-page, note (as in the proof of Lemma
12.11
) that the underlying light condensed abelian group
\pi_{q}\mathbb{E}_{n}
is profinite (it is the inverse limit of itself mod powers of
(p,v_{1},\ldots,v_{n-1})
and these are finite). Thus by Proposition
4.35
there is no higher cohomology for its sections over a light profinite set
S
, and the
H^{0}
is just the continuous functions on
S
with values in
\pi_{q}\mathbb{E}_{n}
. Thus when we resolve
\ast/\mathbb{G}_{n}
by the bar resolution to calculate
f_{\ast}
, we just get the usual complex of continuous cochains. We deduce the
E_{2}
-page is the usual continuous group cohomology (with profinite coefficients), and this gives another perspective on the spectral sequence from
[
DH04
]
.

For an arbitrary spectrum
X
we can do the same thing to get a spectral sequence of light condensed abelian groups

H^{p}(\mathbb{G}_{n};\pi_{q}((X\otimes\mathbb{E}_{n})^{\wedge}_{(p,v_{1},\ldots,v_{n-1})}))\Rightarrow\pi_{q-p}L_{K(n)}X,

but now
\pi_{q}((X\otimes\mathbb{E}_{n})^{\wedge}_{(p,v_{1},\ldots,v_{n-1})})
need not be profinite so in principle one has to take care with
\operatorname{lim}^{1}
-terms when calculating the
E_{2}
-page (of course if
X
is a finite spectrum this is not an issue).

Remark 12.19
.

Suppose
\mathcal{C}\in\operatorname{CAlg}(\operatorname{Pr}^{L}_{st})
. For a finite group
G
, Mathew in
[
Mat16
]
, generalizing
[
Rog08
]
, defined the notion of a faithful
G
-Galois algebra
R
in
\mathcal{C}
. Such an
R
is a particular kind of commutative algebra object in
\mathcal{C}
with
G
-action, or in other words a particular kind of sheaf on
\ast/G
with values in
\operatorname{CAlg}(\mathcal{C})
. Galois descent in this setting says that

\mathcal{C}\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\operatorname{Mod}_{R}(\operatorname{Sh}(\ast/G;\mathcal{C})).

For a profinite group
G
, we can define a faithful
G
-Galois algebra
R
to be a compatible collection of faithful
G/N
-Galois algebras
R_{N}
as
N
runs over the open normal subgroups of
G
. As in
[
CM21
]
4.1, we can equivalently view this as a sheaf
R
on the site
B^{fin}(G)
of finite continuous
G
-sets with values in
\operatorname{CAlg}(\mathcal{C})
, and the finite Galois descent above tautologically induces

\mathcal{C}\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\operatorname{Mod}_{R}(\operatorname{Sh}(B^{fin}G;\mathcal{C})).

When
R
is hypercomplete and
G
has finite cohomological dimension, using
4.32
we can translate between sheaves on
B^{fin}G
and etale sheaves on
\ast/G
to arrive at a statement like
12.17
. This gives another perspective.

13
Solid refinements of duality

The duality theory of
10
takes place in the setting of etale coefficients. For the purposes of the next section, it will be convenient to extend this to the more general
solid
coefficients.

We start with a small recap of the solid theory from
[
CSa
]
.

Definition 13.1
.

A light condensed abelian group
M
is called
solid
if for every
S\in\operatorname{ProfSet}^{light}
, every map
\mathbb{Z}[S]\to M
admits a unique extension to a map
\mathbb{Z}[S]^{\square}\to M
.

Here

\mathbb{Z}[S]^{\square}:=\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}(\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}(\mathbb{Z}[S],\mathbb{Z}),\mathbb{Z})=\varprojlim_{n}\mathbb{Z}[S_{n}]

if
S=\varprojlim_{n}S_{n}
is written as a sequential limit of finite sets.

Recall that the full subcategory of solid abelian groups is closed under all limits and higher derived limits, colimits, and extensions, as well as all internal
\underline{\operatorname{Ext}}^{i}(N,-)
for
N
a light condensed abelian group. For
S\in\operatorname{ProfSet}^{light}
, the object
\mathbb{Z}[S]^{\square}
is solid, and is compact and projective as a solid abelian group, even in the strong sense of internal hom:
\underline{\operatorname{Ext}}^{i}(\mathbb{Z}[S]^{\square},M)=0
for all
i>0
and
M
solid and
\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}(\mathbb{Z}[S]^{\square},-):\operatorname{Solid}\to\operatorname{Solid}
preserves all colimits. Here
\underline{\operatorname{Ext}}^{i}
as before is the internal Ext in light condensed abelian groups. If
(e_{i})_{i\in I}
is a
\mathbb{Z}
-basis of the free abelian group
\operatorname{Cont}(S,\mathbb{Z})
, then the corresponding map

\mathbb{Z}[S]^{\square}\to\prod_{I}\mathbb{Z}

is an isomorphism of light condensed (solid) abelian groups. Note that
I
is countable.

The theory can be extended to spectra, as noted in
[
CS
]
. We give a brief exposition here.

Definition 13.2
.

Let
X
be a light condensed spectrum. Say that
X
is
solid
if the homotopy group
\pi_{n}X
is solid as a light condensed abelian group for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Theorem 13.3
.

1.

The full subcategory
\operatorname{Sp}^{\square}\subset\operatorname{CondSp}^{light}
of solid spectra is closed under all limits and colimits, and if
M,N\in\operatorname{CondSp}^{light}
with
N\in\operatorname{Sp}^{\square}
, we have

\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}(M,N)\in\operatorname{Sp}^{\square}

as well.

2.

If
S=\varprojlim_{n}S_{n}
is a light profinite set, then the free solid spectrum on
S
identifies with
\varprojlim\mathbb{S}[S_{n}]
. Given a
\mathbb{Z}
-basis
(e_{i})_{i\in I}
of
\operatorname{Cont}(S,\mathbb{Z})
, we can furthermore identify this with
\prod_{I}\mathbb{S}
via the map
S\to\prod_{I}\mathbb{S}
, unique up to homotopy, which is given by
e_{i}
on
\pi_{0}
of the
i^{th}
component.

Proof.

To show closure under colimits, it suffices to show closure under finite colimits and closure under infinite direct sums. Infinite direct sums are t-exact in light condensed spectra (it’s a general property of toposes), so that case reduces to the analogous fact for solid abelian groups. The case of finite colimits reduces to that of shifts and cones, and those follow from the fact that the collection of solid abelian groups is an abelian subcategory closed under extensions.

To show closure under limits, similarly we need only show closure under products. For this can filter by the Postnikov tower to reduce to the fact that solid abelian groups are closed under higher derived limits. A similar argument shows the closure under internal Hom.

For 2, note that

\varprojlim_{n}\mathbb{S}[S_{n}]=\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}(\varinjlim_{n}\prod_{S_{n}}\mathbb{S},\mathbb{S}).

We have
\pi_{0}\varinjlim\prod_{S_{n}}\mathbb{S}=\varinjlim\prod_{S_{n}}\mathbb{Z}=C(S;\mathbb{Z})
. Given a
\mathbb{Z}
-basis
(e_{i})_{i\in I}
of the latter abelian group, the induced map

\oplus_{i\in I}\mathbb{S}\to\varinjlim_{n}\prod_{S_{n}}\mathbb{S}

is an isomorphism on
\pi_{\ast}
and hence an isomorphism of spectra. Taking
\underline{\operatorname{Hom}}(-,\mathbb{S})
in light condensed spectra, we deduce that

\varprojlim_{n}\mathbb{S}[S_{n}]\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\prod_{i\in I}\mathbb{S}.

In particular
\varprojlim_{n}\mathbb{S}[S_{n}]
is connective. (Recall that countable products are exact by
4.10
.) Using that
\mathbb{Z}
is pseudocoherent as an
\mathbb{S}
-module and that countable products of hypercovers are hypercovers in light condensed anima by repleteness (
4.10
), we further deduce that

\mathbb{Z}\otimes_{\mathbb{S}}\varprojlim_{n}\mathbb{S}[S_{n}]=\varprojlim_{n}\mathbb{Z}[S_{n}].

To prove that
\mathbb{S}[S]^{\square}=\varprojlim\mathbb{S}[S_{n}]
, we need to show that if
M
is a solid spectrum, then

\operatorname{Hom}(\varprojlim_{n}\mathbb{S}[S_{n}],M)\overset{\sim}{\to}\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{S}[S],M).

By connectivity, for this we can assume
M
is connective. Then filtering by the Postnikov tower and shifting, we can assume
M
is concentrated in degree
0
. Then in particular
M
is a
\mathbb{Z}
-module, and by adjunction and the result
\mathbb{Z}\otimes_{\mathbb{S}}\varprojlim_{n}\mathbb{S}[S_{n}]=\varprojlim_{n}\mathbb{Z}[S_{n}]
of the previous paragraph we reduce to the fact that
\mathbb{Z}[S]^{\square}
is the free solid abelian group on
S
also in the derived sense (e.g. by the projectivity discussed above).
∎

Let us now give some examples of solid spectra.

Example 13.4
.

1.

Recall the unique geometric morphism
\delta^{\ast}:\operatorname{An}\to\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}
, which is fully faithful (
4.15
). It induces a fully faithful embedding
\delta^{\ast}:\operatorname{Sp}\to\operatorname{CondSp}^{light}
; the essential image consists of the
discrete
light condensed spectra. Every discrete light condensed spectrum is solid. Indeed, taking homotopy groups, this reduces to the fact that every discrete light condensed abelian group is solid, which is easy to check from the definition.

2.

Given any diagram of spectra, we can use
\delta^{\ast}
to put it inside light condensed spectra, then take the limit there. The result is solid by
13.3
. Note that this is generally very different from taking the limit just in spectra and then using
\delta^{\ast}
on the result; morally we are considering the limit of spectra “equipped with the inverse limit topology”, not with the “discrete topology”.

3.

The light condensed Morava E-theory spectrum
e^{\ast}\mathbb{E}_{n}
from
12.9
is solid. Indeed, by Lemma
12.11
it is
(p,v_{1},\ldots,v_{n-1})
-complete and its mod
(p,v_{1},\ldots,v_{n-1})
-reduction is discrete, so this follows from 2.

The closure of solid spectra under limits and colimits implies a further closure property. Let
L:\operatorname{Sp}\to\operatorname{Sp}
be any accessible localization functor, with associated full
\infty
-subcategory of local objects
L\operatorname{Sp}\subset\operatorname{Sp}
. For any presentable stable
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
, we get an induced accessible localization of
\mathcal{C}
, namely the quotient gotten by killing the cocomplete full subcategory generated by the objects
A\otimes X
where
X\in\mathcal{C}
and
A\in\operatorname{Sp}
satisfies
LA=0
. Explicitly,
L\mathcal{C}\subset\mathcal{C}
identifies with the full subcategory of
Y\in\mathcal{C}
such that the mapping spectrum
\operatorname{map}(X,Y)\in\operatorname{Sp}
lies in
L\operatorname{Sp}
for all
X\in\mathcal{C}
. More abstractly, comparing universal properties we see that

L\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}\otimes_{\operatorname{Sp}}L\operatorname{Sp}.

In particular, we can apply this with
\mathcal{C}=\operatorname{CondSp}^{light}
. Explicitly,
X\in\operatorname{CondSp}^{light}
lies in
L\operatorname{CondSp}^{light}
if and only if
X(S)\in L\operatorname{Sp}
for all
S\in\operatorname{ProfSet}^{light}
, and the localization
L
of
\operatorname{CondSp}^{light}
can be obtained by applying
L
on the level of presheaves and then hypersheafifying the result. Then we have the following result showing that this
L
-localization passes to solid spectra.

Lemma 13.5
.

Let
L
be an accessible localization of spectra, and let
X\in\operatorname{CondSp}^{light}
.

1.

If
LX=0
, then
L(X^{\square})=0
.

2.

If
X
is solid, then
LX
is solid.

Proof.

For 1, recall that
\operatorname{ker}(L)\subset\operatorname{CondSp}^{light}
is generated under colimits by the objects
A\otimes Y
for
Y\in\operatorname{CondSp}^{light}
and
A\in\operatorname{Sp}
with
LA=0
. Since
(-)^{\square}
preserves colimits, it suffices to show the claim for such
X=A\otimes Y
. But again because
(-)^{\square}
commutes with colimits we have

(A\otimes Y)^{\square}=A\otimes(Y^{\square}),

another basic object in
\operatorname{ker}(L)
, as required.

Now for 2, denote by
X^{\prime}
the fiber of the map
X\to LX
. Then
X^{\prime}\in\operatorname{ker}(L)
, and
X^{\prime}\to X
is the terminal map to
X
from an object in
\operatorname{ker}(L)
. Since
X
is solid, we get a factoring
X^{\prime}\to(X^{\prime})^{\square}\to X
. Applying 1, we have that
(X^{\prime})^{\square}\in\operatorname{ker}(L)
. Thus, by the universal property of
X^{\prime}\to X
we deduce that
X^{\prime}
is a retract of
(X^{\prime})^{\square}
. It follows (again by closure under colimits, or limits) that
X^{\prime}
is also solid, hence so is
LX=\operatorname{cofib}(X^{\prime}\to X)
, as desired.
∎

In particular, the
L
-localization of solid spectra
\operatorname{SolidSp}\to L\operatorname{SolidSp}
is calculated by the
L
-localization on the level of condensed spectra.

Remark 13.6
.

Another compatibility property is that for
X\in\operatorname{CondSp}^{light}
, we have
L(X(\ast))=(LX)(\ast)
. In other words the
L
-localization of the underlying spectrum of
X
can be calculated as the underlying spectrum of
LX
. This holds because hypersheafification on the site of light profinite sets does not change the value on
\ast
(every hypercover of
\ast
is split). Strangely, this can be useful, in conjunction with the previous lemma, for calculating
L
-localizations of spectra.

Now we will discuss the solid tensor product, in manner analogous to the discussion of the (derived) solid tensor product of solid abelian groups in
[
CSa
]
. By adjunction, another way of stating the closure under internal Hom in part 1 of this theorem is the following: if
M
is a light condensed spectrum with
M^{\square}=0
, then
(M\otimes_{\mathbb{S}}N)^{\square}=0
for any light condensed spectrum
N
. In other words, the localization of light condensed spectra to solid spectra is gotten by killing a tensor ideal. It follows that there is a unique symmetric monoidal structure on solid spectra making solidification into a symmetric monoidal functor; this is called the
solid tensor product
. Explicitly, it is gotten by solidifying the tensor product of light condensed spectra:

M\otimes^{\square}_{\mathbb{S}}M=(M\otimes_{\mathbb{S}}N)^{\square}.

The most basic calculation, which follows from the definition, is the following: if
S,T\in\operatorname{ProfSet}^{light}
, then

\mathbb{S}[S]^{\square}\otimes^{\square}_{\mathbb{S}}\mathbb{S}[T]^{\square}\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\mathbb{S}[S\times T]^{\square}.

Using this we get the following, which is the true starting point for solid tensor product calculations.

Lemma 13.7
.

Let
I
and
J
be countable sets. Then

\prod_{I}\mathbb{S}\otimes^{\square}_{\mathbb{S}}\prod_{J}\mathbb{S}\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\prod_{I\times J}\mathbb{S}.

Proof.

Choose light profinite sets
S
and
T
with an
I
-indexed basis of
C(S,\mathbb{Z})
and a
J
-indexed basis of
C(T,\mathbb{Z})
. Then we get an
I\times J
-indexed basis of
C(S\times T;\mathbb{Z})=C(S;\mathbb{Z})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}C(T;\mathbb{Z})
. On the other hand by Theorem
13.3
this data induces
\mathbb{S}[S]^{\square}=\prod_{I}\mathbb{S}
and
\mathbb{S}[T]^{\square}=\prod_{J}\mathbb{S}
and
\mathbb{S}[S\times T]^{\square}=\prod_{I\times J}\mathbb{S}
. Comparing we deduce the claim.
∎

More general solid tensor product calculations can in principle be reduced to this one, using the fact that the solid tensor product commutes with colimits in each variable (a fact which is obvious from the definition). A very simple example is the following: on the full subcategory of
\operatorname{CondSp}^{light}
given by the discrete spectra (those in the essential image of the fully faithful functor
\delta^{\ast}:\operatorname{Sp}\to\operatorname{CondSp}^{light}
), which lies inside
\operatorname{SolidSp}
by
13.4
, the solid tensor product, the light condensed tensor product, and the usual tensor product of spectra all agree (via obvious comparison maps). Another example is the following, expressing a situation in which the solid tensor product behaves as a “completed tensor product”.

Lemma 13.8
.

Suppose
(N_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(M_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
are two towers of connective spectra, with the following property: for all
n\in\mathbb{N},m\in\mathbb{N}
, and
d\in\mathbb{N}
, we have that
\pi_{d}N_{n}
and
\pi_{d}M_{m}
are finitely generated as abelian groups. View the inverse limits
N=\varprojlim_{n}N_{n}
and
M=\varprojlim_{m}M_{m}
as solid spectra, as in Example
13.4
. Then

N\otimes^{\square}_{\mathbb{S}}M\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\varprojlim_{m}(N\otimes^{\square}_{\mathbb{S}}M_{m})

(which then also identifies with
\varprojlim_{n,m}N_{n}\otimes^{\square}_{\mathbb{S}}M_{m}
by applying the same result again to the other variable.)

Proof.

Let
N^{\prime}=\prod_{n}N_{n}
and
M^{\prime}=\prod_{m}M_{m}
. Using the “shift minus identity” fiber sequences
N\to N^{\prime}\to N^{\prime}
and
M\to M^{\prime}\to M^{\prime}
we reduce to showing that

N^{\prime}\otimes^{\square}_{\mathbb{S}}M^{\prime}\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\prod_{n,m}N_{n}\otimes_{\mathbb{S}}M_{m}.

Now, using the hypothesis on the homotopy groups, each
N_{n}
and
M_{m}
admits a resolution, in
\operatorname{Sp}_{\geq 0}
, where each term is a finite product of copies of
\mathbb{S}
. By
4.10
a countable product of resolutions is a resolution in
\operatorname{CondSp}^{light}_{\geq 0}
. In this way we reduce to the case where each
N_{n}
and
M_{m}
is a finite product of copies of
\mathbb{S}
. Then by reindexing the claim reduces to Lemma
13.7
.
∎

Example 13.9
.

1.

The
p
-complete sphere
\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}
in
\operatorname{CondSp}^{light}
is idempotent with respect to the solid tensor product:

\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}\otimes^{\square}_{\mathbb{S}}\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}.

Indeed, Lemma
13.8
implies that
\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}\otimes^{\square}_{\mathbb{S}}\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}
is
p
-complete, so we can test mod
p
, when we reduce to
\mathbb{S}/p\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\mathbb{S}/p
.
In particular, we deduce that
\operatorname{Mod}_{\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}}(\operatorname{SolidSp})
is a full subcategory of
\operatorname{SolidSp}
via the forgetful functor. It contains, but is much bigger than, the category of
p
-complete solid spectra: it is closed under arbitrary (not
p
-completed) colimits inside
\operatorname{SolidSp}
.

2.

If
X
is a connective spectrum each of whose homotopy groups is finitely generated, or a countable inverse limit of such spectra in
\operatorname{SolidSp}
, then for any light profinite set
S=\varprojlim_{n}S_{n}
, we have

X\otimes^{\square}_{\mathbb{S}}\mathbb{S}[S]^{\square}\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\varprojlim_{n}X[S_{n}],

and if
I
is a countable set, then

X\otimes^{\square}_{\mathbb{S}}\prod_{I}\mathbb{S}\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\prod_{I}X.

Only slightly more involved is the following example, which will be used later.

Proposition 13.10
.

Recall the light condensed Morava E-theory spectrum
E_{n}:=e^{\ast}\mathbb{E}_{n}
from Deinition
12.9
, which is solid by
13.4
. If
S=\varprojlim_{m}S_{m}
is a light profinite set, we have

E_{n}\otimes^{\square}_{\mathbb{S}}\mathbb{S}[S]^{\square}\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\varprojlim_{m}E_{n}[S_{m}],

and if
I
is a countable set, then

E_{n}\otimes^{\square}_{\mathbb{S}}\prod_{I}\mathbb{S}\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\prod_{I}E_{n}.

Proof.

It suffices to prove the second statement (e.g. by the Milnor sequence). Let
e_{n}=\tau_{\geq 0}E_{n}
. Then
e_{n}
is
p,u_{1},\ldots,u_{n-1}
-complete (where
\pi_{0}E_{n}\simeq W(\mathbb{F}_{p^{n}})[[u_{1},\ldots,u_{n-1}]]
) and
e_{n}/p,u_{1},\ldots,u_{n-1}
is discrete with finite homotopy. Then we can apply
13.7
to deduce
e_{n}\otimes^{\square}_{\mathbb{S}}\prod_{I}\mathbb{S}=\prod_{I}e_{n}
. We have
E_{n}=e_{n}[\sigma^{-1}]
, so
E_{n}\otimes^{\square}(-)=(e_{n}\otimes^{\square}(-))[\sigma^{-1}]
. On the other hand we also have
\prod_{I}E_{n}=(\prod_{I}e_{n})[\sigma^{-1}]
because inverting
\sigma
just periodizes the pre-periodic homotopy of
\prod_{I}e_{n}
. Comparing we conclude.∎

Now, in this paper we have been studying etale sheaves of spectra on
\ast/G
for various light condensed groups
G
. By definition, this
\infty
-category
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(\ast/G;\operatorname{Sp})
is a full subcategory of
\operatorname{Sh}_{v}(\ast/G;\operatorname{Sp})
, or in other words, the
\infty
-category of spectrum objects in the
\infty
-topos
\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}_{/(\ast/G)}
.

Our goal in this section is to study an
\infty
-category which is intermediate between
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(\ast/G;\operatorname{Sp})
and
\operatorname{Sh}_{v}(\ast/G;\operatorname{Sp})
, and to which the duality of Section
10
extends.

Definition 13.11
.

For a light condensed group
G
, define

\operatorname{Sh}_{\square}(\ast/G;\operatorname{Sp})\subset\operatorname{Sh}_{v}(\ast/G;\operatorname{Sp})

to be the full subcategory of those
v
-sheaves of spectra on
\ast/G
such that on pullback along
\ast\to\ast/G
, the resulting light condensed spectrum is solid.

In other words, this is the
\infty
-category of solid spectra equipped with a
G
-action.

Remark 13.12
.

A similar definition works with
\ast/G
replaced by any light condensed anima which admits a surjection from a discrete set. Using the relative solid theory of Fargues-Scholze
[
FS24
]
Ch. VII, one can even extend to arbitrary light condensed anima. We will not pursue this here.

Remark 13.13
.

The inclusion
\operatorname{Sh}_{\square}(\ast/G;\operatorname{Sp})\subset\operatorname{Sh}_{v}(\ast/G;\operatorname{Sp})
has a left adjoint, again called solidification, such that pullback along
\ast\to\ast/G
commutes with solidification. To see this, and for some other purposes, it can be convenient to take a different perspective. Note that
\operatorname{Sh}_{v}(\ast/G;\operatorname{An})
identifies, by descent, with the
\infty
-category
\operatorname{Mod}_{G}(\operatorname{CondAn}^{light})
, viewing
G
as an associative monoid object in
\operatorname{CondAn}^{light}
with cartesian product symmetric monoidal structure. Passing to spectrum objects, we deduce that

\operatorname{Sh}_{v}(\ast/G;\operatorname{Sp})=\operatorname{Mod}_{\mathbb{S}[G]}(\operatorname{CondSp}^{light}).

In these terms, Definition
13.11
translates into saying that an
\mathbb{S}[G]
-module is solid iff its underlying light condensed spectrum is solid, and the fact that solidification of
\mathbb{S}[G]
-modules commutes with passing to the underlying light condensed spectrum follows formally from the fact that solidification of light condensed spectra is a symmetric monoidal localization. In particular,

\operatorname{Sh}_{\square}(\ast/G;\operatorname{Sp})=\operatorname{Mod}_{\mathbb{S}[G]^{\square}}(\operatorname{SolidSp}).

Let us take the convention that we use
left
modules unless otherwise indicated.

Remark 13.14
.

We will more precisely be interested in
p
-adic solid coefficients. We take this in the broadest possible sense: that of modules over the solid ring
\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}
. Recall from Example
13.9
that this is idempotent, so if
\mathcal{C}
is any
\operatorname{SolidSp}
-linear presentable
\infty
-category (such as
\operatorname{Sh}_{\square}(\ast/G;\operatorname{Sp})
) then
\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}
-modules in
\mathcal{C}
forms a full subcategory of
\mathcal{C}
via the forgetful functor.

Before proving that duality extends to the solid category, we need some lemmas. The first concerns some general module theory in the light condensed setting. Suppose
R
is a light condensed ring, or more precisely an object in
\operatorname{Alg}(\operatorname{CondSp}^{light})
. Then
R(\ast)
is a usual ring spectrum (an object in
\operatorname{Alg}(\operatorname{Sp})
), and there is a functor

\operatorname{Mod}_{R(\ast)}(\operatorname{Sp})\to\operatorname{Mod}_{R}(\operatorname{CondSp}^{light})

sending a (left)
R(\ast)
-module
M
to the
R
-module

R\otimes_{R(\ast)}M.

This is the left adjoint to the obvious functor
N\mapsto N(\ast)
in the other direction.

It is a basic fact that this functor
\operatorname{Mod}_{R(\ast)}\to\operatorname{Mod}_{R}(\operatorname{CondSp}^{light})
is fully faithful. Indeed, its right adjoint preserves colimits (as every cover of
\ast
is split); but the unit of the adjunction is an isomorphism on
R(\ast)
by construction, hence is an isomorphism in general. In particular, we can view
\operatorname{Perf}(R(\ast))
as a full subcategory of
\operatorname{Mod}_{R}(\operatorname{CondSp}^{light})
. A translation is the following.

Definition 13.15
.

For a light condensed ring
R
, say that a (left)
R
-module
M\in\operatorname{Mod}_{R}(\operatorname{CondSp}^{light})
is
perfect
if it lies in the thick subcategory generated by the free rank one
R
-module
R
. Denote this full subcategory by

\operatorname{Perf}(R)\subset\operatorname{Mod}_{R}(\operatorname{CondSp}^{light})
.

Thus, by the above remarks,
\operatorname{Perf}(R)
identifies with the usual
\infty
-category of perfect
R(\ast)
-modules via
M\mapsto M(\ast)
.

Now, here is the first lemma.

Lemma 13.16
.

Let
R
be a connective
p
-complete light condensed ring spectrum, and
M
a connective
p
-complete left
R
-module. Suppose
\mathbb{F}_{p}\otimes_{\mathbb{S}}M\in\operatorname{Perf}(\mathbb{F}_{p}\otimes_{\mathbb{S}}R)
. Then
M\in\operatorname{Perf}(R)
.

Proof.

Consider the natural map

M(\ast)\otimes_{R(\ast)}R\rightarrow M.

Applying
\mathbb{F}_{p}\otimes_{\mathbb{S}}-
, we get the analogous map for the
\mathbb{F}_{p}\otimes_{\mathbb{S}}R
-module
\mathbb{F}_{p}\otimes_{\mathbb{S}}M
, an isomorphism by hypothesis. Since both sides are connective, it follows that our map exhibits the target as the
p
-completion of the source.

Thus we reduce to proving the analogous lemma in the setting of usual (not condensed) ring spectra and modules. In fact, using the existence of homomorphism
\mathbb{F}_{p}\otimes_{\mathbb{S}}R\to(\pi_{0}R)/p
, it suffices to show the following: if
R
is a
p
-complete connective ring spectrum and
M
is a
p
-complete connective
R
-module such that
M_{0}=R_{0}\otimes_{R}M
is a perfect
R_{0}=(\pi_{0}R)/p
-module, then
M\in\operatorname{Perf}(R)
. In the case of
E_{\infty}
-rings this is a special case of
[
Lur17
]
Prop. 8.3.5.5; let us now give the analogous argument in the associative ring case.

We proceed by induction on the Tor-amplitude of the perfect
R_{0}
-module
M_{0}
. Recall that this is the smallest value
b-a
such that for all right
R_{0}
-modules
N
in degree
0
, the tensor product
N\otimes_{R_{0}}M_{0}
lives in degrees
[a,b]
. By shifting we can assume the range of degrees is
[0,b]
, and then the Tor-amplitude is
b
.

First assume the Tor-amplitude
b
is
>0
. Taking
N=R_{0}
we deduce that
M_{0}
lives in degrees
[0,b]
. Being a perfect complex, its lowest nonzero homotopy group is finitely presented. In particular we can choose an
R_{0}
-module map
f_{0}:R_{0}^{d}\to M_{0}
which is surjective on
\pi_{0}
. Lift
f_{0}
arbitrarily to an
R
-module map
f:R^{d}\to M
and let
M^{\prime}=\operatorname{Fib}(R^{d}\to M)
. Since
M
is
p
-complete, by derived Nakayama we have that
f
is surjective on
\pi_{0}
, hence
M^{\prime}
is connective. It is also
p
-complete as
R
and
M
are. Moreover
M^{\prime}_{0}=\operatorname{Fib}(R_{0}^{d}\to M_{0})
has Tor-amplitude
b-1
by the long exact sequence in Tor, so we can conclude by induction.

It remains to handle the case where
M_{0}
is of Tor amplitude
0
. Being in particular a perfect complex concentrated in degree
0
, we have that
M_{0}
is a finitely presented
R_{0}
-module. By the Tor amplitude condition, it is also flat. Therefore it is finitely generated projective. Choose
\alpha_{0}:R_{0}^{d}\to M_{0}
and
\beta_{0}:M_{0}\to R_{0}^{d}
such that
\alpha_{0}\circ\beta_{0}=\operatorname{id}_{M_{0}}
, and let
e_{0}=\beta_{0}\circ\alpha_{0}
, so that
e_{0}
is an idempotent
d\times d
matrix over
R_{0}
.

It is elementary to see that idempotent
d\times d
matrices always lift along square-zero extensions (of associative rings). Thus we can lift
e_{0}
to
(\pi_{0}R)/p^{2}
, then to
(\pi_{0}R)/p^{3}
, etc., and then in the limit to an idempotent
d\times d
matrix over
\varprojlim_{n}(\pi_{0}R)/p^{n}
. As
R
is (derived)
p
-complete, the map
\pi_{0}R\to\varprojlim_{n}(\pi_{0}R)/p^{n}
is another example of a square zero extension, and it follows that we can lift
e_{0}
to an idempotent
n\times n
matrix over
\pi_{0}R
. Thus, we can lift
e_{0}
to an
R
-linear map
e:R^{d}\to R^{d}
which is idempotent up to homotopy:
e^{2}\sim e
. This is enough to guarantee that the map

R^{d}\to e^{-1}R^{d}\oplus(1-e)^{-1}R^{d}

is an isomorphism. To finish the proof, it suffices to show that
M
is isomorphic to
e^{-1}R^{d}
.

As in the inductive argument above, we can lift
\alpha_{0}:R_{0}^{d}\to M_{0}
to an
R
-module map
\alpha:R^{d}\to M
. Replacing
\alpha
by
\alpha\circ e
, we can assume that
\alpha\circ e\sim\alpha
. This allows us to make a map
e^{-1}R^{d}\to M
which reduces to the given isomorphism
e_{0}^{-1}R_{0}^{d}\to M_{0}
when we apply
\mathbb{F}_{p}\otimes_{\mathbb{S}}-
. As both
e^{-1}R^{d}
and
M
are connective and
p
-complete, this implies that the map
e^{-1}R^{d}\to M
is an isomorphism, as required.
∎

Example 13.17
.

Suppose
G
is a light profinite group such that the augmentation module
\mathbb{F}_{p}
is a perfect
\mathbb{F}_{p}[[G]]
-module, where
\mathbb{F}_{p}[[G]]=\varprojlim_{N}\mathbb{F}_{p}[G/N]
denotes the usual completed group ring. (Recall from
[
Laz65
]
that every compact
p
-adic Lie group has an open normal subgroup satisfying this condition.)
Then
\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}\in\operatorname{Perf}(\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}[G]^{\square})
. Indeed, by the lemma it suffices to show that
\mathbb{F}_{p}\in\operatorname{Perf}(\mathbb{F}_{p}[G]^{\square})
. By hypothesis
\mathbb{F}_{p}
admits a finite resolution by finitely generated projective
\mathbb{F}_{p}[[G]]
-modules. But by definition
\mathbb{F}_{p}[G]^{\square}
is exactly
\mathbb{F}_{p}[[G]]
equipped with its natural light profinite structure. Every finitely generated projective
\mathbb{F}_{p}[[G]]
-module gets its induced functorial light profinite topology, and a surjection of light profinite sets is surjective in solid modules by definition, so it follows that the same resolution shows
\mathbb{F}_{p}\in\operatorname{Perf}(\mathbb{F}_{p}[G]^{\square})
, as desired.

Duality concerns the study of the functor
\pi_{\ast}:\operatorname{Sh}_{\square}(\ast/G;\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}})\to\operatorname{Sh}_{\square}(\ast;\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}})
, which is by definition right adjoint to the pullback
\pi^{\ast}
. In terms of the identification
\operatorname{Sh}_{\square}(\ast/G;\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}})=\operatorname{Mod}_{\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}[G]^{\square}}(\operatorname{SolidSp})
of Remark
13.13
,
\pi^{\ast}
corresponds to the restriction of scalars functor associated to the augmentation map
\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}[G]^{\square}\to\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}
. It follows that
\pi_{\ast}
corresponds to the solid internal hom out of
\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}
in
\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}[G]^{\square}
-modules:

\pi_{\ast}M=\operatorname{map}_{\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}[G]^{\square}}(\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}},M).

As usual, discussion of duality starts with identifying situations where
\pi_{\ast}
commutes with colimits and satisfies the projection formula.

Proposition 13.18
.

Let
p
be a prime. Suppose
G
is a light profinite group such that
\mathbb{F}_{p}\in\operatorname{Perf}(\mathbb{F}_{p}[[G]])
. Then

\pi_{\ast}:\operatorname{Sh}_{\square}(\ast/G;\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}})\to\operatorname{Sh}_{\square}(\ast;\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}})

preserves colimits and satisfies the projection formula.

Proof.

We use the above formula
\pi_{\ast}M=\operatorname{map}_{\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}[G]^{\square}}(\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}},M)
. Note that the solid internal hom out of
\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}[G]^{\square}
identifies with the forgetful functor, and this clearly commutes with colimits and satisfies the projection formula. Thus it suffices to show that
\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}
lies in the thick subcategory generated by
\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}[G]^{\square}
. But this was proven in Example
13.17
.
∎

As in the discussion in
7
, the basic idea of the six functor formalism is that, in cases where
\pi_{\ast}
fails to preserve colimits and satisfy the projection formula, one wants a replacement
\pi_{!}
. Here we will implement this idea in a somewhat different way, following the idea of
[
Kle01
]
.

Definition 13.19
.

Let
p
be a prime, and suppose
G
is a light profinite group which admits an open subgroup
N
such that
\mathbb{F}_{p}\in\operatorname{Perf}(\mathbb{F}_{p}[[N]])
. Define

\pi_{!}:\operatorname{Sh}_{\square}(\ast/G;\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}})\to\operatorname{Sh}_{\square}(\ast;\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}})

to be the left Kan extension of the restriction of
\pi_{\ast}
to the full subcategory of compact objects in
\operatorname{Sh}_{\square}(\ast/G;\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}})
. Thus
\pi_{!}
comes with a natural transformation
\pi_{!}\to\pi_{\ast}
.

Remark 13.20
.

Note that
\operatorname{SolidSp}
is compactly generated with compact generator
\prod_{I}\mathbb{S}
for countably infinite
I
. Hence
\operatorname{Mod}_{\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}[G]^{\square}}(\operatorname{SolidSp})
is also compactly generated, with compact generator
\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}[G]^{\square}\otimes^{\square}_{\mathbb{S}}(\prod_{I}\mathbb{S})
. Thus this is in principle a sensible definition. Moreover by Lemma
13.7
the full subcategory of compact objects
\operatorname{SolidSp}^{\aleph_{0}}\subset\operatorname{SolidSp}
is closed under solid tensor product, so we also see that the action of
\operatorname{SolidSp}
on
\operatorname{Mod}_{\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}[G]^{\square}}(\operatorname{SolidSp})
restricts to an action on the level of compact objects.

Lemma 13.21
.

Let
p
be a prime. Suppose
G
is a light profinite group with an open subgroup
H
such that
\mathbb{F}_{p}\in\operatorname{Perf}(\mathbb{F}_{p}[[H]])
. Write
f:\ast/H\to\ast/G
for the map induced by the inclusion, and continue to write
\pi:\ast/G\to\ast
for the projection. For any
M\in\operatorname{Sh}_{\square}(\ast/G;\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}})
which is in the essential image of
f_{\ast}
, we have:

1.

for any
X\in\operatorname{Sh}_{\square}(\ast;\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}})
, the natural map
X\otimes\pi_{\ast}M\to\pi_{\ast}(X\otimes M)
is an isomorphism;

2.

the natural map
\pi_{!}M\to\pi_{\ast}M
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

Note that
f
satisfies the projection formula because it’s finite etale. Since
\pi^{\prime}:\ast/H\to\ast
satisfies the projection formula by Proposition
13.18
, we deduce 1. For 2, consider the natural transformation

\pi_{!}\circ f_{\ast}\to\pi_{\ast}\circ f_{\ast}=\pi^{\prime}_{\ast}.

It suffices to show this is an isomorphism. Again because
f
is finite etale,
f_{\ast}
preserves colimits; thus so does
\pi_{!}\circ f_{\ast}
. So does
\pi^{\prime}_{\ast}
by Proposition
13.18
. Thus it suffices to show that this natural transformation is an isomorphism on the compact generator
\prod_{I}\mathbb{S}\otimes\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}[H]^{\square}
of Remark
13.20
. For that, in turn, since
\pi_{!}\to\pi_{\ast}
is an isomorphism on compact objects it suffices to show
f_{\ast}(\prod_{I}\mathbb{S}\otimes^{\square}\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}[H]^{\square})\simeq\prod_{I}\mathbb{S}\otimes^{\square}\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}[G]^{\square}
. By projection formula for
f_{\ast}
, this reduces to
f_{\ast}\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}[H]^{\square}\simeq\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}[G]^{\square}
which follows by ambidexterity of
f
(which holds due to
f
being finite etale).
∎

Proposition 13.22
.

Let
p
be a prime. Suppose
G
is a light profinite group which admits an open subgroup
H
such that
\mathbb{F}_{p}\in\operatorname{Perf}(\mathbb{F}_{p}[[H]])
. The functor
\pi_{!}:\operatorname{Sh}_{\square}(\ast/G;\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}})\to\operatorname{Sh}_{\square}(\ast;\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}})
has the natural structure of a
\operatorname{Sh}_{\square}(\ast;\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}})
-linear functor.

Proof.

As in the proof of the previous lemma, note that the compact generator of
\operatorname{Sh}_{\square}(\ast/G;\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}})
lies in the essential image of
f_{\ast}
. Thus, by the previous lemma we have that
\pi_{\ast}
, when restricted to compact objects, satisfies the projection formula, hence is canonically
\operatorname{Sh}_{\square}(\ast;\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}})^{\aleph_{0}}
-linear on compact objects. Passing to Ind-categories we deduce the claim.
∎

Remark 13.23
.

By the Morita theory of
[
Lur17
]
Prop. 7.1.2.4, it follows that in module theoretic terms, we can describe
\pi_{!}
as follows. Define

\mathbb{D}_{G}=\pi_{\ast}(\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}[G]^{\square})=\operatorname{map}_{\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}[G]^{\square}}(\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}},\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}[G]^{\square}),

where the right hand side denotes the solid mapping spectrum in solid left
\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}[G]^{\square}
-modules. We view
\mathbb{D}_{G}
as a right
\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}[G]^{\square}
-module using the (unit) bimodule structure of
\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}[G]^{\square}
. In other words,
\mathbb{D}_{G}
is the value on
\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}[G]^{\square}
of the right adjoint to the functor
\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}\otimes^{\square}_{\mathbb{S}}-
from right modules to bimodules (using the augmentation left module structure on
\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}
). Then

\pi_{!}M=\mathbb{D}_{G}\otimes^{\square}_{\mathbb{S}[G]^{\square}}M

as a functor from
\operatorname{Mod}_{\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}[G]^{\square}}(\operatorname{SolidSp})\to\operatorname{Mod}_{\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}}(\operatorname{SolidSp})
.

Now we compare this
\pi_{!}
with the etale
\pi_{!}
defined earlier in Section
7
. For this we pass to the
p
-complete setting. Recall that the natural pullback functor

\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(\ast/G;\operatorname{Sp}_{\widehat{p}})\to\operatorname{Sh}_{v}(\ast/G;\operatorname{Sp}_{\widehat{p}})

is fully faithful. The essential image consists of those
p
-complete v-sheaves whose mod
p
reduction is etale as a
v
-sheaf of spectra (
7.4
). By Example
13.4
, any such is solid. Thus we can naturally view

\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(\ast/G;\operatorname{Sp}_{\widehat{p}})\subset\operatorname{Sh}_{\square}(\ast/G;\operatorname{Sp}_{\widehat{p}})

as the full subcategory of those
p
-complete solid sheaves on
\ast/G
whose mod
p
reduction is etale (“discrete”) on pullback to
\ast
. Trivially, pullback functors on both sides are compatible with this inclusion. Now we show compatibility with
\pi_{!}
functors.

Proposition 13.24
.

Let
p
be a prime. Suppose
G
is a light profinite group which admits an open subgroup
H
such that
\mathbb{F}_{p}\in\operatorname{Perf}(\mathbb{F}_{p}[[H]])
. The functor

\pi_{!}:\operatorname{Sh}_{\square}(\ast/G;\operatorname{Sp}_{\widehat{p}})\to\operatorname{Sh}_{\square}(\ast;\operatorname{Sp}_{\widehat{p}})

sends
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(\ast/G;\operatorname{Sp}_{\widehat{p}})
inside
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(\ast;\operatorname{Sp}_{\widehat{p}})=\operatorname{Sp}_{\widehat{p}}
, and on that level canonically identifies with the
\pi_{!}
from the etale theory in a unique manner compatible with the natural transformation to
\pi_{\ast}
(see the proof for more precision).

Proof.

Let us use a superscript “et” to denote the etale variants of the various functors. We have a natural transformations

\pi^{et}_{!}\to\pi^{et}_{\ast}\to\pi_{\ast}\leftarrow\pi_{!}

of functors
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(\ast/G;\operatorname{Sp}_{\widehat{p}})\to\operatorname{Sh}_{\square}(\ast;\operatorname{Sp}_{\widehat{p}})
.
For any
H\subset G
as in the statement, if
f:\ast/H\to\ast/G
, then
f^{et}_{\ast}=f_{\ast}
because
f
is finite etale (on underlying spectra,
f_{\ast}
is just a finite direct sum). We claim the following: if
M\in\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(\ast/G;\operatorname{Sp}_{\widehat{p}})
lies in the essential image of
f_{\ast}
, then:

1.

\pi^{et}_{\ast}(M)\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\pi_{\ast}(M)
;

2.

\pi^{et}_{!}(M)\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\pi^{et}_{\ast}(M)
;

3.

\pi_{!}(M)\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\pi_{\ast}(M)
.

For claim 1, it suffices to show that
\pi^{\prime}_{\ast}
preserves the etale full subcategories, where
\pi^{\prime}:\ast/H\to\ast
. This follows by resolving
\ast/H
by copies of the light profinite sets
H^{n}
and using a Postnikov tower argument. Claim 2 follows from
7.20
. Finally, Claim 3 follows from Proposition
13.21
.
Combining 1 and 3, it follows in particular that
\pi_{!}(M)
is etale for any such
M
. But by Example
6.14
, the etale category is generated under colimits by objects of the form
M=f_{\ast}N
, so this implies that
\pi_{!}(M)
is etale for any etale
M
, as claimed.

For the second claim, note that since both
\pi_{!}
and
\pi_{!}^{et}
preserve colimits, natural transformations out of either of them are uniquely determined by their effect on any full subcategory of compact generators. Again by
6.14
such can be chosen of the form
f_{\ast}(N)
, whence the unique identification
\pi_{!}=\pi_{!}^{et}
compatible with the map to
\pi_{\ast}
, as claimed.
∎

Now we turn to the discussion of the right adjoint
\pi^{!}
to
\pi_{!}
. In terms of the Morita-theoretic perspective from Remark
13.23
, this is described as follows:

\pi^{!}X=\operatorname{map}(\mathbb{D}_{G},X).

In other words, we take the solid mapping spectrum from
\mathbb{D}_{G}
to
X
, equipped with the left
\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}[G]^{\square}
-module structure coming from the right module structure on
\mathbb{D}_{G}
. The following result shows the compatibility of this
\pi^{!}
with the etale one.

Proposition 13.25
.

Let
p
be a prime. Suppose
G
is a light profinite group which admits an open subgroup
H
such that
\mathbb{F}_{p}\in\operatorname{Perf}(\mathbb{F}_{p}[[H]])
.

We have that
\pi^{!}
sends etale sheaves to etale sheaves, and the canonical comparison map
(\pi^{!})^{et}\to\pi^{!}
is an isomorphism on etale sheaves.

Proof.

By the above formula for
\pi^{!}
, for the first claim it suffices to show that the solid mapping spectrum out of
\mathbb{D}_{G}
sends etale sheaves (on
\ast
) to etale sheaves (on
\ast
). Recall that
\mathbb{D}_{G}=\pi_{\ast}\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}[G]^{\square}
. By ambidexterity for
\ast/H\to\ast/G
, it follows that
\mathbb{D}_{G}\simeq\mathbb{D}_{H}
as solid spectra. Thus we reduce to the case where
\mathbb{F}_{p}\in\operatorname{Perf}(\mathbb{F}_{p}[[G]])
, which by Example
13.17
implies that
\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}\in\operatorname{Perf}(\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}[G]^{\square})
. It follows that
\mathbb{D}_{G}
lies in the thick subcategory (of solid spectra) generated by
\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}[G]^{\square}
. Thus it suffices to show that the internal mapping spectrum out of
\mathbb{S}[S]
, for any light profinite set
S
, sends etale spectra to etale spectra. But this follows from
4.15
.

We have just shown that
\pi^{!}
sends etale sheaves to etale sheaves. As the same is true for
\pi_{!}
by the previous result, it follows a fortiori by the defining universal properties that
\pi^{!}=(\pi^{!})^{et}
on etale sheaves, finishing the proof.
∎

Corollary 13.26
.

Let
p
be a prime. Suppose
G
is a light profinite group which admits an open subgroup
H
such that
\mathbb{F}_{p}\in\operatorname{Perf}(\mathbb{F}_{p}[[H]])
. Then:

1.

The etale dualizing sheaf
\pi^{!}(\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}})
for
\ast/G
from
10
identifies with the solid mapping spectrum

\operatorname{map}(\mathbb{D}_{G},\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}})

equipped with
G
-action coming from the right module structure on
\mathbb{D}_{G}
(
13.23
).

2.

Suppose
G
is a virtual (mod
p
) Poincaré duality group. Then
\mathbb{D}_{G}
is itself etale, and identifies with the dual (= inverse) of the etale dualizing sheaf.

Proof.

Part 1 follows by applying the previous proposition to the unit etale sheaf
\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}
. For part 2, note that in the proof of the previous proposition we showed that as a solid spectrum,
\mathbb{D}_{G}
lies in the thick subcategory generated by the
M=\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}[S]^{\square}
for light profinite
S
. But for any such
M
, we have

M\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\operatorname{map}(M,\operatorname{map}(M,\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}))

by definition of solidification (see
13.3
). It follows that we can read 1 in reverse, getting
\mathbb{D}_{G}\simeq\operatorname{map}(e^{\ast}\pi^{!}\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}},\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}})
as underlying
p
-complete spectra. Since
G
is a duality group,
\pi^{!}\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}
is invertible, indeed a shift of the unit. (Indeed, we see as in the proof of
8.12
that
\pi^{!}\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}
is bounded below, so by
8.8
it suffices to see that
\pi^{!}\mathbb{F}_{p}
is one dimensional in a single degree, but this is exactly the mod
p
duality condition.)
∎

We ended the previous section with the theorem expressing that
K(n)
-local spectra are the same thing as
\mathbb{G}_{n}
-semilinear
K(n)
-local
E_{n}
-modules. This result does not wholly extend to the solid context, but at least half of it does:

Proposition 13.27
.

Let
p
be a prime and
n\geq 1
. For
X\in\operatorname{SolidSp}
, we have

L_{K(n)}X=\pi_{\ast}(L_{K(n)}(X\otimes^{\square}\mathbb{E}_{n})),

where
\pi:\ast/\mathbb{G}_{n}\to\ast
and we view
\mathbb{E}_{n}\in\operatorname{Sh}_{\square}(\ast/\mathbb{G}_{n};\operatorname{Sp})
.

Proof.

Recall from
[
Laz65
]
that any compact
p
-adic Lie group
G
admits an open normal subgroup
N
with
\mathbb{F}_{p}\in\operatorname{Perf}(\mathbb{F}_{p}[[N]])
. Then
\ast/G=(\ast/N)_{G/N}
, and it follows from Lemma
13.18
and Tate vanishing in
K(n)
-local homotopy theory (
[
GS96
]
,
[
Kuh04
]
) that
\pi_{!}\to\pi_{\ast}
is an isomorphism in the
K(n)
-local context, and
\pi_{\ast}
satisfies the projection formula. It follows that the claim reduces to the case
X=\mathbb{S}
, which is
12.14
.
∎

Remark 13.28
.

This means that the functor

L_{K(n)}\operatorname{SolidSp}\to\operatorname{Mod}_{\mathbb{E}_{n}}(\operatorname{Sh}_{\square}(\ast/\mathbb{G}_{n};L_{K(n)}\operatorname{Sp}))

defined by
X\mapsto L_{K(n)}(X\otimes^{\square}\mathbb{E}_{n})
is fully faithful. As hinted at above, in contrast to the discrete setting it is not essentially surjective. To show this, let us exhibit two non-isomorphic objects in the target each of which map to
E_{n}
under
\pi_{\ast}
.

The first is the obvious one,
L_{K(n)}(E_{n}\otimes^{\square}\mathbb{E}_{n})
, which maps to
E_{n}
under
\pi_{\ast}
by the above result. To produce the second one, start with
\mathbb{S}[G]^{\square}\otimes^{\square}\mathbb{E}_{n}
, which is
K(n)
-local by Proposition
13.10
. Note that by definition
\mathbb{S}[G]^{\square}
is the solidification of
e_{\natural}\mathbb{S}
, where
e_{\natural}
denotes the left adjoint to
e^{\ast}
on v-sheaves. Thus by the projection formula for
e_{\natural}
(which is formal by slice topos nonsense) we deduce

\mathbb{S}[G]^{\square}\otimes^{\square}\mathbb{E}_{n}\simeq\mathbb{S}[G]^{\square}\otimes^{\square}E_{n}.

(We have left the
\mathbb{E}_{n}
-module category, but that’s okay because we only need to be able to calculate
\pi_{\ast}
.) But now by the projection formula for
\pi_{\ast}
we get that

\pi_{\ast}(\mathbb{S}[G]^{\square}\otimes^{\square}E_{n})\simeq(e^{\ast}\mathbb{D}_{G})\otimes^{\square}E_{n}.

Thus if we take our object
\mathbb{S}[G]^{\square}\otimes^{\square}\mathbb{E}_{n}
and tensor it with
e^{\ast}\pi^{!}\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}
, this cancels the
e^{\ast}\mathbb{D}_{G}
by Corollary
13.26
and so we get another object whose
\pi_{\ast}
gives
E_{n}
. These objects are not isomorphic, even on underlying solid spectra, because the first one is etale in the
K(n)
-local sense while the second one is not.

Here is a corollary in ordinary homotopy theory. Of course, it can also be proved directly, but it’s not completely obvious.

Corollary 13.29
.

Let
p
be a prime,
n\geq 1
, and
I
a countable set. Then for the spectrum
\prod_{I}\mathbb{S}\in\operatorname{Sp}
, we have

L_{K(n)}\prod_{I}\mathbb{S}=\prod_{I}L_{K(n)}\mathbb{S}.

Proof.

By Remark
13.6
, it suffices to show that the
K(n)
-localization of the
solid
spectrum
\prod_{I}\mathbb{S}
identifies with
\prod_{I}L_{K(n)}\mathbb{S}
. But
13.10
shows that

\prod_{I}\mathbb{S}\otimes^{\square}\mathbb{E}_{n}=\prod_{I}\mathbb{E}_{n}.

In particular this is already
K(n)
-local, and as
\pi_{\ast}
commutes with limits we deduce the claim from Proposition
13.27
.
∎

Remark 13.30
.

The result also holds without cardinality restriction on
I
. On the other hand, it seems, based on the precise way the telescope conjecture fails (
[
Bur+23
]
), that for
n\geq 2
the analogous statement is false for
L_{T(n)}
.

14
Application to self-duality of Morava E-theory, following Beaudry-Goerss-Hopkins-Stojanoska

Hopkins proved the intriguing result that the
K(n)
-local spectrum
E_{n}
is
self-dual
up to a shift — not in the strong sense of categorical dualizability (which would be false, essentially because
E_{n}
is an infinite extension of
L_{K(n)}\mathbb{S}
) — but in the weaker sense that the internal Hom in
K(n)
-local spectra from
E_{n}
to
L_{K(n)}\mathbb{S}
identifies with
\Sigma^{-n^{2}}E_{n}
(see
[
Str00
]
for an account). A more structured version of this result, taking into account the action of the Morava stabilizer group
\mathbb{G}_{n}
, was proved in
[
Bea+22
]
. Here we revisit these results from the perspective of this paper.

We start with a formulation of this self-duality in the setting of etale sheaves. This is essential a formal consequence of the Galois structure. Recall the Morava stabilizer group
\mathbb{G}_{n}
and the structured Morava E-theory
\mathbb{E}_{n}
, which is a commutative algebra object in
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(\ast/\mathbb{G}_{n};L_{K(n)}\operatorname{Sp})
(
12.9
). Recall also the dualizing object

L_{K(n)}\pi^{!}(\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}})\in\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(\ast/\mathbb{G}_{n};L_{K(n)}\operatorname{Sp})

for
\pi:\ast/\mathbb{G}_{n}\to\ast
, which is invertible by
8.12
and identifies with
L_{K(n)}\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}^{\operatorname{ad}_{\mathbb{G}_{n}}}
by
10.14
.

Lemma 14.1
.

Let
p
be a prime and
n\geq 1
. Consider the map

c:\mathbb{E}_{n}\to L_{K(n)}\pi^{!}(\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}})

in
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(\ast/\mathbb{G}_{n};L_{K(n)}\operatorname{Sp})
which is adjoint (via
8.12
) to the inverse of the isomorphism

L_{K(n)}\mathbb{S}\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\pi_{\ast}\mathbb{E}_{n}

from
12.14
.

Then the composition with multiplication

\mathbb{E}_{n}\otimes\mathbb{E}_{n}\to\mathbb{E}_{n}\overset{c}{\rightarrow}L_{K(n)}\pi^{!}(\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}})

is a perfect pairing in the weak sense that it induces

\mathbb{E}_{n}\overset{\sim}{\rightarrow}\underline{\operatorname{map}}(\mathbb{E}_{n},L_{K(n)}\pi^{!}(\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}})),

where
\underline{\operatorname{map}}
denotes the internal mapping spectrum in
\operatorname{Sh}_{et}(\ast/\mathbb{G}_{n};L_{K(n)}\operatorname{Sp})
.

Proof.

As
\underline{\operatorname{map}}(\mathbb{E}_{n},-)
gives the right adjoint to the forgetful functor from
\mathbb{E}_{n}
-modules to underlying
K(n)
-local etale sheaves, another interpretation of the map

\mathbb{E}_{n}\rightarrow\underline{\operatorname{map}}(\mathbb{E}_{n},L_{K(n)}\pi^{!}(\mathbb{S}_{\widehat{p}}))

we’re trying to prove is an isomorphism is that it is the unique
\mathbb{E}_{n}
-module map which restricts to
c
on the unit of
\mathbb{E}_{n}
. By Theorem
12.17
, it suffices to show that this map induces an isomorphism of
K(n)
-local spectra on applying
\pi_{\ast}
. But this is exactly how
c
was chosen.
∎

It is important to note that there is no a priori relation between this self-duality of
\mathbb{E}_{n}
in the etale category and the self-duality of
E_{n}
proved by Hopkins. This is because the internal hom in etale sheaves does not generally commute with pullbacks (the relevant pullback here being along
e:\ast\to\ast/\mathbb{G}_{n}
). Indeed, in a purely algebraic Galois setting, say an infinite
G
-Galois extension
K\to L
of characteristic
p
fields where
G
is a mod
p
Poincare duality group, the analog of this proposition still holds (
L
is weakly self-dual as a
K
-module in etale sheaves on
\ast/G
up to a twist) by the same proof (see Remark
12.19
), but it is clearly not the case that
L
is weakly self-dual as a
K
-module forgetting the
G
-action.

To recover Hopkins’ result, we therefore need some ingredient which is special to the situation of
L_{K(n)}\mathbb{S}\to E_{n}
. In our presentation this ingredient will be Proposition
13.10
, which stems from the fact that although
E_{n}
is an infinite extension of
L_{K(n)}\mathbb{S}
, it still has enough finiteness properties for some purposes (it is 2-periodic with profinite homotopy groups).

To give the precise argument addressing this, note that internal hom in v-sheaves does commute with arbitrary pullbacks. This is a tautological feature of slice
\infty
